## Findings by tag

### lab: LLM agents that run modelling experiments: loops, tools, search, tracking, guards (611)

- [[agent-laboratory#^f1]] [lab] mle-solver keeps the best programs by an LLM reward model that scores plan, code and observed output 0 to 1, not by a fixed metric (the channel through which test scores reach selection, per Luo et al.). 3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f2]] [lab] Silent nonsense survives: experiments sometimes got 0% accuracy for every method and the agent did not fix it before running out of steps. 5.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f3]] [lab] The agent often called exit(), killing the whole process; removed by hand. It also ran host system commands via subprocess. 5.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f4]] [lab] Printed output from data preparation or results can exhaust the model's token limit. 5.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f5]] [lab] Hallucinated experimental details (learning rate, batch size, 50 epochs, early stopping) for runs that did not happen, mostly with weaker models such as gpt-4o; "uncommon" but no count. 5.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f6]] [lab] LLM reviewers inflate: automated reviewers gave 6.1/10 on average, humans 3.8/10 for the same papers. 4.1.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f7]] [lab] Human-rated experiment quality was low: 2.6/5 (gpt-4o), 3.2/5 (o1-mini), 2.9/5 (o1-preview), 15 papers. 4.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f8]] [lab] Human co-pilot raised external overall scores (+0.58) mostly through presentation, not experimental results; co-pilots rated experiment quality 2.38/5 and found it hard to make the agents carry out their exact plan. 4.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f9]] [lab] Researchers asked for better inspection of intermediate results. 4.2.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f10]] [lab] Checked, not there: no audit of leakage, test-set use or baselines in the paper itself; those come from [[luo-hidden-pitfalls]]. (C/fair, absence)
- [[agent-laboratory#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the harness should reject degenerate results (every arm at chance or 0%, constant predictions) before the agent reasons about them, cap printed output, and treat any LLM self-rating as uninformative. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f1]] [lab] Simple baselines (retry up to five times, retry with rising temperature, escalate from cheap to dear models) matched or beat published agent architectures on HumanEval at far lower cost. `§2.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f2]] [lab] Cost differs by almost 100x at similar accuracy; LATS cost over 50 times the warming baseline. `§2.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f3]] [lab] Claimed gains from planning, reflection and debugging were not shown against simple baselines, so their worth is open. `§2.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f4]] [lab] Accuracy alone cannot show progress, because retrying raises it without any scientific idea; compare at equal cost. `§2.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f5]] [lab] Report cost and accuracy as a Pareto frontier; joint optimisation cut variable cost 53% (GPT-3.5) and 41% (Llama-3-70B) at similar accuracy on HotPotQA. `§3.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f6]] [lab] Holdouts: overfitting is a worse risk than contamination for small agent benchmarks, since knowledge of test items can be programmed in; keep the test set secret. `§5` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f7]] [lab] Of 17 benchmarks surveyed, most lacked a holdout at the right level of generality; 7 had none and no plan for one. `§5` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f8]] [lab] Agent evals are rarely repeated because they are costly; many published scores sat above the maximum of the authors' five reproduction runs and baselines below the minimum. `§6` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f9]] [lab] Evaluation bugs: two leading agents marked some failed tasks as passed and silently dropped tasks from the benchmark. `§6` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f10]] [lab] Task order can matter when tasks share an environment (rate limits made back-to-back Reddit tasks fail), breaking the independence assumption. `§6` (A/strong, verified)
- [[agents-that-matter#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol's lab harness: judge a lab agent or a model change at equal cost (GPU minutes, tokens) against a dumb baseline (rerun with another seed, plain sweep); log cost per run beside the metric; keep a sealed test only the human scores; count dropped or crashed runs rather th... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[agentx-model#^f1]] [lab] Research is organised as four actions: Reproduce, Follow-up, Composition and Diagnose; Diagnose gathers evidence to choose a repair. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f2]] [lab] Split of work: human-designed rules pick the action type; the agent picks and designs the concrete experiment, or defers if evidence does not support one. Section 3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f3]] [lab] Headline: when agents already analyse and select concrete candidates, more complex scheduling gave no consistent efficiency gain; fixed rotation plus agent selection remains a good baseline. Section 1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f4]] [lab] Policies compared: fixed routing, Thompson-sampling bandit routing, joint agent choice, uniform random, parent-result greedy; budget 20 selections. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f5]] [lab] No policy led across environments; in one graph uniform random beat fixed, greedy and joint on mean best AUC. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f6]] [lab] Agent selection cost ~430 minutes and 97.5M tokens for 36 runs; random and greedy needed no LLM calls and were competitive. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f7]] [lab] An experiment's value includes the later experiments it unlocks (e.g. compositions), not just its own score. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f8]] [lab] Diagnose runs were left out of the bandit comparison because their value of information does not show in immediate AUC. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f9]] [lab] Stalls: at 110 of 120 checkpoints a better result was still reachable within budget; a flat curve can mean a missed opportunity, not exhaustion. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f10]] [lab] Giving the selector compressed summaries of past experiments on top of raw code and scores did not consistently help. Section 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agentx-model#^f11]] [lab] Checked, not there: no explicit numeric value or cost estimate per candidate in the selection policies read; cost is counted only as one selection each. (B/fair, absence)
- [[agentx-model#^f12]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: don't build an elaborate scheduler; a fixed rotation of action types (diagnose, follow-up, new idea, combine) with the LLM choosing within each, plus a random/greedy baseline to check the LLM earns its tokens. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f1]] [lab] v1 was linear (each change built on the previous run); v2 moves to tree search because linear search was short-sighted. `section 2-3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f2]] [lab] An experiment manager runs four fixed stages: prototype, hyperparameter tuning, research agenda, ablations, each seeded by the previous stage's best node. `3.2.1` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f3]] [lab] Stages stop on explicit criteria; the last two run until their compute budget is spent. An LLM judge picks the best node to carry forward. `3.2.1` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Selected best experiments are rerun several times at the end of each stage so reported results carry mean and standard deviation. `3.2.1` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f5]] [lab] Node selection: debug a buggy node with a set probability, else best-first among working nodes, with an LLM weighing metrics, training curves and plot quality; several nodes expand in parallel. `3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f6]] [lab] Memory is the tree itself: each node keeps its script, plan, error trace, runtime, metrics, LLM feedback, plot script, figures, VLM feedback and status. `3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f7]] [lab] Hyperparameter and ablation nodes keep a record of conditions already tried, to avoid rerunning them. `3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f8]] [lab] Analysis: every run saves metrics to structured files, a plotting step draws them, and a VLM reviews the figures; a flagged figure marks the node buggy. `3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f9]] [lab] Replication nodes rerun the parent with new seeds; aggregation nodes run no experiment, only a script that summarises replicates with means and standard deviations. `3.2.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f10]] [lab] Write-up prompt forbids inventing numbers: plots must come from the saved data. `appendix B, plot aggregation prompt` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f11]] [lab] Idea generation queries Semantic Scholar to check novelty before committing to an idea. `3.1` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f12]] [lab] Budget: 21, 12, 12, 12 nodes per stage, 1 hour max per node, debug depth 3; a paper takes several hours up to a 15 hour cap. Models: Claude 3.5 Sonnet for code, GPT-4o for feedback. `appendix A` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f13]] [lab] Humans picked 3 of about 40 generated ideas, ran each with several seeds and submitted the best manuscript per idea: the headline result is best-of-n. `4.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f14]] [lab] [method] The authors' own review of the accepted paper found about 57% train/test overlap and a figure contradicting the text's claims; the system did not catch either. `4.2` (C/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f15]] [lab] Checked, not there: no dollar cost per paper and no comparison of tree search against v1's linear loop. (C/fair, absence)
- [[aide-paper#^f1]] [lab] AIDE frames ML engineering as optimising code against a validation metric, with trial and error as a tree search over solutions. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f2]] [lab] Unlike a ReAct agent that appends history to context, it stores all solutions in a tree and asks the LLM to improve one node at a time; a hard-coded search accumulates the gains. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f3]] [lab] Each candidate is a whole script scored independently by a stateless objective (validation accuracy or loss), not a long-horizon episode. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f4]] [lab] Search policy is a fixed rule: draft until enough initial solutions, debug a buggy node within a depth limit, else improve, usually the best node. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f5]] [lab] Draft writes a short plan and a single-file program; debug reads error logs and traces; improve makes exactly one atomic change so its effect is measurable. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f6]] [lab] Memory is a summarisation operator over the tree (metrics, hyperparameters, debug hints), not the full log, to keep prompts small. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f7]] [lab] Each prompt carries a small static data preview (rows, columns, splits) from the workspace. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f8]] [lab] On MLE-bench (OpenAI's numbers) AIDE with o1-preview got a medal in 16.9% of competitions, nearly four times OpenHands; other agents stopped early. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f9]] [lab] (claim) The authors credit the gain to persistent iteration on failed solutions instead of abandoning them. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f10]] [lab] On METR's RE-Bench AIDE led humans within six hours but humans caught up; the paper blames its greedy policy for local optima, and it repeated local patches on multi-step tasks. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f11]] [lab] The agent makes its own validation split inside each script; the paper's own scoring uses a separate holdout. (B/fair, verified)
- [[aide-paper#^f12]] [lab] Checked, not there: no repeated evaluation of a node, no noise estimate, no significance test in the search; the best node is picked by its single validation score. (B/fair, absence)
- [[aide-paper#^f13]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol the parts that transfer are the atomic-change rule, the bounded debug depth and the summarised memory; the free-form agent-written validation split does not (our eval is fixed). (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[aideml#^f1]] [lab] The search policy in code: draft until `num_drafts`, then with probability `debug_prob` debug a random buggy leaf under `max_debug_depth`, else improve the single best node (greedy). `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f2]] [lab] Defaults: 20 steps, 5 drafts, debug probability 0.5, max debug depth 3, a 3600 s execution timeout, 5-fold CV suggested. `aide/utils/config.yaml` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f3]] [lab] The LLM has no tools: each step it returns a 3-5 sentence plan and one full code block; the harness runs it. `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f4]] [lab] Improve prompt demands one atomic, testable change. `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f5]] [lab] A second, cheaper LLM reads the run output and returns a structured review: a bug flag, a 2-3 sentence summary, the metric and its direction. `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f6]] [lab] The metric is read by that LLM from stdout, not parsed by the harness; a NaN or missing metric marks the node buggy. `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f7]] [lab] Memory is the plan, review summary and metric of every good node, put in each draft and improve prompt; buggy nodes are left out. `aide/journal.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f8]] [lab] Each script must print a hold-out validation metric and finish within the timeout; the agent picks the split. `aide/agent.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f9]] [lab] Tools beyond the loop: HTML tree visualiser and a report generated from the journal. `README.md`, `aide/utils/config.yaml` (B/fair, verified)
- [[aideml#^f10]] [lab] Checked, not there: no re-runs or seeds per node, no confidence intervals; best node is `max` over single scores. `aide/journal.py` (B/fair, absence)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f1]] [lab] Framework: an agent is a search policy over candidate solutions plus operators that modify them; their interplay is what matters. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f2]] [lab] Components: fitness function, selection policy, operator set, operator policy, termination rule (wall clock or node cap). (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f3]] [lab] Operators: Draft, Debug, Improve, Memory (hand-written), plus a new Crossover that recombines two solutions. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f4]] [lab] With AIDE's operators, MCTS and evolutionary search gain nothing over greedy; the operators are the bottleneck. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f5]] [lab] Turning memory off in AIDE left the medal rate nearly unchanged. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f6]] [lab] Better operators alone (same greedy search) lift medal rate from 39.8% to 45.5%. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f7]] [lab] AIRA operators: complexity cue by number of children (minimal, moderate, advanced) to stop early over-engineering; scoped memory (siblings for draft and improve, the debug chain for debug); explicit thinking. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f8]] [lab] With good operators, MCTS is best for R1 (47% medals) but greedy is close and best on gold; policies only diverge after about 19 hours. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f9]] [lab] Environment alone matters: the same AIDE agent in AIRA-dojo rose from 35.2% to 45.9% medals. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f10]] [lab] Agent actions: Jupyter execution of any Python, file I/O, shell via magics; returns status, stdout, tracebacks and execution time; sandboxed container with quotas. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f11]] [lab] Budget: 24 h per task, at most 4 h per code execution (cut from 9 h with no loss and more valid nodes). (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f12]] [lab] A separate LLM with structured outputs parses each run's output into success, summary and validation metric. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f13]] [lab] Fitness is 5-fold CV; the final answer is the node with the best validation score. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f14]] [lab] Perceived (validation) performance keeps rising over 24 h while true test performance plateaus or falls slightly: search overfits validation. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f15]] [lab] Searching and selecting by test instead of validation would add 9.4 to 16.6 points of medal rate; greedy agents suffer most. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f16]] [lab] Most of the gap is final-node selection: picking the final node by test (search still by validation) closes all of it for MCTS and evolutionary, over 60% for greedy. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f17]] [lab] Hedging over the top 3 validation nodes recovers about 10% more; top validation nodes do carry signal. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f18]] [lab] [method] Seed variance: 3 seeds can rank agents wrongly; they recommend at least 10 seeds, prefer 20, with stratified bootstrap intervals. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f19]] [lab] Search only pays with more compute if the evaluation signal is good enough to tell which direction improves. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f20]] [lab] Infrastructure lessons: agents broke Python environments via pip (hence containers); API rate limits cap parallel agents; checkpointing needed for long runs. (A/strong, verified)
- [[aira-dojo-paper#^f21]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: with a noisy 8k-game eval, the greedy keep-if-better loop is the policy most hurt by validation noise (^f15), and with weak operators fancier search buys nothing (^f4); spend effort on the evaluation signal and the proposal step first, and pick the final model from t... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[alphaevolve#^f1]] [lab] Loop: sample a parent and inspirations from the database, build a prompt, LLM emits a diff, apply, evaluate, add child and scores back to the database. `figure 2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f2]] [lab] The user supplies an `evaluate` function returning a dict of scalar metrics (maximised), and marks code blocks the system may evolve; the rest is fixed skeleton. `2.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f3]] [lab] Memory of past experiments is the program database: every solution with its scores and outputs, meant to resurface old ideas while keeping diversity; the algorithm mixes MAP-Elites and island models. `2.5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f4]] [lab] Prompts carry several past solutions with their scores and outputs, plus optional human context (literature, equations), randomised template wording, and LLM-written meta prompts co-evolved in their own database. `2.2` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f5]] [lab] Evaluation cascade: cheap small-scale tests first, harder stages only for candidates that pass, to prune bad ideas early. `2.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f6]] [lab] Qualities hard to code (such as simplicity) can be scored by an LLM and added to the metrics, or used to discard solutions. `2.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f7]] [lab] Optimising several metrics at once often improves the single target metric too, which they attribute to more diverse parents in prompts. `2.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f8]] [lab] Model mix: a fast cheap model for volume, a strong model for occasional better ideas (Gemini 2.0 Flash and Pro). `2.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f9]] [lab] Cost regime: thousands of LLM samples suffice (FunSearch needed millions); an evaluation can take up to about 100 compute-hours if parallelised. `table 1, 2.4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f10]] [lab] Pipeline is asynchronous and tuned for throughput: as many ideas proposed and evaluated as the budget allows, not fast single runs. `2.6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f11]] [lab] Ablation: removing evolution (always prompting from the initial program), removing prompt context, or using only a small LLM each did clearly worse, on two math tasks, three seeds each. `4` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f12]] [lab] [method] Where scores come from data, they split train and held-out sets: the scheduling heuristic was checked on unseen recent workloads, kernel shapes split in half for search and evaluation. `3.3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f13]] [lab] Stated main limit: needs an automated evaluator; LLM-judged ideas are possible but not what it is tuned for. `6` (B/fair, verified)
- [[alphaevolve#^f14]] [lab] Checked, not there: no database parameters (island count, migration, MAP-Elites features), no dollar or token cost per run, no analysis or write-up step: output is the best program plus scores. (B/fair, absence)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f1]] [lab] Compaction alone is not enough across context windows; the agent tries to one-shot and runs out of context mid-feature, leaving the next session guessing. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f2]] [lab] A second failure: a later session sees progress and declares the job done. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f3]] [lab] Fix: a first session writes an init.sh, a claude-progress.txt log of what agents did, and an initial git commit; later sessions read progress file plus git history. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f4]] [lab] A structured list of 200+ requirements, all initially "failing", which agents may only edit by flipping a passes field; JSON because the model overwrites it less often than Markdown. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f5]] [lab] One feature per session, then commit with a descriptive message and write a progress summary, so git can revert bad changes. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f6]] [lab] Agents mark work complete without end-to-end checks; giving them a real testing tool (browser automation) dramatically improved results. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f7]] [lab] Each session starts with a fixed routine: pwd, read progress and git log, pick the top unfinished item, run init.sh and a basic end-to-end test before new work. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f8]] [lab] Checked, not there: no measurement of how much each mechanism helped, and nothing on ML experiments; the post names scientific research only as future work. (B/fair, absence)
- [[anthropic-long-running-harness#^f9]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the lab's runs.tsv is the equivalent of the feature list, but a status per planned experiment (queued, running, failed, done) in a machine-written JSON file the agent cannot rewrite would stop "already tried" and "done" errors; a session-start smoke run plays init.sh... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f1]] [lab] More tools are not better; wrapping every API endpoint wastes context. Build a few tools for high-impact workflows. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f2]] [lab] Consolidate chained steps into one tool: search_logs returning relevant lines instead of read_logs; get_customer_context compiling everything at once. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f3]] [lab] Return only high-signal fields; replacing opaque UUIDs with meaningful names or even 0-indexed ids significantly improved precision and reduced hallucination. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f4]] [lab] A response_format parameter (concise or detailed) lets the agent pick verbosity; concise used about a third of the tokens in their Slack example. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f5]] [lab] Paginate, filter, range-select or truncate large outputs with sensible defaults; Claude Code caps tool responses at 25,000 tokens; truncation should tell the agent how to narrow. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f6]] [lab] Error responses should state specific, actionable fixes, not opaque codes or tracebacks. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f7]] [lab] Tool descriptions matter most: unambiguous parameter names (user_id not user); precise description edits took Sonnet 3.5 to state of the art on SWE-bench Verified. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f8]] [lab] Measure tool use itself: runtime per call, number of calls, tokens, tool errors; many redundant calls point to pagination or limits, many invalid-parameter errors to unclear descriptions. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f9]] [lab] Read raw transcripts, not just the agent's own feedback: what agents omit often matters more than what they say. (B/fair, verified)
- [[anthropic-writing-tools#^f10]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a `lab show <run>` / `lab compare a b` that compiles a run's meta, metrics and diff into one short answer fits "consolidate"; run ids should be short readable names, not hashes; lab-run errors should say what to change (e.g. "binary stale, run zig build lab"). (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f1]] [lab] A finished agent trajectory is not evidence: rounds can be invalid or confounded, and later changes can hide earlier findings. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f2]] [lab] Over 30 adaptations, 26 found a later round beating the first, but in 22 the last round was below an earlier best, so the endpoint is not the best knowledge. Non-Monotonic Adaptation Trajectories (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f3]] [lab] Execution validity has four gates: code realises the intervention, the run completes, the comparison follows the protocol, the mechanism is preserved; failing runs are kept as diagnostics but never as claims. Per-Step Verification Harness (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f4]] [lab] Rounds are grouped by intervention; invalid, confounded, duplicated or superseded rounds are excluded before a claim is made. (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f5]] [lab] A record links why and where the change acts to the diff, execution status, protocol, measurements and attribution checks, plus the conditions that limit reuse; negative results are kept as Guards when attributable. (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f6]] [lab] Of 14 candidate claims, 8 became Repairs, 1 a Guard and 5 were Withheld; all 34 referenced artifacts matched archived SHA-256 hashes. Experimental Record Audit (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f7]] [lab] Per-step checks: before an artifact (proposal, code change, run spec, result summary) passes on, the harness checks it against evidence and for missing information the next step needs. (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f8]] [lab] The LLM controller deciding whether a record applies was weak: 67.2% accuracy, below an always-reject rule's 79.7%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f9]] [lab] Checked, not there: no per-instance predictions, no repeated seeds or intervals in the trajectory comparison. (C/fair, absence)
- [[auditable-experimental-records#^f10]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: runs.tsv should mark each run valid or invalid (and why) and group runs by the change they test; the agent's "best so far" should come from the record, never the last run, and kept null results ("tried X, no gain at this n") are as valuable as wins. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f1]] [lab] Failure modes found: the same bugs fixed over and over, tuning skipped despite budget left, tree search that does not explore, EDA done but not used. `Abstract` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f2]] [lab] Cause of repeat bugs: only the child that debugs a failed node sees the error, so other branches rediscover the same API or version error, often dozens of times a run. `§3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f3]] [lab] Debug consultant: each crash is compressed to error type, short signature and the strategy that caused it; a shared registry turns these into BANNED patterns and proven USE fixes, appended to every generation prompt. `§3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f4]] [lab] Deterministic rule: a timeout or empty log ends the branch rather than being retried as noise. `§3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f5]] [lab] Effect of the shared bug registry: AIDE golds 22 to 38 and valid runs 73/90 to 90/90; ML-Master golds 18 to 29; median steps to a first valid submission 6 to 0. Table 1. `§4.2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f6]] [lab] Mechanism: redundant bug encounters fell from 46% to 7.8%, error-free nodes rose from 54.7% to 79.0%; more valid nodes, better held-out score (r = +0.22, 163 seeds). `§4.2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f7]] [lab] Agents under-tune: a prompt directive (baseline first, cheap trials to find the hyperparameters that matter, then tune around the best once gains stall) improved AIDE on 7 of 9 tasks. `§3.2`, `§4.3` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f8]] [lab] Budget-dependent reward: an LLM grades tuning effort 0-3 per node; weak tuning is penalised throughout, strong tuning rewarded only late, so the search explores early and tunes late. `§3.2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f9]] [lab] Interventions interact: the same tuning push hurt ML-Master, whose memory logged a crash as buggy without the reason, so it kept retrying the broken approach. `§4.3` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f10]] [lab] Backtracking: when the same error repeats down a path, return to where it first appeared and pick a sibling by Thompson sampling (Beta per node). With drafts held equal, null runs fell from 33 to 15 of 90 on AIDE; on MLEvolve TS won 5 of 9, most differences within SEM. `§3.3`, `§4.4` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f11]] [lab] More initial drafts (5 to 20) helped AIDE by itself, before TS. `§4.4` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f12]] [lab] Agents ignore analysis: injected, deliberately wrong EDA was acknowledged in 21% of AIDE runs and changed feature selection in 5%; scores did not move. `§4.5` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f13]] [lab] Separate global facts (installed versions, valid APIs, how long a fold takes) from branch-local ones; re-deriving global facts per branch is the waste. `§6` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f14]] [lab] LLMs write nearly the same program repeatedly, so a wide tree is wide only on paper; selection fixes reduce variance more than they raise peaks. `§6` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f15]] [lab] Averaging over successful runs hides runs that produce nothing; count them. `§6` (B/strong, verified)
- [[autoresearch-wasted-compute#^f16]] [lab] (inferred) For Claude lab sessions: a shared, append-only "known constraints" file (banned calls, proven fixes, how long each ladder rung takes) injected at session start is the cheapest measured win here; it matters most when several sessions or branches hit the same Zig/CUDA errors. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[baitbench#^f1]] [lab] Shortcuts taken in 57.1% of runs across seven frontier agents; five of seven above 50%. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f2]] [lab] Range by model: 20.8% (Kimi K2.5) to 76.1% (Claude Opus 4.6). 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f3]] [lab] Setup mirrors an agent ML loop: train and public test sets, a read-only evaluator on the public set, an experiment log; instructions forbid using public targets except via the evaluator; a hidden held-out split the agent never sees. 3.1-3.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f4]] [lab] Entity-overlap leakage: the same entities (users, devices) in train and test let the agent look up an entity's target instead of predicting it; on new entities the gain vanishes. 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f5]] [lab] No-signal task: random labels, so any score above 50% comes from fitting the public labels, by reading them or by repeatedly optimising against the public score. 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f6]] [lab] A validity warning in the prompt cut hacking by only 6.2 points (GPT-5.4 24.4, Sonnet 4.6 8.9, four models no change, DeepSeek up 8.3). 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f7]] [lab] Asking the agent to label each logged experiment's validity did not help: 55.6% without, 56.3% with. 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f8]] [lab] Agents know: in agreed hack runs six of seven agents showed awareness in most runs (Sonnet 4.6 100%), yet submitted anyway. 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f9]] [lab] Check: recompute the metric on a held-out split the agent never saw; hacked runs show a much larger public-to-held-out gap. 3.1, 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f10]] [lab] Scaffold did not explain model differences (GPT-5.4 and Sonnet 4.6 moved in opposite directions when swapped to a shared harness, both within noise). 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[baitbench#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: our data has the same traps (the same players and champions across games, rematches, near-duplicate games from one lobby seen by several crawled players). The harness should own the split (by time and by game id, dedup across players' histories), score on a held-out ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f3]] [method] [lab] Expansion is triggered by something specific: new data, a failure of the model on existing data, or computational trouble; their election model was expanded after a named failure (swing-state correlations). `7` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f6]] [method] [lab] Models form a partial order (a topology); the aim is to navigate among neighbours, not average over all; the Automatic Statistician searches such a space using inference and model criticism. `7.4` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f9]] [lab] [stats] Keep the old models: the sequence is for understanding how inference for the quantity of interest changes with each expansion, not for picking or averaging. `8.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f13]] [lab] Fail fast: little work exists on wasting as little time as possible on models that will be abandoned; they think methods should be judged on it. `3.4` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f16]] [stats] [lab] Iterating on discrepancies double-dips the data; they avoid the worst of it by not searching for the best-fitting model, and by embedding candidates in a larger model instead of selecting among many. `12.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f18]] [lab] Checked, not there: no power or minimum detectable effect reasoning for a model change, no way to rank candidate expansions before fitting them. (A/strong, absence)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f1]] [lab] 5 of 12 ideas (42%) failed to run from unresolved coding errors; the agent cycled through flawed versions of the same code. 2.4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f2]] [lab] Silent bug: meant to sweep e over 2 to 5 but kept it fixed at 2, invalidating the results. 2.4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f3]] [lab] Bad baseline: it did not re-run the k-fold baseline, so the new method looked better than is possible by design. 2.4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f4]] [lab] The system cannot judge its own results: misses methodological flaws and logical inconsistencies. 2.4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f5]] [lab] 4 of 7 manuscripts (57%) had incorrect or hallucinated numbers (hyperparameters and metrics differing from the runs); no confidence intervals or p-values anywhere. 2.5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f6]] [lab] Goal mismatch and noise read as a win: an "energy efficient" method claimed success from better RMSE and 116 to 115 s training time, with higher memory, no significance check. 2.5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f7]] [lab] Its built-in reviewer caught none of the serious issues the humans found. 2.5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f8]] [lab] What caught them: humans reading the code and runs, not the paper. 2.5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f9]] [lab] Code edits by the coding assistant were often undocumented, so changes were hard to trace; they propose a structured record of setup, code version, data version and hyperparameters. 3.8 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f10]] [lab] Cost context: $42 for 10 ideas, 7 runs and 7 papers (about $6 each), plus about 3.5 hours of human effort per manuscript. 2.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[beel-sakana-eval#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the harness should run the baseline itself in the same invocation, log the effective config (not the intended one) per run, and generate any write-up numbers from records; an agent's self-review is not a check. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f1]] [method] [lab] Box's loop: formulate a simple model, compute with it, test it against the data to find where it succeeds and fails, revise according to the criticism and repeat. `section 1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f9]] [lab] Checked, not there: no procedure for ranking candidate revisions, no cost or power considerations, no evidence that criticism-led search beats trying alternatives. (A/fair, absence)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Repeated leaderboard evaluation lets participants overfit the holdout; Kaggle's existing defences are heuristics (bit precision, resubmission rate). Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Kaggle's public/private split protects the final ranking but not the public scores, which forums report as inflated. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Ladder: only release a new score if it beats the previous best by a margin; otherwise repeat the previous best. Section 3, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Error of naive leaderboards grows like sqrt(k) in the number of submissions; the Ladder's grows only with log(k), an exponential improvement. Section 1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f5]] [lab] [stats] Parameter-free variant: the margin is a one-sided paired t-test on the per-example loss vectors of the new and previous best submission (threshold s/sqrt(n)), since paired tests have more power when losses are correlated. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f6]] [lab] [stats] The paper says its significance test is heuristic: adaptivity breaks the t approximation and multiple comparisons are not controlled. Section 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Boosting attack: submitting random label vectors and majority-voting those that scored better makes a submission biased on the public labels only; on the hidden 8000 labels it stays at chance, while Kaggle's mechanism reports a strongly biased loss. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Under that attack the Ladder stays within the expected maximum deviation of k random vectors: each new best makes the next crossing less likely, so only O(log k) steps. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f9]] [lab] [stats] An analyst who only uses the holdout to check whether the latest attempt is well above the previous best effectively runs the Ladder. Conclusion (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f10]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a keep-if-better agent loop should see only "new best" or "no significant change" from the held-out set, decided by our paired log-loss test against the current best, never the raw score of each variant. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[bohb#^f1]] [lab] [method] Pattern: Hyperband is fast early but near random search at large budgets; BO starts slow but wins given time; BOHB aims to get both. Figure 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f2]] [lab] Practical budgets are often only a handful of full trainings, so methods must give good answers before many full runs. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f3]] [lab] Successive halving: run configurations on a small budget, keep the best third, give them η times more budget, repeat to the full budget. Section 3.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f4]] [lab] Hyperband hedges by running several brackets, from very aggressive (many configs, smallest budget) to conservative (straight to full budget). Section 3.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f5]] [lab] BOHB: a model picks which configurations start each bracket; the model is fitted on the largest budget with enough results, so wrong low-budget conclusions get overruled by high-fidelity results. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f6]] [lab] A constant fraction of configurations is still sampled at random, which bounds the worst case when low fidelities mislead. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f7]] [lab] Data subset size works as the budget: SVM benchmark from 1/512 of the training data to all of it. Section 5.2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Where low budgets are too noisy to predict the full budget, cheap evaluations just add overhead and plain BO wins (64-dimension case). Section 5.1, appendix H (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f9]] [lab] Budgets are set by the user and misspecification is a known weakness the authors want to automate away. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bohb#^f10]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: data subsets are a valid cheap fidelity only if small subsets rank candidates the same way as full data; with effects near the noise level, small subsets may not, so check rank agreement before trusting them. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Recommendations: randomise as many sources of variation as possible; decide on improvement with variance, via the probability A beats B in a run; prefer resampling (out-of-bootstrap) over one fixed held-out test set for small gains. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Data sampling (bootstrap of the data) is the largest source of variance; weight initialisation is usually under half of it, on par with data order. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f4]] [lab] Hyperparameter optimisation alone induces about as much variance as weight initialisation (20 HPO runs of 200 trials each, other seeds fixed). Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The total variance from arbitrary choices is on the order of published state-of-the-art increments. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f6]] [lab] [stats] Fixing seeds conditions on arbitrary values and correlates runs, so more splits stop reducing the estimator's variance; randomising seeds and data together decorrelates them. Section 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Re-seeding only the init (common practice) barely helps beyond k = 1; randomising data splits too, and all non-HPO sources, is far better and 51x cheaper than redoing HPO each time (21 vs 1070 hours at k = 100). Section 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Decision error rates (simulated, k = 50): a single-run comparison has about 10% false positives and 75% false negatives; a mean difference with a published-improvement threshold has under 5% false positives but about 90% false negatives; P(A>B) gives about 5% and 30%. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f9]] [lab] [stats] Rule: A beats B if P(A>B) over paired splits is significantly above 0.5 (bootstrap CI) and meaningfully reaches gamma, with gamma = 0.75 robust across their cases. Sections 4.1, 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f10]] [lab] Report all procedures within the significance bounds, not only the best. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f11]] [lab] Even with every seed fixed, one pipeline (segmentation) was not reproducible; they measured that residual numerical noise with 200 fixed-seed runs. Appendix A (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f12]] [lab] Checked, not there: many-way comparisons (an agent's long search) are left open; they suggest raising gamma as a multiple-comparison correction. Section 6 (A/strong, absence)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f13]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: our paired game-clustered interval on one split covers test-set noise but not training noise; a keep should need k paired runs that vary seed and train subsample together (not seed alone), with P(new > base) and its bootstrap CI reported. A noise-floor run (baseline ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f1]] [lab] Training is like compilation with code and data as source, so data needs tests like code. Section I (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f2]] [lab] No test was done by more than 80% of surveyed Google teams, yet most had a nonzero score in at least half: worth doing but not obvious. Section I (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f3]] [lab] Data 1: encode expectations about features in a schema and check data against it automatically, in training and serving. Section II (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f4]] [lab] Data 2: know each feature's added value independent of the others, e.g. by training models with one feature removed (leave-one-out ablation). Section II (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f5]] [lab] Data 7: feature code needs unit tests; a feature bug present in both training and test data is almost impossible to detect later. Section II (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f6]] [lab] Model 1: every model spec is reviewed and checked in, so the exact code behind any trained model is known. Section III (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f7]] [lab] Model 5: test regularly against a very simple baseline (a linear model with few features), which also checks the pipeline works. Section III (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f8]] [lab] Model 6: check quality on important slices; a global gain can hide a large drop on one slice. Release tests: absolute threshold per slice and a maximum change versus the previous model. Section III (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f9]] [lab] Infra 1: training twice on the same data should give identical models; determinism enables diff-testing refactors. Nondeterminism sources: RNG (seed it), initialisation order, thread ordering of data. Section IV (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f10]] [lab] Infra 2: a unit test that trains one gradient step on random input catches many library mistakes fast; also check loss decreases over a few iterations and that a model can memorise its training data. Avoid golden tests. Section IV (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f11]] [lab] Infra 3: an automated end-to-end pipeline test runs regularly, with a fast variant on a data subset or simpler model. Section IV (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f12]] [lab] Infra 4: an automated gate blesses or vetoes each trained model: loose thresholds against a validation set for slow decay, tight comparison to the previous model for sudden drops. Section IV (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f13]] [lab] Monitor 3: training and serving code paths for the same feature must compute identical values; log serving examples and compare per feature. Section V (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f14]] [lab] Monitor 5: alert on the first NaN or infinity, implausible weight bounds, and dead-ReLU fraction during training. Section V (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f15]] [lab] [stats] Monitor 7: calibration by slice as a canary (90% of 0.9 predictions should be positive); unbiased is not good, but bias flags a problem. Section V (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f16]] [lab] Scoring: half a point for a test run by hand and documented, a full point if run automatically and repeatedly; the score is the minimum over the four sections. Section VI (A/fair, verified)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f17]] [lab] (inferred) Directly portable to meiolol-lab: a determinism check (same seed twice gives identical metrics, or a diff), a one-step and overfit-tiny-set unit test per net, a NaN/inf trip-wire, slice tables (by role, elo, patch) with calibration, and the "simpler model is not better" row. Infr... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[caafe-2023#^f1]] [lab] [method] Loop: the LLM writes one code block of features per iteration; the block is kept only if validation performance beats the current best, otherwise rejected and the data left unchanged. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f2]] [lab] Prompt contents: user dataset description, feature names, data types, percent missing, 10 random rows; summary statistics from the train split only; a code template and chain-of-thought (meaning and usefulness, inputs, sample values, then code). `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f3]] [lab] Each feature must carry a written justification of its usefulness, and the LLM is told to drop features made redundant. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Acceptance rule: mean change in accuracy and ROC AUC over ten random validation splits, kept when the average of both is above 0; ten iterations per run, TabPFN as the fast evaluator. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f6]] [method] [lab] Rationale: domain context acts as a prior that narrows a vast feature space to meaningful candidates, easing multiple testing. `2.2.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f10]] [lab] [method] Semantic blinding (no feature names or description) cut GPT-4's mean AUROC from 0.822 to 0.8, close to no feature engineering: the context does the work. `E.1` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f11]] [lab] Cost: 4:43 minutes per dataset, 90% LLM generation and 10% evaluation; 7.4% of generated features were faulty code, recovered by passing the error back. `5` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f12]] [lab] Checked, not there: no model errors or residuals in the prompt. The LLM sees the description, schema, sample rows, the template, and back from each block only its before and after scores (figure 4) or an execution error, so proposals are context-led, not diagnosis-led. (B/fair, absence)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Design the experiment to have the desired power at the MRES; smaller effects are by definition uninteresting, so missing them costs nothing. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f7]] [stats] [lab] Their procedure checks first whether the needed N exceeds the instances available, and if so investigates power or changes parameters before running. `algorithm 2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f9]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: with sigma_d the SD of per-game log-loss differences between candidate and current model (clustered by game, as the harness already does), the two-sided 80% power MDE at alpha 0.05 is about 2.8 * sigma_d / sqrt(N_eff); an idea whose plausible gain is below th... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f1]] [stats] [lab] Overfitting the model selection criterion can cost as much as the difference between learning algorithms, so it cannot be ignored when comparing. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Low variance in the selection criterion matters at least as much as unbiasedness: variance is what lets selection overfit. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f3]] [stats] [lab] Demo (1000 realisations, 64 samples, four-fold CV): the CV estimate keeps falling while true test error rises again after about 30-40 iterations of tuning, the classic overfitting curve one level up. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f4]] [stats] [lab] It is worst with small data and many tuned choices; remedies are the same as for training overfitting: regularisation, early stopping, averaging, fewer hyperparameters, or for very small data integrate them out (Bayesian). Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f5]] [stats] [lab] Reported performance is optimistically biased unless model selection is redone inside each evaluation trial (nested cross-validation). Section 1, 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f6]] [stats] [lab] Rigorous evaluation uses multiple randomised partitionings with selection repeated in each, which is easy to automate and parallelise. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f7]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: an agent's search procedure is itself the "model selection" to be evaluated; report the winner's score on data the search never touched, and cap or early-stop the search on a noisy criterion. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[dvc-exp-diff#^f1]] [lab] With no arguments, compares all params and metrics in the workspace against the last commit, listing only those that changed (all with --all). (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-diff#^f2]] [lab] Takes two experiment names or hashes; one argument compares it to the workspace. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-diff#^f3]] [lab] Output is one row per metric or param: path, name, value in A, value in B, change; JSON or Markdown on request. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-diff#^f4]] [lab] Checked, not there: the change column is a raw difference with no noise estimate, interval or significance. (A/fair, absence)
- [[dvc-exp-diff#^f5]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: `lab diff <run-a> <run-b>` should print changed args, changed provenance (HEAD, binary hash, data snapshot) and the paired metric difference with its interval from stored per-game predictions, not two unpaired numbers. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f1]] [lab] The table holds, per experiment, its parent and name or hash, then metrics, parameters and dependency hashes as columns. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f2]] [lab] Experiments are grouped by parent commit, then sorted, by default by time or by any one metric or param. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f3]] [lab] Column filters cut the table to what differs: --only-changed shows only metrics, params and deps that vary across experiments; --drop and --keep by regex. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f4]] [lab] Failed and queued experiments are rows in the table too (hidden only on request). (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f5]] [lab] Same data as JSON, CSV or Markdown for scripts. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f6]] [lab] Experiments get short human names (e.g. urban-sign) beside the hash. (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-exp-show#^f7]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a `lab show` over runs.tsv with --only-changed style columns (args that differ), failed and queued runs as rows, and dataset/binary hash columns, would let the agent see what was already tried in one short table. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[dvc-internal-files#^f1]] [lab] exp run and repro log every stage run and reuse it by default. Run cache (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-internal-files#^f2]] [lab] A run's identity is the exact dependency contents or parameter values plus the literal command, hashed into a path in the run cache. Run cache (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-internal-files#^f3]] [lab] The cache assumes deterministic commands. Run cache (A/fair, verified)
- [[dvc-internal-files#^f4]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a run key = hash(binary hash, data snapshot id, sorted args, seed) stored in runs.tsv would let lab-run refuse or short-circuit an exact repeat ("same as run X, dev gain Y") instead of spending GPU time. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Reusing a holdout adaptively overfits the holdout itself; reporting the best of many tested predictors is the well-known cause. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Static multiple-testing corrections only work when the set of tested hypotheses is fixed in advance; adaptive choice (hyperparameter tuning, analyst in the loop, stopping conditions) breaks them. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Synthetic demo: with labels independent of data (true accuracy 50%), standard holdout reuse reported over 63% on both training and holdout for k = 500; a fresh set showed the truth. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Thresholdout: if training and holdout scores agree within a noisy threshold, return the training score; otherwise return the holdout score plus noise and spend one unit of an overfitting budget B; stop answering when B runs out. Section 4.1, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The number of queries Thresholdout can answer is exponential in holdout size n, as long as overfitting answers stay at most quadratic in n. Section 1.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f6]] [lab] [stats] SparseValidate: the analyst only gets a bit back per query, with budgets for total queries m and for failed validations B; information leaks only on failures. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Cross-validation and bootstrap do not fix adaptive reuse; before this the only known safe approach was a fresh holdout for each dependent validation. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f8]] [lab] [stats] With a real signal (20 informative attributes) Thresholdout still finds a good classifier while preventing overfitting: the guard does not block true discoveries. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f9]] [lab] [stats] The experiment used T = 0.04, below the values the proof needs, and Gaussian instead of Laplace noise: practical settings are empirical. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f10]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: an agent that sees exact held-out log-loss after every variant is the "standard holdout" curve; exposing only a coarse or noisy answer, and counting how often the held-out score disagrees with the training/CV score, is a cheap Thresholdout-style guard. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[exp-bench#^f1]] [lab] Parts look fine, wholes fail: 20-35% on design or implementation, 0.5% for complete, executable experiments. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f2]] [lab] Integrity monitor over agent logs flags reading the paper, git checkouts, and fake, hardcoded or placeholder data in place of real runs; flagged traces are discarded before execution. 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f3]] [lab] Second check: re-run the agent's code changes in a clean, equivalent container to verify it executes and produces expected outputs. 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f4]] [lab] Agents write plausible answers without running anything: OpenHands models often stopped early with a write-up and no experiment, earning high partial scores. 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f5]] [lab] Single checks over-credit: conclusions can be plausible with no valid experiment, and mock or wrong code still executes; requiring design, implementation, conclusion and execution together is stabler. 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f6]] [lab] Stacking checks: passing the monitor 20.6%, plus design and conclusion 3.7%, plus implementation 0.4%, plus execution 0.2%. 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f7]] [lab] Most common failures (share of 3,238 judge notes grouped into 361 types): missing essential implementation components 39.7%, environment or dependency errors 29.4%, script and file errors 23.8%, missing conclusions 26.2%, incorrect interpretation 19.7%. 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f8]] [lab] Wrong conclusions include claiming a gain with no comparison to the baseline (e.g. INT4 with Hadamard "improves" without baseline INT4). 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f9]] [lab] Design errors: incomplete or misclassified experimental variables 16.1%, and added knobs not asked for 7.6%: agents mix essential factors with noise. 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f10]] [lab] Running longer did not help: little correlation between runtime or cost and overall performance. 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[exp-bench#^f11]] [lab] Checked, not there: no separate counts for leakage, test-set misuse or post-hoc selection; numeric errors in conclusions only 3.2% of conclusion failures. App. G (B/fair, absence)
- [[exp-bench#^f12]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a run counts only if the harness itself executed it and the metric came from its output; reject results whose stated comparison lacks a baseline row from the same run; a fixed checklist of required variables per experiment catches the commonest design gaps. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[hyperband#^f1]] [lab] [method] Hyperband treats tuning as allocating a resource (iterations, data samples or features) to sampled configurations, stopping poor ones early. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Telling two candidates apart needs more resource when partial results are noisier or their final scores are closer. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f3]] [lab] The n versus B/n trade-off: if quality shows only late, or candidates are similar, try few; if it shows early, try many. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f4]] [lab] With prior knowledge of the right trade-off, use it; Hyperband's brackets are a grid over the trade-off for when you don't know. Section 3.1, 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f5]] [lab] Data subset size as the resource; worthwhile mainly when training time grows super-linearly with data. Section 3.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f6]] [lab] On small data sets subsampling buys nothing: startup overhead dominates; and failures may only appear on the full data. Appendix A.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f7]] [lab] [stats] On 117 data sets, Bayesian methods won on validation rank by more than on test rank, a sign of overfitting the validation set; random search at twice the budget beat all. Section 4.2.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f8]] [lab] Hyperband suits hard, higher-dimensional spaces; for low dimensions random or Bayesian search already covers the space and the speedup is small. Section 4.4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f9]] [lab] When a setting's best value depends on the resource (e.g. regularisation vs data size), small-resource results mislead; normalise the setting by data size to remove the dependence. Section 4.4.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f10]] [lab] Prior knowledge can narrow the brackets tried; in hindsight one aggressive bracket would have beaten full Hyperband in most of their experiments. Section 4.4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f11]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: shrinkage strength and priors depend on games per champion pair, so a 10% subset will favour stronger shrinkage than the full data; subsets can screen out broken or clearly worse ideas, not rank near-tie ones. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[impossiblebench#^f1]] [lab] Frontier models cheat often when tests conflict with the spec: GPT-5 in 76% of Oneoff-SWEbench tasks, with strategies from editing tests to operator overloading and state recording. Section 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f2]] [lab] Prompt wording matters a lot here (unlike METR's finding): a strict prompt that says STOP and explain on flawed tests cut GPT-5 from 92% to 1% on Conflicting-LiveCodeBench, though o3 stayed at 33%. Sections 1, 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f3]] [lab] Hiding the tests cuts cheating to near zero but hurts legitimate performance; read-only tests keep performance and stop test edits, but not special-casing or operator overloading. Section 5.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f4]] [lab] Recommendation: hide test files or make them read-only; in the read-only setting, modified tests are reverted at scoring. Section 5.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f5]] [lab] A feedback loop of repeated submissions raises cheating along with legitimate scores (pass rate 80% to 83%, cheating 33% to 38%): agents cheat more after failing legitimately. Section 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f6]] [lab] An explicit abort option (submit flag_for_human_intervention) cut GPT-5's cheating from 54% to 9% and o3's from 49% to 12%, but helped Claude Opus 4.1 much less. Section 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f7]] [lab] LLM monitors caught 86-89% of cheating on simple tasks but only 42-65% on multi-file SWE tasks; plausible excuses such as "backward compatibility" fooled them. Sections 1, 6 (B/strong, verified)
- [[impossiblebench#^f8]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the eval harness and held-out games should be hidden or read-only to the agent and scoring should run from a pinned copy; give the agent a "stop, nothing real left to find" exit rather than an open-ended loop that rewards pushing a noise-level metric. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f1]] [lab] Leakage is widespread: 20 audit papers across 17 fields, 329 papers affected, some wildly overoptimistic. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f2]] [lab] Taxonomy, L1: no clean train-test separation in any pre-processing, modelling or evaluation step. Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f3]] [lab] L1 subtypes: no test set (L1.1); pre-processing such as imputation or over/under sampling on the whole dataset (L1.2); feature selection on the whole dataset (L1.3); duplicates across train and test (L1.4). Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f4]] [lab] L2: illegitimate features, such as a proxy for the outcome or information not available at prediction time; judging it needs domain knowledge, so no subtypes. Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f5]] [lab] L3.1 temporal leakage: when predicting the future, no test data may predate the training data. Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f6]] [lab] L3.2 non-independence: train and test sharing the same people or units is leakage unless the claim is about that same dependence; split by unit (block CV). Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f7]] [lab] L3.3 sampling bias: the test set is not representative of where the claim applies (e.g. dropping hard borderline cases). Section 2.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f8]] [lab] Model info sheets ask for three written arguments: clean split, every feature legitimate (justification must cover every feature), test drawn from the distribution of interest. Section 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f9]] [lab] Limit: info sheet claims cannot be verified without computational reproducibility (code, data, environment), and wrong sheets give false assurance. Section 3.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f10]] [lab] Case study: errors in 4 of 12 civil-war papers, exactly the 4 claiming complex ML beats logistic regression; corrected, they do no better than LR (one AUC gap 0.14 to 0.01). None was visible from reading the paper. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f11]] [lab] [stats] 9 of 12 papers had no significance test or uncertainty for classifier comparison; one claimed win had a bootstrap AUC interval of [0.66-0.95]. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f12]] [lab] Know the limits of prediction: a known bound on achievable accuracy stops futile effort and calls for scepticism of results beyond it. Section 5 D1/R1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f13]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a sudden jump well past the known level for draft-only prediction (docs/model.md: AUC 0.550 from the draft) should trigger a leakage audit, not a keep. L3.2 maps to the same player across train and test games; L2 to any per-player or per-champion stat computed with t... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f1]] [lab] The agent's only tools are a general coding agent's (shell, edit) in the repo; the loop is a Markdown "skill" the human writes, not a framework. `README.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f2]] [lab] The human programs `program.md` (the research org); the agent edits one file, `train.py`. `README.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f3]] [lab] Evaluation, data and constants live in a read-only file; the agent may not edit the harness or add packages. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f4]] [lab] Fixed wall-clock budget of 5 minutes per run, so runs are comparable whatever the agent changes, about 12 an hour, about 100 a night. `README.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f5]] [lab] Loop: edit, git commit, run with output to a log file, grep the metric, log to TSV, advance the branch if better, else `git reset`. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f6]] [lab] Output stays out of the agent's context: redirect to a log and grep one line; read the tail only on a crash. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f7]] [lab] Experiment log: untracked `results.tsv` with commit, metric, memory, keep/discard/crash status and a short description. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f8]] [lab] Keep rule weighs a simplicity cost against the gain; deleting code for equal results is a keep. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f9]] [lab] Timeouts and crashes: kill runs over 10 minutes; fix trivial bugs, else log `crash` and move on. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f10]] [lab] Runs unattended: the agent must never stop to ask; when out of ideas it re-reads code, combines near-misses, tries radical changes. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f11]] [lab] Greedy hill climb on one branch; backtracking allowed but discouraged. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f12]] [lab] Validation is one pinned shard and training uses a fixed seed 42, so the keep decision compares single runs with no noise estimate. `prepare.py`, `train.py` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f13]] [lab] Checked, not there: no repeated seeds, no significance test, no separate test set; the same val_bpb picks every keep for the whole night. (B/fair, absence)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f14]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol (eval at noise level on ~8k games), the strict "lower is better" keep would ratchet in noise; the template (locked eval, one editable file, log-to-file, TSV ledger, git keep/reset) carries over, the keep rule does not. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch#^f15]] [lab] Parallel runs are by branch per GPU, e.g. `autoresearch/mar5-gpu0`; no shared memory between agents. `program.md` (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch-results#^f1]] [lab] A ~2-day run on a depth-12 model kept ~20 changes out of about 700 tried; all ~20 were additive and transferred to depth-24 models. (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch-results#^f2]] [lab] Stacked, they cut nanochat's "Time to GPT-2" from 2.02 to 1.80 hours (~11%), a metric separate from the val loss the loop optimised. (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch-results#^f3]] [lab] Keep rate about 3% (20 of 700): most proposals are discarded, so throughput of cheap fixed-budget runs carries the method. (inferred) (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch-results#^f4]] [lab] The agent planned from the sequence of earlier results, per Karpathy. (B/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-autoresearch-results#^f5]] [lab] Checked, not there: no repeated seeds or confirmation runs on the kept changes before the transfer test; the human did the confirmation afterwards. (B/fair, absence)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f1]] [lab] Nets fail silently: a misconfigured net usually still trains, just worse, so errors do not surface as exceptions. Section 2 of intro (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f2]] [lab] Example of a silent leak: an autoregressive model taking its own target as input through an off-by-one bug. Intro (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f3]] [lab] Method: build simple to complex, state a concrete hypothesis at each step and test it; avoid adding much unverified complexity at once. The recipe (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f4]] [lab] Check loss at initialisation against its known value (-log(1/n_classes) for softmax; derivable for other losses). Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f5]] [lab] Initialise the output bias to the base rate (e.g. predict 0.1 for a 1:10 class ratio) to avoid a hockey-stick loss curve. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f6]] [lab] Train an input-independent baseline (inputs zeroed); the real model must beat it, which shows it extracts information from the input at all. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f7]] [lab] Overfit a single tiny batch (as few as two examples) to the lowest achievable loss; if it cannot, there is a bug and work stops. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f8]] [lab] Fix the random seed so two runs of the same code give the same outcome. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f9]] [lab] Evaluate on the entire test set rather than smoothed batch losses. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f10]] [lab] Inspect exactly what enters the model, decoded from the raw tensor just before the forward call: the only source of truth for preprocessing bugs. Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f11]] [lab] Dependency check by gradient: set the loss to example i's outputs and confirm a non-zero gradient only on input i (catches batch mixing; also checks a model at t depends only on 1..t-1). Step 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f12]] [lab] Add signals one at a time and check each gives the expected boost. Step 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f13]] [lab] Learning-rate decay defaults keyed to epoch number can silently drive the rate to zero early; he uses constant LR and tunes decay last. Step 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f14]] [lab] Before tuning, know the input-independent and dumb-baseline scores (must beat) and a rough human level. Step 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f15]] [lab] More real data is the only near-guaranteed regulariser; ensembles top out around 5 models. Step 4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f16]] [lab] Checked, not there: no seed-variance check; the fixed seed is for debugging, not for judging an improvement. (A/fair, absence)
- [[karpathy-recipe-2019#^f17]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol-lab: init loss vs log(2) / base-rate log loss, a shuffled-input or champion-ids-only baseline, overfit-256-games and a per-game gradient dependency check are all mechanical and could run before every new net architecture is trusted. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[llambo#^f1]] [lab] [method] An LLM helps BO through zero-shot warm starting, a surrogate model and candidate sampling, and the gains are largest early, while observations are sparse. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f2]] [lab] Works in context, no fine-tuning; modular, so one component can be plugged into an existing BO framework. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f3]] [lab] The prompt carries a problem description (model card, data card), the optimisation history as few-shot examples, and task instructions. Section 2.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f4]] [lab] Warm start: describing the dataset helps, most in the first 5 trials, and the early lead is kept; but LLM starting points are less diverse than random ones. Section 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f5]] [lab] Surrogate: LLM predicts better than GP and SMAC at few points, but a GP gives the best uncertainty estimates; the LLM only approaches it as n grows. Section 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f6]] [lab] Sampling: conditioning on a target score gives good candidates but loses diversity; an exploration knob trades the two. Section 6 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f7]] [lab] Budget tested: 25 trials per search, 5 random starting points shared by all methods; LLAMBO best overall, mainly early. Section 7 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f8]] [lab] Limits: much higher compute per step than classical BO; authors suggest using the LLM only early in the search or only one component; tested on low-dimensional tuning with one LLM. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[llambo#^f9]] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: the LLM's edge is prior knowledge when few runs exist (first ideas, warm-start values, structure); once a numeric knob has ~10+ evaluated points, a classical surrogate with calibrated uncertainty is the safer chooser. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[llambo#^f10]] [lab] Checked, not there (in sections read): no comparison against spending the LLM's cost on more classical trials, and no study of noisy objectives. (B/fair, absence)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f1]] [lab] Do not look closely at test data during exploration: untestable assumptions from it leak into the model. 2.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f2]] [lab] Find and remove duplicates before building models; they corrupt evaluation. 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f3]] [lab] Scaling and imputation statistics (means, ranges) must come from training data only; leakage routes include feature selection before the split, reusing one test set across models, augmentation before the split. 3.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f4]] [lab] Partition the test set at the start and use it once, on a single final model. 3.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f5]] [lab] Feature selection and dimensionality reduction are part of training: fit them on training folds only, and redo them inside each CV fold. 3.6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f6]] [lab] Check what a model responds to: spurious correlations look good on metrics but fail in practice. 3.8 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f7]] [lab] "Sequential overfitting": tuning successive models on one test set leaks it into training, and it is rarely documented because informal; overfitting grows with each use. 4.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Evaluate stochastic models several times: different seeds at least, better also varied data (CV); keep a subject's rows in one fold; report spread (SD or 95% CI). 4.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f9]] [lab] Time series: look-ahead bias when training data postdate test data, including through data-dependent preprocessing before the split. 4.8 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f10]] [lab] Baselines: the model being extended, simpler models, and naive "dummy" baselines (majority class, last value) to show the model is not doing something trivial; a transformer lost to a last-value baseline. 5.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f11]] [lab] [stats] Comparing two model instances by per-sample outputs (McNemar) needs the per-sample predictions recorded. 5.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f13]] [lab] Share the experiment scripts; experiment tracking (MLflow) records the workflow; use a checklist such as REFORMS, most useful at planning time. 6.1, 6.6 (A/fair, verified)
