- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f5]] [method] [stats] Observational values split a coefficient's credit between correlated features, and higher correlation slows their convergence. Section 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f6]] [method] Observational values give importance to a feature left out of the model (BMI) through its correlation with included ones, and shrink the others. Section 3.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f7]] [method] True to the data: on simulated drug response from real gene expression (40 causal of 1000), observational Shapley on a Lasso found more true features than interventional; interventional on an Elastic Net (which spreads credit itself) did as well and was far cheaper (seconds vs 6 hours). ... (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f8]] [method] Authors' default: true to the model for most explainable AI uses. Section 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us the question "how much does each thing matter to winning at minute t" is about the game, not about one GBDT, so it is a true-to-the-data question: interventional TreeSHAP on a GBDT will hand gold lead's credit to whichever of gold, XP or kills the trees happened to spli... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f1]] [method] [stats] Augmentation is averaging over the orbits of a symmetry group that keeps the data distribution (approximately) invariant, and it reduces variance. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f2]] [method] Gains are largest with limited data. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Under only approximate invariance, augmentation adds bias as it cuts variance, so it may not help; the trade-off depends on the distance between the data and its transformed copy. Section 1, item 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Augmented maximum likelihood cannot always match a model where the invariance is built in by constraint. Section 1, item 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f5]] [method] (inferred) Team swap with the side flag flipped is an exact symmetry; a model of the form f(blue) - f(red) + side term already has it built in, so augmentation adds nothing there. It helps for models that don't (a raw MLP over 10 slots). Swapping without flipping side is approximate (sid... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f1]] [method] Model: logit = sum of hero biases difference + within-team synergy pairs (bilinear a_i P a_j) + cross-team opposition pairs (a_i Q a_j); trained by AdaGrad on log loss. Eqn. 3, 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f2]] [method] [data] Data: HotS 1,814,066 matches (Dec 2016, hotslogs), HoN 1,101,046 (Dec 2014 to Apr 2015), Dota 2 3,056,596 (11 Feb to 2 Mar 2016, Semenov et al.'s data, normal-skill subset); all ranked or matchmade 5v5 with no major game update in the window. Table 1, section Performance Evaluatio... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f3]] [method] Split: 10-fold cross-validation, 8:1:1 train, validation, test; random folds, not time. AUC compared by paired t-test. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f4]] [method] Test AUC (Table 2). Dota 2: LR 0.6875, GBDT 0.7014, FM 0.7143, GAE 0.7143. HotS: LR 0.6095, GBDT 0.6375, FM 0.6440, GAE 0.6437. HoN: LR 0.6115, GBDT 0.6144, FM 0.6154, GAE 0.6220. No accuracy or log loss reported. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f5]] [method] Additive-only LR is worst in every game; pairwise interactions add 0.027 AUC (Dota), 0.035 (HotS), 0.011 (HoN) at 1-3M games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f6]] [method] GBDT, which can capture higher-order interactions, lands between LR and the pairwise models; higher-order structure did not help. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f7]] [method] FM and the embedding model tie on Dota and HotS: both factorise pairwise interactions, so the parameterisation matters less than having low-rank pair terms. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f8]] [method] No player skill terms: they argue ranked matchmaking equalises skill, so draft-only is reasonable for their data. (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Draft-only AUC differs a lot by game at similar data size: 0.71 Dota, 0.64 HotS, 0.62 HoN. The ceiling is set by the game's balance, not the model; LoL ranked at 0.55 (ours) sits below all three. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f10]] [method] Optimiser: AdaGrad on mini-batches of matches; hyperparameters (regularisation, latent dimension K, trees) chosen per fold on the validation split; K = 75 in the Dota case study. Checked, not there: the chosen K and penalty per game in the AUC table, epochs. Eqn. 5, Performance Evaluatio... (B/fair, absence)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f2]] [method] Gold diff and XP diff dominate random-forest importance at minute 10; the per-team totals add nothing once diffs are in. Pre-processing (D/weak, verified)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f3]] [method] Dragons, heralds, towers, wards and first blood dropped as unimportant at 10 minutes (by assertion and low Gini importance, not by testing). Pre-processing (D/weak, verified)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f4]] [method] Logistic regression on kills, deaths, gold diff, XP diff: 73.1% accuracy on a random 40% test split; gold diff alone 72.4%. Table 1 (D/weak, verified)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f7]] [method] (inferred) A fair minute-10 floor for us: logistic on gold diff alone should reach about 72-73% in high Elo; lower Elo games are noisier, so expect a little less. Anything we build must beat gold-diff-only at each minute, not just overall. (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f2]] [method] Features: 216-dim one-hot, one block per side (Radiant heroes, Dire heroes). Section IV.A (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f3]] [method] [data] Side-swap averaging at prediction: score the match as is and with the teams swapped, average p and 1 - p_swapped, so the model uses both side configurations. Section IV.B (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f4]] [method] LR learning curve plateaus: test accuracy approaches 69.8% at about 18,000 training games; train and test accuracy close (no overfitting, so more data does not help an additive model). Section IV.C (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f5]] [method] Weighted kNN (pair and higher interactions via match similarity) reached 67.4% in CV and nearly 70% at 50,000 games, still rising, i.e. interaction models need more data than LR before they pay. Section IV.D (C/fair, verified)
- [[conley-perry-2013#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Random-hero modes (all random, random draft) in the sample make hero strength vary more than in chosen drafts, which may inflate draft-only accuracy relative to a pure draft queue. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f1]] [method] Importance as predictive power: the restricted model f_S marginalises missing features over their conditional distribution, and v_f(S) is the drop in loss versus the mean prediction. Section 2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f2]] [method] Three families: drop one feature (permutation, conditional permutation, mean imputation, ablation), use one feature alone (univariate), or all subsets (SAGE). Ablation fails for correlated features; univariate misses complementary ones. Section 2.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f3]] [method] SAGE = Shapley values of v_f: they sum to the model's total predictive power (efficiency), and perfectly correlated features get equal shares (symmetry), under the conditional formulation. Section 3.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f4]] [method] SAGE equals the average over the data of per-row SHAP values of the loss, but computing it that way needs 2-4 orders of magnitude more model evaluations than their sampler. Section 3.1, Supplement G (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f5]] [method] The practical estimator samples missing features from the marginal, not the conditional, which moves it towards the model's mechanism and away from information value. Section 3.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f6]] [method] With marginal sampling, perfectly correlated features may not get equal shares, and a feature the model ignores gets zero even if a correlated one carries its information. Supplement D.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f8]] [method] [stats] Sum of importances vs loss of models retrained on feature subsets (correlation): SAGE best or near best, but permutation is close on tabular data (Bank 0.9937 vs 0.9918, Bike 0.9815 vs 0.9798, Credit 0.9565 vs 0.9571); ablation collapses with many features (MNIST -0.4536). Table 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us: SAGE over 5-7 feature groups at minute t gives a split of the model's log-loss skill that sums to the whole, which LOCO cannot (LOCO gives unique shares that sum to less). With groups, the correlated-within-group problem disappears; between-group correlation (gold vs t... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f10]] [method] (inferred) A universal-predictive-power version (refit a model per subset of groups) is affordable with 6 groups: 2^6 = 64 LightGBM fits per minute bucket, exact Shapley over groups with no sampling and no extrapolation; this is SAGE's v(S) rather than v_f(S). (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f5]] [method] Class-only models (class per pick plus number of unique classes, own team only) score about chance: gradient boosting 0.516, SVM 0.515. (D/weak, verified)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f6]] [method] Final SVM: champion names, unique-class count, 7 class-count columns, plus the opponent's same features (26 inputs): 0.55 accuracy. No ablation of class counts vs champion ids. (D/weak, verified)
- [[cram678-comp-winrate#^f7]] [method] Checked, not there: log loss, AUC, a champion-id-only baseline, a temporal split, damage type, CC or any profile feature beyond class. (D/weak, absence)
- [[csgo-skill-rating-2024#^f5]] [method] Choosing matches the model is unsure about (likeliest draw) adds 1-1.5 points over random selection at equal training size. (C/fair, verified)
- [[csgo-skill-rating-2024#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: pro CS:GO ratings reach 62-64% because pro matches are not skill-balanced; solo-queue LoL is matchmade, so the same systems sit near 50-55% ([[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f3]], [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f4]]). The one transferable point: ratings per player beat r... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f1]] [method] An action's value is the change it makes to the probability of scoring soon plus minus the change to the probability of conceding soon (offence plus defence). Section 3.1, eq. 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f2]] [method] Scoring and conceding are kept separate because their effects can be asymmetric and context dependent. Section 3.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f3]] [method] Labels use a short horizon: did the team score (or concede) within the next k = 10 actions, not the match result. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f4]] [method] The state is approximated by the last three actions plus scoreline context (goals for, against, difference), because teams change play with the score. Section 4.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f5]] [method] [stats] Probabilities must be well calibrated; gradient boosted trees (CatBoost) won, Brier and AUC used for evaluation. Section 4, 6.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f6]] [method] Values carry team and league strength: valuable actions are easier in weaker leagues and in top clubs with strong teammates. Section 7 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For LoL the short-horizon idea maps to "next tower or inhibitor within N minutes" or "gold lead in 5 minutes" as well as the final win, which is far less noisy per event than win/loss and gives a gold equivalent directly. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f10]] [method] (inferred) Their scoreline features are our gold, kill and tower differences at the event minute; the "strong teammates" leak is our pre-game strength (draft win chance, average rank), which belongs in the state too. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f1]] [method] Recipe: train with a proper scoring rule (log loss or Brier), M = 5 networks from random initialisations, average predictions. Algorithm 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f2]] [method] Bagging is a good diversity source for convex learners without intrinsic randomness; for NNs random init plus shuffling on full data was enough and bagging hurt. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f3]] [method] Even 5 members significantly improve uncertainty quality; adversarial training is optional. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f4]] [method] Cost: M times the parameters and training (trivially parallel); can be distilled into one model. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-ensembles-2017#^f5]] [method] (inferred) A convex logistic model has a single optimum, so random-init ensembling does nothing for it; ensembling pays only once we use non-convex models (FM, set nets) and mainly improves calibration, i.e. log loss, not AUC. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[deep-sets#^f1]] [method] Any permutation-invariant function of a set from a countable universe (e.g. champions) can be written as rho(sum of phi(x)): embed each element, sum, then apply a network. Theorem 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f2]] [method] DeepSets architecture: transform each element independently, add the representations, then process the sum with a normal network; optional conditioning on extra information. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f3]] [method] Treating a set as a sequence (LSTM, GRU) generalised worse than DeepSets with similar parameter counts; trained on 100k sets. Section 4.1.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[deep-sets#^f4]] [method] (inferred) A logistic model on summed champion one-hots is already a DeepSets with linear phi and rho; the gain from a deep version comes only from the non-linear rho, i.e. learning team-level composition effects (damage mix, frontline) from the summed embedding. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f2]] [method] Protocol: games sorted by time, new players get default ratings (1500 Elo and Glicko, 25 TrueSkill), predict from current ratings then update; default parameters (k = 10, beta 4.16, tau 0.833). (C/fair, verified)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f5]] [method] "Best players" set-up: top 1000 by most recent rating, scored on their first 10 games; LoL 76-78% with MAX. (C/fair, verified)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f6]] [method] Their conclusion: MAX (team = best member) beats SUM in most cases, so the strongest player carries more weight. (C/fair, verified)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f7]] [method] (inferred) The best-players set-up selects on the outcome: a player ranked top by final rating has, by construction, won more of the games being scored, so the 76% is leakage, and the MAX advantage there may be the same artefact (MAX keys on the very player selected). (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f8]] [method] (inferred) For us: about 50% for ratings from scratch on sparse LoL data matches our own experience that player-history terms help only once a player has earlier games (53.2% to 54.5% overall). Ratings need history; a cold start is worth nothing, so per-slot features must say how much hi... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Aggregation matters for us too: a team score as the sum of five slot terms is the default; a max or spread term (best player's edge, weakest player's deficit) is a cheap test on our net. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f1]] [method] [stats] Team success as a function on lineups splits orthogonally into first (individual), second (pair) up to fifth-order parts; ANOVA is a special case. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f3]] [method] [stats] Raw shares are misleading because higher-order spaces have more dimensions; they compare against the shares after randomly permuting lineup values. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f8]] [stats] [method] (inferred) The permutation null is cheap and transfers directly: fit our pair block (ally role pairs, enemy role pairs) on real data and on data with outcomes shuffled within patch, and compare the pair-block share of explained log loss (or the fitted pair variance) with the shuf... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f2]] [method] Features: one-hot heroes per side plus three advantage features from a synergy matrix (pair win rate as teammates) and a counter matrix (pair win rate as opponents); model is logistic regression. `README.md`, `preprocessing/dataset.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f3]] [method] Headline: about 0.65 ROC AUC with LR or neural nets; example run at 4500+ MMR: 112,057 train games, 8,390 test, test AUC 0.628, accuracy 59.2%. `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f4]] [method] Neural networks matched LR at best, after much tuning. `README.md` FAQ (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f5]] [method] Learning curve plateaus after about 200k games; author suggests at least 150k. `README.md` FAQ (C/fair, verified)
- [[dota2-predictor#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Possible leakage: by default the synergy and counter matrices are loaded from `pretrained/synergies_all.csv` and `counters_all.csv` (named "all") for both train and test sets, so pair win rates likely include the test games' outcomes. `preprocessing/dataset.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftcompromise#^f1]] [tools] [method] Compared recommendations from its own tool and four existing LoL draft tools by win rate and champion choice. (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f6]] [method] [ux/trust] Explicitly stats-only, no opinions; not ML. `FAQDialog.tsx` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f7]] [stats] [method] Matchup expectation is additive on an Elo-like logit scale: expected rating = rating(A's role win rate) - rating(B's role win rate), with rating(w) = -400 log10(1/w - 1). `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts`, `packages/core/src/rating/ratings.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f8]] [stats] [method] [ux/uncertainty] Thin matchups are shrunk with pseudo-games: priorGames games at the additive expected win rate are added to the observed wins and games, then the matchup's rating is reported as the deviation from that expectation. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f1]] [method] [data] Time split: matches sorted by timestamp, first 85% train, next 5% validation, last 10% test. Section 5.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f3]] [method] Baselines re-implemented under one protocol: majority class, L2 logistic regression, a feed-forward NN, HOI (factorisation machine with pairwise player interactions), OptMatch (GNN hero embeddings plus self-attention), NeuralAC (attention over synergy and competition). Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f4]] [method] Post-draft accuracy, Table 9. LoL top 0.1%: majority 50.4%, LR 53.2%, NN 53.4%, HOI (FM) 53.4%, NeuralAC 53.5%, DraftRec without history 54.3%, OptMatch (with history) 54.5%, DraftRec 56.2%. Dota 2: majority 51.8%, LR 61.3%, NN 61.1%, HOI 60.8%, OptMatch 61.1%, NeuralAC 61.1%, DraftRec 6... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f5]] [method] On Dota 2 plain logistic regression was the best post-draft predictor. (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f6]] [method] Models with players' match histories beat draft-only ones on both datasets in the per-turn setting. Section 5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f7]] [method] (inferred) In LoL high elo, draft-only architectures (LR, NN, FM, attention) span 53.2 to 54.3% accuracy, about 1 point; adding player history adds about 2 more. On 50k mixed-rank Dota games all reach about 61%, no model beats LR. From ^f4. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f8]] [method] History length: gains from player history converge around the last 50 games. Appendix B.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f9]] [method] Metrics are accuracy and mean absolute error only; checked, not there: no AUC, log loss or calibration. Section 5.2 (B/fair, absence)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f10]] [method] (inferred) Dota draft-only accuracy (61%) is far above LoL's (53%) on the same models; mixed ranks and the 2015 meta (stronger heroes, more imbalance) are likely causes, so Dota accuracies do not transfer as targets for ranked LoL. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f11]] [method] Training setup for all re-implemented models: 2 layers, Adam (0.9, 0.999), gradient clipping at l2 norm 5, 64 dims per attention head. Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f12]] [method] Common tuning grid: learning rate {1e-2, 1e-3, 1e-4} with cosine annealing, l2 {1e-2 to 1e-5}, dropout {0, 0.1, 0.2}, hidden size d {64, 128, 256}, batch {256, 512}; history length L {0 to 100} for history models. Checked, not there: chosen values per model, epoch counts, early stopping ... (B/fair, absence)
- [[draftrec-2022#^f13]] [method] Multi-task: DraftRec's outcome head is trained jointly with the champion recommendation head; team vectors are average-pooled player representations, subtracted, then two fully connected layers and a sigmoid. Section 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f1]] [stats] [method] P-splines: many equally spaced basis functions plus a difference penalty on neighbouring coefficients; smoothness is set by the penalty weight alone. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f3]] [stats] [method] For data already on an equally spaced discrete grid, use the identity basis: one value per point, a banded system; this is Whittaker's 1923 smoother for life tables. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f6]] [stats] [method] Lambda need not be hand-tuned: in the mixed-model view it is the ratio of residual variance to the variance of the differences, re-estimated in a few iterations. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f8]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve: y_t = mean diff at minute t, weight w_t = n_t / s_t^2 (games alive at t over within-minute variance); solve (W + lambda D2'D2) z = W y, a pentadiagonal system of ~30 unknowns. Thin late minutes get small weight and follow the smooth trend instead o... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[esta-csgo-2022#^f1]] [method] [data] Set inputs: each player is an element (position, HP, equipment, flags) with global state copied in; vector inputs aggregate per team. One game state sampled per round, random 70/10/20 split, per map. Section 5.2-5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[esta-csgo-2022#^f2]] [method] Configs: Deep Sets with one dense layer encoder and one dense layer decoder, mean pooling; Set Transformer with one ISAB and one head, PMA decoder; hidden 128, batch 32, Adam 1e-3, 100 epochs, early stop 10. Section 5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[esta-csgo-2022#^f3]] [method] [data] Data: 1,558 demos in all, 132-289 per map (one sampled state per round). Section 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[esta-csgo-2022#^f4]] [method] Result: vector models, especially the plain MLP, beat both set models on log loss; Deep Sets slightly beat Set Transformer. Dust2 LL: LightGBM 0.433, XGBoost 0.437, MLP 0.415, Deep Sets 0.435, Set Transformer 0.458. Section 5.5, Table 1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[esta-csgo-2022#^f5]] [method] (inferred) At small data the attention model was the worst of five: a set architecture is not a free win over sensible aggregate features, and the simplest permutation-invariant pooling beat attention. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f1]] [method] Binary loss: Brier score (squared error), regressing the student on the teacher's positive-class probability for synthetic rows and on 0/1 for real rows; it slightly beat their multiclass strategy, as in Bucila. Section 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f2]] [method] Synthetic rows: Gibbs sampling from a masked self-attention model of each feature given the rest, started at real rows; one round (GIB-1) was best; ten times the training rows, capped at 10^6. Section 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Binary tasks, mean test accuracy of the best single student: standard training 82.85, Hinton soft labels on real rows only (KNOW) 83.89, MUNGE 83.72, GIB-1 84.21, teacher ensemble 84.40: GIB-1 keeps about 88% of the teacher's gain, soft labels alone about 67%. Table 1 (cells spli... (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f4]] [method] Distillation gave nothing where the teacher was no better than the best single model. Section 5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f5]] [method] Neural students gain most; tree students (LightGBM, CatBoost, RF) gain less but are often still the best single model after distillation. Section 5.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f6]] [method] Distilled single models were over 10x faster and more accurate than H2O/AutoSklearn ensembles. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f7]] [method] Longer Gibbs chains drift from the data distribution and hurt: stay close to real rows. Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f8]] [method] (inferred) The draft analogue of GIB-1: take a real draft, resample one slot's champion from its conditional given the other nine (we can estimate it from pick rates by role and co-pick counts), label with the teacher ensemble. One swap per synthetic draft keeps it near the manifold (^f7... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Expect soft labels on real games alone to give the smaller share (^f3: ~67% vs ~88% with synthetic rows); synthetic drafts are the cheap extra and run on the gpu-box (teacher inference) once per teacher. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f1]] [method] FMs beat Poly2 (free pair weights) on sparse data because each feature's vector is learned from all its other pairs, so rare or unseen pairs still get sensible predictions. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f2]] [method] Accuracy vs speed trade-off: FFM best logloss, LM fastest but worst, FM a good balance; on Criteo (45.8M rows) private logloss LM 0.462, Poly2 and FM about 0.449, FFM 0.446 (Table 3, no verbatim quote). Section 4.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f3]] [method] When FFM helps: most features categorical and the one-hot set highly sparse; not when data is dense or interactions don't matter (adult: all models matched the linear one). Section 4.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f4]] [method] FFMs overfit easily and logloss is sensitive to the number of epochs; they use validation early stopping, which worked better than other tricks they tried. Section 4.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f5]] [method] Model size is O(n f k), n features times f fields times k; latent sizes used were small (k = 4 for FFM vs 40-100 for FM in Table 3). Section 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ffm-juan-2016#^f6]] [method] (inferred) A draft has natural fields (ally role slots, enemy role slots); FFM would let a champion's synergy vector differ from its counter vector. With 170 champions, 10 fields and k = 4, that is ~7k parameters, cheap at 10^5 games. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f1]] [method] [stats] MNIST: depth-8 soft tree 94.45% on labels, 96.76% on soft targets (mixed with true labels), teacher net 99.21%: about half the gap. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f2]] [method] [stats] Connect4 (game outcome from board): 78.63% on labels, 80.60% distilled. (C/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f3]] [method] [stats] Letter: 78.0% on labels, 81.0% distilled from an ensemble at 95.9%: under a fifth of the gap. (C/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f4]] [method] (inferred) A restricted student keeps only the share of the teacher that fits its form; for an additive draft student that share is the additive plus chosen-pair part. Expect a gap and measure it rather than assume the 80-97% figures from flexible students. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f1]] [method] No universally superior solution between GBDT and deep models; a tuned ResNet-like MLP is a strong baseline and FT-Transformer wins most of their tasks. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f2]] [method] Tuning makes plain MLP and ResNet competitive; MLP stays a good sanity check. Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f3]] [method] Protocol: Optuna TPE (Bayesian) tuning on the validation set, 15 seeds per tuned config, ensembles of 5 averaged models; comparing ensembles because GBDT is itself an ensemble and DL benefits more from ensembling. Section 4.3, 4.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f4]] [method] Cost: FT-Transformer needs more hardware and time than ResNet; attention is quadratic in the number of features. Section 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f5]] [method] DL beating GBDT on most of their tasks reflects a benchmark tilted to DL-friendly problems; tuned GBDT dominates on some. Section 4.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f6]] [method] (inferred) With 10 categorical slots, FT-Transformer is a Set-Transformer-like model over pick tokens; tokens are few, so the cost concern is training time per epoch, not attention size. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f7]] [method] Training protocol: AdamW, no learning rate schedule, a predefined batch size per dataset, early stopping with patience 16 epochs; categoricals as embeddings of one shared size. Section 4.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f8]] [method] Default FT-Transformer (an untuned educated guess): 3 layers, embedding size 192, 8 heads, ReGLU with FFN factor 4/3, attention dropout 0.2, FFN dropout 0.1, residual dropout 0, AdamW lr 1e-4, weight decay 1e-5 (0 for tokenizer, LayerNorm, biases). Table 12 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f9]] [method] Ensembles of the default FT-Transformer were about as good as ensembles of tuned ones. Section 4.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f10]] [method] FT-Transformer tuning space: 1-4 layers (1-6 on large sets), embedding 64-512, residual dropout {0, U[0, 0.2]} (0 on large sets), attention and FFN dropout U[0, 0.5], FFN factor U[2/3, 8/3], lr LogUniform[1e-5, 1e-3] (3e-5 to 3e-4 on large sets), weight decay LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-3], 100 ... (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f11]] [method] Tuning outcomes: attention dropout always helped and FFN dropout was usually non-zero, but residual-branch dropout was rarely chosen; PreNorm kept optimisation simple without warmup. E.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f12]] [method] ResNet tuning space: 1-8 blocks (1-16 large), width 64-512 (64-1024 large), hidden factor U[1, 4], hidden dropout U[0, 0.5], residual dropout {0, U[0, 0.5]}, lr LogUniform[1e-5, 1e-2], weight decay {0, LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-3]}; tuned inner dropout usually above outer, outer often 0. Table... (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f13]] [method] MLP tuning space: 1-8 layers (1-16 large), width 1-512 (1-1024 large), dropout {0, U[0, 0.5]}, lr LogUniform[1e-5, 1e-2], weight decay {0, LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-3]}, 100 iterations. Table 15 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ft-transformer-2021#^f14]] [method] Batch size values per dataset in Table 7 were lost in the text snapshot (column not extracted). (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f1]] [method] [stats] Window size trade-off: a short window adapts fast but hurts in stable periods; a long window is better when stable but reacts slowly. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f2]] [method] [stats] Recency is not relevance when data is noisy or concepts recur; slow change longer than the window also defeats windowing. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f3]] [method] [stats] Abrupt forgetting raises reactivity but also the risk of fitting noise. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f4]] [method] [stats] Gradual forgetting keeps all examples and weights them by age, e.g. exponential w = exp(-lambda j), lambda = 0 being equal weights. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f5]] [method] In Netflix-style collaborative filtering, windowing and instance weighting were not the best choice; the winners modelled each kind of change separately (Koren 2010). Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f6]] [method] (inferred) With a label as noisy as a LoL win (f2, f3), a hard last-patch window fits noise; exponential decay with lambda tuned on a time-split validation set, or explicit time-varying champion terms (f5), are the candidates. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gcn-wp-2022#^f1]] [method] Graph: one node per team-game with that game's stat deltas (gold, kills, objectives, vision vs opponent), edges to the opponent's node and the team's previous game; homogeneous Kipf GCN, semi-supervised node classification. Algorithm 1, section IV-A (D/weak, verified)
- [[gcn-wp-2022#^f2]] [method] Leak handling by label shifting: the symmetric adjacency lets convolution look forward, so a one-convolution model predicts further ahead. Section IV-C (D/weak, verified)
- [[gcn-wp-2022#^f4]] [method] Table IV accuracy: GCN-cheby 1 layer delta 0.619, SCOPE (tuned Elo) 0.597, random forest on last-5-game deltas 0.578, GCN-cheby 2 layers 0.568, plain GCN 0.551. More layers hurt. (D/weak, verified)
- [[gcn-wp-2022#^f5]] [method] Checked, not there: no game counts for the test season, no intervals or repeated runs, no log loss, no LR or TrueSkill baseline, no draft or player-level inputs, no solo-queue data. (D/weak, absence)
- [[gcn-wp-2022#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: nothing transferable. Pro leagues are a dense round-robin graph of 10 fixed teams; ranked solo queue is a sparse graph of ad hoc teams, where the "neighbourhood" of a game is mostly strangers. (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f1]] [stats] [method] Treat every ANOVA row (main effects, interactions) as a batch of random effects with mean 0 and a standard deviation estimated from data. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For us: the matchup interaction gamma_AB (beyond A's average lead and B's average deficit) is the batch to shrink; if its estimated variance per role and minute is small, most thin matchups should collapse to the additive prediction. Floor tau^2 above zero (or report i... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f1]] [stats] [method] Multilevel model: the coefficients get a model of their own whose parameters are estimated from the data. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f2]] [stats] [method] County intercept alpha_j ~ N(gamma0 + gamma1 u_j, sigma_alpha^2): shrunk towards a regression on the county-level predictor u_j (uranium), and sigma_alpha is the spread left beyond what u_j explains. Section 2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Our kit-group slope maps to gamma: a champion-role's slope on an enemy trait ~ N(role mean + sum over its groups of gamma_g, tau^2). The champion's own part is then its deviation from what role and groups predict, so the group part and the champion part don't overlap by constr... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f1]] [stats] [method] Group-level predictor: county intercepts ~ N(gamma0 + gamma1 u_j, sigma_alpha^2); with lmer the group predictor is copied to each row (u.full, the county value per house). Section 12.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f4]] [stats] [method] Varying slopes with a group-level predictor: (alpha_j, beta_j) bivariate normal with means gamma0 + gamma1 u_j each; fitted as y ~ x + u + x:u + (1 + x / county). The cross-level interaction x:u is the group predictor's effect on the slope. Section 13.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f6]] [method] The same model is equally a regression with 4 + 2J predictors (constant, v, x, v x, group indicators and their x interactions) and three error terms; which view to show depends on whether the unit's values or the coefficients matter. Section 13.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f11]] [method] (inferred) Our model in their terms: row = seat, x = enemy trait (z), j = champion-role, u_j = kit memberships (0/1, one column per group on that trait) and role. Fit = trait + role:trait + group:trait (the cross-level interactions, ^f4) + (trait / champion-role) random slopes. The group... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f1]] [stats] [method] Tango's method: talent variance = observed variance between teams minus the theoretical luck variance; luck from the binomial. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f2]] [stats] [method] The regression constant k is the number of games where luck SD equals talent SD; add k games of league-average performance to every record. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve at minute t: k_t = sigma_t^2 / tau_t^2, where sigma_t^2 is the within-matchup game-to-game variance of gold diff at t and tau_t^2 the between-matchup variance of true interaction effects; estimate = (n * observed + k_t * baseline) / (n + k_t). This ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[gelo-2022#^f1]] [method] Graph: players are nodes, an edge joins two who met, weighted by the win-loss gap between them; embeddings come from DeepWalk-style random walks. Section III-B (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f2]] [method] The embeddings only adjust Elo after the fact: active players (above an elbow in games played) get bonus points scaled by cosine similarity to the top player's embedding. Section III-D (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f3]] [method] Time split: 13 time units in date order, rate on two units, score the next two, 10 rolling windows, 5 runs each; metric is the share of matches the higher-rated side loses. Section IV-B (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f4]] [method] Cold start is not addressed: players new in the test window get the average rating, as in Elo. Section IV-B (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f5]] [method] Table I mean error, Elo vs GElo: SC2 0.2634 vs 0.2544, ATP 0.3319 vs 0.3263, CS 1 0.3754 vs 0.3627, CS 2 0.3929 vs 0.3774 (all significant); LoL pro 0.3794 vs 0.3803, not significant. (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f6]] [method] The gain over Elo shrinks as the share of new players grows (slope -0.027, p = 0.082). Section IV-C (C/fair, verified)
- [[gelo-2022#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: no baseline beyond vanilla Elo (no Glicko, TrueSkill, logistic or Bradley-Terry fit on all games), no team-level model (5v5 games still rate by Elo on players), no log loss. (C/fair, absence)
- [[gelo-2022#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us: a who-beat-whom player graph mostly corrects a rating system that updates too slowly; our logistic player terms fit all games jointly, which already lets indirect opponents inform each other, so expect little. And it gives nothing to the 74% of players with no earlier ... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[gh-devrel-1099#^f2]] [method] [policy] Planned AI-powered recommendations for TFT units/items/traits, and LoL later. (C/fair, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f1]] [stats] [method] GLS and Gibbs for crossed random effects cost more than N^(3/2); backfitting inside penalised quasi-likelihood brings it to O(N) per iteration. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f2]] [method] Fit: Schall (1991) penalised IRLS. Each step is a weighted ridge on a working response, with each random-effect batch penalised by 1 / sigma^2 of that batch. Section 3.1, eq. 14 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f3]] [stats] [method] Variance update per batch: sigma^2 = //a//^2 / (R - nu), where nu is the trace of the batch's block of the inverse penalised Hessian over sigma^2, a degrees-of-freedom estimate; an EM step after the Laplace approximation. Section 3.1, eq. 16 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f5]] [method] Gotcha: a fixed predictor that is a property of the row level (an item's material, a customer's profile) overlaps the random effects' span, slowing alternating updates; the penalised estimates for that subset must sum to zero. Fix: update fixed effects together with each random-effect bl... (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f6]] [method] Clubbing mattered much more for binary outcomes than for least squares. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f11]] [method] (inferred) For us: a group-level slope fitted as a fixed coefficient with champion-role random slopes nested inside it is exactly their material/item case. ^f9 says the group slope's se from a fit without the champion level is far too small (the kit groups' p values from pooled seat rows... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f12]] [method] (inferred) ^f2/^f3 is a no-MCMC recipe for us: penalised IRLS with one ridge penalty per batch (role slopes, group slopes, champion-role slopes), and Schall's update for each batch's variance between IRLS passes; at ~8M seat rows and a few thousand slope parameters the exact Hessian bloc... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f1]] [method] [data] Data: every even-strength NHL goal over four seasons, 1,467 players, 18,154 goals; outcome is which side scored. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f2]] [method] Model: logistic on team indicators (alpha) and +1/-1 player indicators (beta) per goal. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f3]] [stats] [method] Priors: Laplace (L1) on player effects for selection, normal (L2) on nuisance team effects; the L1 penalty gets a hyperprior so the data pick it. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f5]] [method] Pair terms: add columns that are row-wise products of pairs of player columns; sparse fitting made it 20 seconds, other solvers failed. (B/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f1]] [method] Tree-based models remain state of the art on medium-sized tabular data (~10k samples), even ignoring their speed. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f2]] [method] The benchmark excludes high-cardinality categoricals and caps training size, so our regime (170-level categorical slots, 10^5+ rows) is outside it. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f3]] [method] At 50k rows the gap between NNs and trees shrinks in most cases (not rigorously studied). Appendix A.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f4]] [method] Tuning does not close the gap: trees win at every random search budget; each NN search iteration is also slower. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f5]] [method] Why NNs lose: they are hurt by uninformative features, their rotation invariance, and difficulty learning irregular functions. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f6]] [method] Very large data and high-cardinality categoricals are listed as open questions. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Our draft data is the opposite of their regime: no uninformative or skewed numerical features, a smooth additive target (champion strength) plus small interactions, and 10x to 1000x more rows. Their result does not transfer; it neither favours nor rules out NNs for us. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f8]] [method] NN protocol: 300 epochs with early stopping and checkpointing (best validation model kept), patience 40 for MLP, ResNet and FT-Transformer; their MLP adds ReduceOnPlateau lr scheduling. Appendix, 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f9]] [method] MLP space and default: 1-8 layers (default 4), width 16-1024 (256), dropout 0-0.5 (0.2), lr LogUniform[1e-5, 1e-2] (1e-3), category embedding 64-512 (128), lr scheduler on, batch {256, 512, 1024} (512). Table 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f10]] [method] FT-Transformer space reuses Gorishniy's (default 3 layers, 192 dims, attention dropout 0.2, FFN dropout 0.1, lr 1e-4, wd 1e-5) and adds batch {256, 512, 1024}, default 512, no lr scheduler by default. Table 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[grinsztajn-tabular-2022#^f11]] [method] ResNet default: 8 blocks of 256, hidden factor 2, hidden and residual dropout 0.2, lr 1e-3, weight decay 1e-7, batchnorm, scheduler on, batch 512. Table 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f1]] [method] Older (shallow) neural networks gave well-calibrated binary probabilities; modern high-capacity ones are miscalibrated. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f2]] [method] Miscalibration comes from overfitting log loss while 0/1 error still improves; overfitting shows in probabilities, not accuracy. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f3]] [method] Less weight decay hurts calibration. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f4]] [method] All calibration methods are post-processing fitted on a held-out validation set; Platt scaling fits sigma(a z + b) on validation NLL with the network frozen. Section 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f5]] [method] Temperature scaling (one parameter) is simplest, fastest and often most effective, and it does not change accuracy. Sections 4.2, 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[guo-calibration-2017#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: judge model changes by held-out log loss, not accuracy (accuracy can improve while probabilities degrade), and refit a Platt or temperature step on a recent validation slice after any non-linear model. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f1]] [method] [stats] Generalisation error falls as a power law in training-set size; empirical exponents sit between -0.07 and -0.35, flatter than the -0.5 theory predicts. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f2]] [method] Better architectures and optimisers shift the curve down (intercept) but do not change its steepness. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f3]] [method] [stats] Three regions: best guessing at small data, then power-law scaling, then a floor at irreducible (Bayes) error. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f4]] [method] [stats] A counting model for a weighted coin flip converges with exponent -0.5, the textbook rate. Section 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f5]] [method] Method cost: for each data shard, re-tune model size (best-fit capacity grows sublinearly with data), so curve points are each tuned, not one fixed model. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f1]] [method] Ensemble teacher: average the members' predictive distributions (arithmetic or geometric mean) as soft targets. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f2]] [method] [stats] Soft targets give more information per case and less gradient variance, so the student needs less data. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f3]] [method] Loss: cross-entropy to soft targets at temperature T, plus a small hard-label term; scale soft gradients by T^2. Section 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f4]] [method] At high temperature distillation equals squared-error logit matching (Ba and Caruana's loss). Section 2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f5]] [method] A small term toward the true labels helps: the student errs toward the right answer. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f6]] [method] [stats] Ten seeds of one architecture (no data variation needed) gave frame accuracy 58.9% to 61.1%; one distilled net reached 60.8%, over 80% of the gain. Table 1, section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f7]] [method] Seed diversity alone was enough for the ensemble; varying training data did not change results. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f8]] [method] [stats] Soft targets as regulariser: on 3% of the data, hard targets overfit (44.5% with early stopping) while soft targets from a full-data model reached 57% (full data 58.9%) without early stopping. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For a binary outcome the softmax over two classes is a sigmoid of one logit, and temperature only rescales that logit; there are no tiny off-class probabilities to reveal, so the useful choice reduces to probability-space cross-entropy (T=1) vs logit L2 (T large). With teacher... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f10]] [method] (inferred) ^f8 is the rare-pair case: a pair with 30 games is our "3% of data". The teacher, having learnt from every game the two champions played, gives each of those 30 games a soft target, and synthetic drafts containing the pair add more; the label-fitted pair term would be mostly n... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f1]] [method] Pre-game features in the Dota literature: one-hot hero vectors (226-dim or 113-dim tri-state), hand-picked hero combos, role counts; plus player skill, player-hero history and friendship ties. Section III-A (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f2]] [method] Across the reviewed Dota work, LR was the most used model and beat neural networks and random forests in several comparisons. Section III-B (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f3]] [method] Reported accuracies are on incomparable datasets (size, bracket, method vary), so direct comparison is not possible. Section III-C (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f4]] [method] Table I lists Semenov et al. 2016 at 5,071,858 matches (FM and XGB, pre-game, normal to very high skill, 70%) and Conley 2013 at 18,000 (top 8%, 70%). (Table text interleaved in raw; read from the layout.) (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f5]] [method] [draft] Higher skill games are harder to predict (Semenov's finding); pros expected to be harder still. Section VI (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f6]] [method] Live (in-game) pro prediction reached 85% at 5 minutes, outside our pre-game scope; listed only so in-game numbers are not confused with draft ones. (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f7]] [method] [data] Percent of game time (used by earlier pro papers) cannot be used live, since length is known only after the game; they predict at clock minutes. Section III-C (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f9]] [method] One model per minute t, each on a 5-minute window of team features (Radiant, Dire, R minus D and per-minute change for each); features restricted to what the live feed gives: damage, kills, last hits, net worth, tower damage, XP. Section IV-A, Table II (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f10]] [method] At minute 20 LR, RF and LightGBM all land at 77.2-77.5% on mixed data; net worth difference alone gives 74-76%. Pro test: 70.8-74.6%. Section V-C, Table III (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f11]] [method] [draft] Hero combinations alone predicted pro games at only 55.8%: pros pick to counter, so draft adds little there. Section V (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f12]] [method] Neural nets not used: underperformed in prior work and need more data than their 5.7k games. Section V-A (B/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f14]] [method] (inferred) Their 85% at minute 5 on 28 games is noise, not a result; the numbers to keep are the 77% at minute 20 with any model, and net worth diff alone within 1-3 points of all features. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[hok-team-composition-2019#^f4]] [method] Prediction: XGBoost, 5-fold nested CV on third-week games; feature sets are baseline (time), player info (rank, recent win rates), role properties (one- and two-role combinations crossed with per-role player stats) and total. (B/fair, verified)
- [[hok-team-composition-2019#^f5]] [method] Lift from composition: total 70.4% vs player info 67.7% (+2.7 pp); role properties beat player info by 0.9 pp on all games and 1.6 pp on games with equal average rank. (B/fair, verified)
- [[hok-team-composition-2019#^f6]] [method] [draft] Two-role combinations capture almost all of it; three- to five-role (full composition) features add nothing more. (B/fair, verified)
- [[hok-team-composition-2019#^f7]] [method] Checked, not there: no hero-id model, so whether role features add to hero ids is untested; role features are mixed with per-role player win rates, so pure composition effect is not isolated; no AUC or log loss; no time-based split. (B/fair, absence)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f1]] [method] Permute-and-predict (Breiman permutation importance, PDP, ICE) can be highly misleading when features are dependent, and over-emphasises correlated features. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f2]] [method] Mechanism: permuting one of two correlated features creates rows with no training data nearby, so the score depends on how the model extrapolates there. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f3]] [method] [stats] Simulation: at rho 0.9, random forests and neural nets rank x1, x2 above features with larger true coefficients; linear models keep the true order. Strong correlation keeps the bias as N grows. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f4]] [method] Alternatives: conditional importance (resample x_j given the rest), dropped importance (LOCO, refit without x_j), permute-and-relearn, condition-and-relearn; each needs another model, either a refit or a model of x_j given the rest. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f5]] [method] [stats] Under squared error all four target the same quantity, E(f(x) - f_-j(x_-j))^2 (Theorem 2); in simulation they agree on the ordering and shrink the importance of correlated features. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f6]] [method] Compensation effect: refit and conditional measures give a feature only its unique share; two strongly correlated features each look weak. The reliable diagnostic is to remove or permute the pair jointly and refit: a joint importance not recoverable from single-feature scores. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f7]] [method] [stats] Permute-and-relearn tests reject far above 5% for noise features correlated with real ones; substitutes from the true conditional or model-X knockoffs hold about 5%. Any test comparing models with features dropped or permuted shares the issue. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f8]] [method] Authors' gold standard: condition-and-relearn (conditional substitutes plus a refit). Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f9]] [method] Shapley values avoid nothing by default: computed carelessly they share the same extrapolation bias. Section 7 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f10]] [method] (inferred) For us: plain permutation of gold lead in a GBDT that also sees XP lead, kills and towers scores rows like "+5k gold, -3 kills, behind on towers" that never happen at minute 15; per-feature scores from it are not usable. Group the correlated state features (the joint importanc... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f2]] [method] Two families: RDN-Boost (relational dependency networks learning from ground facts) vs XGBoost on 66 hand-built features (pi-ratings, PageRank of the results graph, form, league table context). Sections 3-4, Table 2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f3]] [method] Forward-in-time validation: each season predicted by a model trained on all earlier seasons. Section 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f4]] [method] Feature-based XGBoost classification won (validation RPS 0.2055, challenge test 0.2063, first place); the relational learner came close but trained for weeks vs hours. Sections 5-7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f5]] [method] Ablation: pi-ratings alone 0.2067 RPS; adding PageRank and historical strength 0.2061; all 66 features 0.2055. The graph feature and form add about 0.0006 each. Table 5 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f6]] [method] Authors' reading: most of the signal is the rating; more features gave very small edges. Section 8 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f7]] [method] The bookmaker beat the best model on every league (22,000 games with odds): outside information wins. Section 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: no logistic regression or linear model on the same features, so GBDT vs LR is not measured; no player-level data at all. (B/strong, absence)
- [[hubacek-soccer-gbdt-2019#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us: (d) a GBDT whose main input is a good rating, with dense history features around it, is the shape that wins forward tests; but expect the trees to add little beyond the rating, and the graph-derived rating (PageRank) to add less than a tenth of a percent. Our equivalen... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f1]] [method] [stats] Plus-minus models face both kinds of interaction we care about: teammates and opponents. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f3]] [stats] [method] Alternative to CV: Fearnhead and Taylor 2011 estimate the prior variances (separate offence and defence Gaussians) by maximum likelihood, an empirical Bayes variance component per block. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f4]] [method] [stats] NHL logistic plus-minus (Gramacy et al. 2013): L1 or L2 penalised logistic regression on which side scored each goal, MCMC for the full posterior, so pairwise "player A better than B" probabilities. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f6]] [stats] [method] Thomas et al. 2013 screened the 1,000 pairs with most shared minutes, lasso to select, MCMC to fit: 221 pairs non-zero. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f7]] [method] Cost: MCMC took up to 60 processor hours for 200,000 outcomes and 2,600 variables. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f13]] [method] Checked, not there: no review of factorised (low-rank) pair models, no variance share of pair terms vs main effects, no esports. (A/fair, absence)
- [[hyperband#^f1]] [lab] [method] Hyperband treats tuning as allocating a resource (iterations, data samples or features) to sampled configurations, stopping poor ones early. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f1]] [method] Sequential ignorability: (a) treatment independent of potential mediators and outcomes given pretreatment covariates; (b) mediator independent of potential outcomes given treatment and pretreatment covariates. Introduction (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f2]] [method] Part (b) does not follow from randomising the treatment, and only pretreatment covariates may be conditioned on. Sequential ignorability assumption (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f3]] [method] Under that assumption average causal mediation effects are identified without functional form assumptions, so any mediator and outcome model can be used. Nonparametric identification (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f4]] [method] Estimator: draw the mediator from the mediator model under treatment t', then the outcome from the outcome model under treatment t and that mediator draw; average (quasi-Bayesian draws or nonparametric bootstrap for intervals). Estimation algorithms (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f5]] [method] [stats] Mediation and direct effects differ by treatment arm (delta(1) vs delta(0)) when there is interaction; they sum to the total only under no interaction or when averaged across arms. Defining causal mediation effects (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f10]] [method] Continuous (nonbinary) treatment: define effects for chosen treatment levels against a baseline and plot them over treatment intensity; the same algorithms apply. Nonbinary treatment (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f11]] [method] [stats] Sensitivity analysis: rho, the correlation between mediator and outcome model errors, is 0 under sequential ignorability; report the rho at which the effect, and its interval, cross zero. Sensitivity analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f12]] [method] Rho can be restated as how much variance an omitted confounder explains in the mediator and outcome (R-squared, pseudo-R-squared for binary), with a sign assumption; e.g. "ability" would need 60% and 30%. Coefficients of determination (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f14]] [method] They call a mediation analysis incomplete without sensitivity analysis. Sensitivity analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f15]] [method] Checked, not there: multiple mediators, panel or time-varying mediators, multilevel data left for future work. Introduction, Concluding remarks (A/strong, absence)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f16]] [method] (inferred) For us: treatment = draft score (continuous), so report mediation for a fixed contrast (e.g. draft win logit at its 25th vs 75th percentile, or a one-sd change) and a curve over treatment levels, not one number. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f17]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: the natural plug-in is our own models. Mediator model = gold@14 (or the 14:00 state) given draft and pre-game covariates; outcome model = win given draft, state and covariates (meiolol-state's model at minute 14). Simulate: for each game, draw M under draft d',... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f1]] [method] CANN implementation: the base model's log prediction joins the net's output node via a skip connection, not the hidden layers; output weights either fixed at 1 (fixed CANN) or trainable (flexible CANN). 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f2]] [method] [stats] CANN on a GBM base was lowest deviance for frequency on all four sets; the plain FFNN was the one model slightly worse on the low-feature set. 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f3]] [method] Trainable output weights (flexible CANN) beat fixed weights in most cases. 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f4]] [method] Small nets suffice: tuning ranges were 1-4 hidden layers of 10-50 nodes, dropout 0-0.1, batch 10k-50k for frequency. Table 3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f5]] [method] Seed variance: every model trained 3 times and out-of-sample loss averaged, with 20% of training data held out for early stopping. 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f6]] [method] Learned categorical embeddings (autoencoder) helped the plain FFNN but made negligible difference to the CANN for frequency. 4.4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f7]] [method] (inferred) A residual net on a good base inherits its main effects, so the input encoding and net size matter less; gains over the base are small and need paired tests (Diebold-Mariano) at our noise level. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[invenglobal-five-factors#^f1]] [method] Method: random forest feature importances ranking factors in a team's win. (D/weak, verified)
- [[invenglobal-five-factors#^f4]] [method] Checked, not there: data size, patch, how support type was labelled, any accuracy or lift from champion attributes; damage mix, team tank count. (D/weak, absence)

- [[itero-draft-model#^f1]] [method] [tools] Pro-play model: predicts results from both drafts on top of bookmaker odds; built over about a year. (D/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f2]] [method] (claim) Bookmakers average 63-65% accuracy on pro games; adding draft data reaches 70-73%. (D/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f3]] [method] Model is stacked: sub-models (simple linear models, gradient boosted trees) predict parts of the game and are combined. (D/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f5]] [method] Features tested include solo queue data for pro games, champion strength vs player experience on it, counters and synergies, kept only if they improve held-out prediction. (D/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f6]] [method] Checked, not there: no data size, log loss, AUC, rank or solo queue draft-only figure. (D/fair, absence)
- [[itero-draft-sq#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: how AD share is computed (champion averages vs realised in-game damage), bucket counts, or any lift of the feature in iTero's model. (C/weak, absence)
- [[itero-draft-sq#^f10]] [method] Mastery dominates its feature list: sub-5k mastery 41.5% vs 54.5% at 500k+. (C/weak, verified)
- [[itero-home#^f3]] [tools] [method] AI models are customisable on the paid tier; free tier with ads includes all features shown. (B/weak, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f1]] [method] Names the bias we care about: some actions are taken when already ahead (or as a last resort), inflating (or deflating) their raw win rates. Section 4.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f2]] [method] Win probability added for an action is win chance at the end of its effect window minus at its start (end of game if the effect lasts). Section 4.3, eq. 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f3]] [method] [stats] Two statistics per action: mean initial win chance (selection: is it taken in winning states) and mean win probability added (impact), averaged over many similar states. Section 4.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f6]] [method] State features: per-player position, level, gold, damage dealt and taken, K/D/A; per-team voidgrubs, dragons, barons, turrets, inhibitors; time; average rank as the only pre-game feature. Table 5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f11]] [method] (inferred) The effect-window choice matters for objectives: Baron's window is its buff (about 3 minutes, then until the push resolves); measuring to game end folds in everything after. A fixed short window (e.g. 2 to 5 minutes) is the VAEP-style answer and is less noisy. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f12]] [method] (inferred) Their recipe on our data, per event type: W (selection) and delta-W (impact) by minute band and lead band, game-clustered bootstrap for intervals, and a gold equivalent from the model's win-per-gold slope at that minute. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f13]] [method] The win model is per state, not a sequence: earlier states are ignored, assuming momentum is negligible (as found in traditional sports). Section 5.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f14]] [method] Secondary report of Silva et al. 2018 (the first LoL in-game RNN): 7,621 pro games 2015-2018, pre-game categorical inputs plus in-game time series, accuracy 63.9% at 5 minutes to 83.5% at 25. Section 3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[janzing-2020-causal-shapley#^f1]] [method] Claim: dropping a feature should average it over its unconditional (marginal) distribution, not the conditional; SHAP's practical approximation is therefore the right quantity and "improving" it towards conditionals is a mistake. Abstract (B/strong, verified)
- [[janzing-2020-causal-shapley#^f2]] [method] The causality is that of the algorithm: the inputs cause the output; real world causal relations between features are explicitly out of scope. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[janzing-2020-causal-shapley#^f3]] [method] With f(x1, x2) = x1 and x1, x2 perfectly dependent, conditional Shapley gives the unused x2 non-zero credit (fails Sensitivity); interventional gives zero. Section 3, Example 1 and Lemma 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[janzing-2020-causal-shapley#^f4]] [method] SHAP's tree explainer was changed to the interventional form after this work. Section 3, footnote 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[janzing-2020-causal-shapley#^f5]] [method] (inferred) For us: interventional SHAP answers "which inputs does this GBDT read", a model-audit question. "How much does gold lead matter to winning" is a question about games, where Janzing's argument does not apply and the off-manifold rows it needs (gold +5k with towers -3 at minute ... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f1]] [method] [stats] Draft-only on patch 16.6 test (15,092 games, trained on 16.1-16.5): every model lands at AUC about 0.554 and 53.5-53.8% accuracy; log loss 0.6883-0.6894 vs constant 0.6930. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.1 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f2]] [method] Per-role champion strength logistic regression (L2, C=0.01) is not beaten by pairs, factorization machine or transformer; differences within bootstrap noise. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f3]] [method] XGBoost on the same one-hot inputs is significantly worse (-0.0009 log loss vs strength, CI excludes 0). `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f4]] [method] Transformer and FM needed a warm start from the fitted logistic regression; from scratch, mini-batch noise kept the strength part below the logistic optimum. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 6.4 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f5]] [method] [stats] Symmetry built in: score = side + h(blue, red) - h(red, blue), so swapping teams flips the probability; XGBoost gets both orientations as training rows instead. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f12]] [method] A first draft-only attempt gave probabilities clustered near 0.5, called close to random by the author. `README.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f13]] [method] (inferred) Its 53.5% / AUC 0.554 at 73-110k games matches meiolol's 53.3-53.5% / AUC 0.550 at 90k almost exactly, so meiolol is at the honest draft-only level for that data size. (C/strong, unquoted)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f14]] [method] Transformer: score = side + h(blue, red) - h(red, blue), h = per-role champion strength (embedding of size 1 per champion x role) + an interaction module; interaction is a pre-norm transformer encoder over 10 picks (champion + role + team embeddings, a CLS token), width 32, 2 layers, 4 h... (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f15]] [method] FM interaction: champion vector of size k (std 0.01 init) plus a role vector; synergy = per-role-pair weight times dot product over teammate pairs, matchup = per-role-pair weight times e_i K e_j over enemy pairs, K initialised to 0.1 times identity. `src/draft_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f16]] [method] Warm start: before training, side and strength terms are set to a scikit-learn L2 logistic regression with the same penalty (C = 1 / (2 lam_strength)); epoch 0 (pure LR) is kept if interactions never improve validation loss. `src/draft_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f17]] [method] Optimiser: Adam with two parameter groups, strengths at lr 1e-3 (only fine-tuned) and interactions at their own lr; L2 penalties scaled by 1/N added to each mini-batch loss, on weights and embeddings only (lam_strength 50, equal to sklearn C=0.01). No schedule, no gradient clipping. `src... (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f18]] [method] Grids: FM k in {4, 8} x lam_interaction in {5, 20}, lr 1e-3, batch 1024, up to 30 epochs, patience 5; transformer width 32, lam_interaction in {1, 5, 20}, lr 1e-3, batch 512, up to 30 epochs, patience 3; early stopping on validation log loss, best epoch restored. `src/draft_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f19]] [method] [stats] Selected configs: FM k=4, lam 20, best at epoch 14; transformer lam 1, best at epoch 2, i.e. the transformer barely moved from the LR warm start before validation loss turned. `outputs/model_comparison/configs.json` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f21]] [method] [stats] Test log loss (patch 16.6): LR strength 0.68851, LR with pair one-hots 0.68834, FM 0.68861, transformer 0.68847, XGBoost 0.68943; accuracy 53.5%, 53.6%, 53.8%, 53.6%, 53.6%. `outputs/model_comparison/results.csv` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f22]] [method] No augmentation: the nets are antisymmetric by construction, so side swap is not needed; only XGBoost trains on both orientations. Unseen champions map to id 0. `src/draft_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jihan-yin-champ-select#^f3]] [method] Best model about 60%; player features beat champion features (champion.gg win rate, matchup win rate) when trained alone. (D/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f3]] [method] Features: blue minus red diffs (team gold, XP, level, CS, damage, per-role stats, dragons, heralds, barons, towers by group, inhibitors by lane, first blood, first tower) plus a 3-minute change of each diff, a cheap stand-in for a sequence model. `xgboost_engineering.py` add_momentum_fea... (C/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f4]] [method] LR and XGBoost tie overall: AUC 0.817 vs 0.816, Brier 0.173 vs 0.174. README (C/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f6]] [method] Economy (gold, XP, levels) alone keeps 99% of full-model AUC, objectives alone 94%; objectives matter most in close games and at high ranks. README (C/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: no draft or champion features, no buff or respawn timers, no sequence model. (C/fair, absence)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f9]] [method] (inferred) These per-band numbers are the bar for our minute model without draft: log loss about 0.67 at minutes 1-5, 0.52 at 11-15, 0.39 at 21-25. A draft term should help most in the 1-10 band, where the state model is near 0.6-0.67. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f1]] [method] Win-rate predictor: a simple 3-layer network over the 10 hero indices, sigmoid output, used as the reward for tree search. Section III.C (C/weak, verified)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f3]] [method] Evaluation: accuracy, AUC and F1 averaged over 10-fold cross validation (random folds, not time). Section IV.B (C/weak, verified)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f4]] [method] Human games (Fig. 7b): NN accuracy 0.617, AUC 0.694; LR 0.568, AUC 0.642; GBDT 0.561, AUC 0.648. AI self-play games: NN 0.847 accuracy, AUC 0.908. (C/weak, verified)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f5]] [method] Every model does worse on human games than AI self-play games, which the authors put down to varied human players. (C/weak, verified)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f6]] [method] (inferred) At 30M one-patch games, an embedding NN over picks beat LR by 0.05 AUC, unlike the 0.00-0.03 gaps elsewhere ([[chen-avatar-embedding-2018#^f4]], [[draftrec-2022#^f4]]). Possible reasons: much more data supports more interaction terms, or a weak LR baseline; unresolved. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[juewu-draft-2020#^f7]] [method] Win-rate predictor training: Adam, learning rate 1e-4; heroes as indices 0-94. Checked, not there: layer widths, embedding size, dropout, epochs, batch size for the predictor (the batch 400 and L2 1e-4 in section IV.C are for the MCTS policy and value network). Section IV.B-C (C/weak, absence)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f1]] [method] A plain MLP with a per-dataset combination of 13 regularisers beat recent tabular architectures and XGBoost on 40 datasets. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f2]] [method] The 13 candidates: batch norm, SWA, lookahead, weight decay, dropout, snapshot ensembles, skip connections, multi-branch, shake-drop, shake-shake, mixup, cutmix, cutout, FGSM adversarial training. Table 1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f3]] [method] Fixed backbone: 9-layer MLP of 512 units, AdamW at lr 1e-3, batch 128, cosine annealing with restarts (15 epochs, multiplier 2), 105 epochs. 5.1-5.2, Table 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f4]] [method] No regulariser or combination was chosen on every dataset; the dataset- specific cocktail beat both the 5 most chosen and the 5 best single regularisers. C.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f5]] [method] Tuning the learning rate per dataset on top did not help significantly (21 wins, 18 losses, p = 0.7). C.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f6]] [method] Cost: BOHB up to 4 days or 40 x D configurations per dataset, one split. 5.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[kadra-cocktails-2021#^f7]] [method] (inferred) The transferable lesson for a low-signal target is "regularise hard and tune the regularisers, not the architecture"; a 9x512 net is far more capacity than our ~0.004 nats of signal needs, so start small (cf. [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f2]]). (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f2]] [method] Input: 295 features of game time, champion composition, gold and XP difference and kills so far, into a 295-256-256-2 MLP. Section IV (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f3]] [method] Argues accuracy is the wrong target for a winner predictor; the output must be a calibrated probability. Section I (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Unlike images, LoL confidences spread evenly over the range, so under- as well as over-confidence matters. Section I (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f8]] [method] (inferred) A pooled ECE near 1% can hide large errors in the early-minute band, where most rows sit near 50%; we should report ECE and log loss per minute band, and fit a temperature (or isotonic) on a held-out game set, per band if needed. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f3]] [method] [stats] Design: regression discontinuity with the half-time score difference as running variable and cutoff zero; a jump in win rate at zero is causal if everything else is continuous there. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f8]] [method] (inferred) LoL designs in this spirit: (a) randomised events: drake elements and the soul type are random, so the win effect of a given soul or drake type (and its interaction with the comp) is identified without the "already ahead" bias, conditional on having taken it; (b) near-even sta... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f1]] [method] [stats] Many delicate shifts happen at once, each with few instances; time windows and instance decay lose too much signal by discarding data. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f2]] [method] [stats] Measured: across exponential decay rates on two model families, quality improved as decay was moderated, best with no decay at all. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f3]] [method] [stats] Why: old data still carries persistent and cross-entity patterns, so down-weighting it loses signal with the noise when data per entity is scarce. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
