- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f4]] [method] What worked: split each item's bias into a stationary part plus a per-time-bin offset (30 bins of ~10 weeks), all L2-regularised; bin size balances resolution against data per bin, and many sizes gave similar accuracy. Section 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f5]] [method] Time-binned item effects gave a small gain (RMSE 0.9799 to 0.9771); user drift mattered more. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Direct analogue for us: champion rating = stationary term + per-patch offset (bin = patch, a known change point) with its own, stronger L2, all games kept at full weight. Champions untouched by a patch keep near-zero offsets. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f1]] [method] Interventional value functions evaluate the model on out-of-distribution rows, so attributions depend on how the model extrapolates; Slack et al. exploited this to hide discrimination from KernelSHAP. Section 3.1.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f2]] [method] Conditional value functions need extra modelling of the data for any model besides trees, and TreeSHAP's leaf-based estimate cannot credit variables the trees do not split on. Section 3.1.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f3]] [method] Under conditional Shapley, adding a redundant copy of a feature changes every feature's attribution: the answer depends on which features are declared players. Section 3.1.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f4]] [method] Causal fix (asymmetric Shapley, Frye et al.): order causal ancestors before descendants so a redundant descendant gets zero; results are sensitive to how much causal knowledge is assumed. Section 3.1.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f5]] [method] Shapley on non-additive models: for a product of independent zero-centred features every feature gets f(x)/d whatever its size, so attributions say little about interactions. Section 3.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f6]] [method] Authors' conclusion: Shapley is ill-suited as a general solution; prefer approaches built for the specific use case. Section 5 (B/strong, verified)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For us: if gold lead, gold per minute and kill lead are all players, each gets a slice and "economy" looks smaller next to dragons than if gold were one feature (^f3). Fix the players as a pre-declared list of groups (economy, towers, dragons, barons and heralds, levels and XP... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[kumar-2020-shapley-problems#^f8]] [method] (inferred) A natural causal order exists in League (kills and CS produce gold, gold and XP produce towers and dragons), so an asymmetric ordering could assign credit "upstream"; but the order is a choice and changes the answer (^f4), so report symmetric group shares and treat any ordered... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f6]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: the honest default is partly conditional (plain mean of games still running at minute t), labelled "games still running"; do not smooth or model across minutes in a way that imputes ended games (a smoother weighted by n_t is fine, it only borrows ac... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[label-smoothing-muller#^f1]] [method] Label smoothing mixes hard targets with the uniform distribution; it prevents over-confidence and improves generalisation and calibration of multi-class networks. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[label-smoothing-muller#^f2]] [method] Label smoothing calibrates about as well as post-hoc temperature scaling. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[label-smoothing-muller#^f3]] [method] Cost: a teacher trained with label smoothing distils much worse into a student. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[label-smoothing-muller#^f4]] [method] Checked, not there: no binary or low-signal tasks, nothing on noisy labels or data size. (A/fair, absence)
- [[label-smoothing-muller#^f5]] [method] (inferred) For a win model whose predictions sit within a few points of 50%, over-confidence is not the failure mode; L2 or early stopping plus post-hoc calibration address the same thing with one less knob. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f1]] [method] [tools] Model: regularised logistic regression on full team comps with champion strength, synergy and counter terms. (C/fair, verified)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f4]] [draft] [method] Argues the lane matchup explains only a small part of champion performance variation, and that pairwise win rates confound counters with champion strength and with duo correlation (Xayah and Rakan). (C/fair, verified)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f5]] [tools] [method] Pick Now scores a candidate over all possible upcoming enemy and ally picks, weighted by how likely each is. (C/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f1]] [stats] [method] Multiple membership: a lower-level unit belongs to several higher-level units at once (students with several teachers), unlike strict nesting. Introduction (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f3]] [method] Model: y_i = b0 + b1 x_i + sum over the unit's groups of w_ji u_j + e_i, u_j ~ N(0, sigma_u^2); weights w (usually shares summing to 1) say how much the unit belongs to each group. Section 3-4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f4]] [method] Weights are a modelling choice; try several as a sensitivity check. Section 3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f5]] [method] Group-level predictors must respect the structure: enter the weighted average of the predictor over the unit's groups. Section 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Two readings for our kit groups. Weights summing to 1 (the standard MM form) say "a champion in two groups is half of each"; weights of 1 each say kit traits add (auto-attack punisher and true damage both apply). Our groups are different traits into different enemy traits (G1 ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f1]] [method] [stats] Mains and interactions are not identifiable: mass can move between them with identical predictions, so contradictory readings describe one model. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f2]] [method] Pure interaction: the variance no smaller subset of features can describe; equals the functional ANOVA decomposition. Abstract, Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f3]] [method] [stats] Even centring is not enough with correlated inputs: x1 x2 terms move strength between mains depending on the chosen centres. Section 1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f4]] [method] Condition: each effect tensor's every one-dimensional slice has mean zero under a weight w; for discrete features fANOVA under uniform w is effect coding. Section 3.2, 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f5]] [method] Mass-moving: subtract each slice's weighted mean and add it to the lower-order term, highest order first, down to the intercept; predictions never change; converges in one pass for uniform weights, a few otherwise. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: overlapping (multiple-membership) groups, shrinkage or priors, uncertainty of purified effects. (A/strong, absence)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For us: champion-role slope = role part + group part + own part is a nested additive model; naming each part needs the fANOVA rule: the own parts have game-weighted mean 0 within each group (and the group parts mean 0 within each role), weights = seat counts in the data (^f6: ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f10]] [method] (inferred) With overlapping groups (Fiora in G1 and G5, Sivir in G3 and G6), slice-means don't nest; the analogue is a weighted projection: own parts orthogonal (by seat weight) to the membership columns and role indicators, i.e. the residual of a weighted regression of own parts on memb... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f3]] [method] BT is additive: a composition's rating is the sum of its champions' ratings. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f4]] [method] On LoL test folds only additive BT beats chance (53.6%); per-composition win value, pairwise win, neural rating and the neural counter table score 50.9-51.1% while reaching 79-91% on training folds (Table 1). (B/fair, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f5]] [method] [stats] The authors read LoL as overfitting: draft counter-picking and the huge composition space make unseen matchups hard; more games needed. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Same pattern as [[jcj-lol-draft#^f2]] at a similar size: an additive champion model is the honest baseline that flexible models fail to beat below a few hundred thousand games. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[linnet-2017#^f1]] [method] Graph: lineups are nodes, a directed edge from i to j when lineup j outscored lineup i, weighted by the margin; node2vec embeddings feed a Bradley-Terry logistic model. Definition 1.1, section 2 (C/weak, verified)
- [[linnet-2017#^f2]] [method] Split: random 80% of a season's matchups to fit, 20% to test. Section 3 (C/weak, verified)
- [[linnet-2017#^f3]] [method] Result: LinNet 67% accuracy, APM 55%, PageRank 53%; Brier 0.19 vs 0.25 for a constant 0.5. Section 3 (C/weak, verified)
- [[linnet-2017#^f4]] [method] Unseen lineups (no node) get a player-overlap weighted average of the team's known lineups' vectors; the author names a regression on players as the better future fix. Section 2, section 4 (C/weak, verified)
- [[linnet-2017#^f5]] [method] Checked, not there: no time split, no lineup-id logistic or lineup-level plus-minus baseline (the like-for-like comparison), no game counts per test lineup. (C/weak, absence)
- [[linnet-2017#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: no evidence for (b). A graph embedding of who beat whom is a smoothed win record; without an id baseline at the same level and a time split, the lift says nothing about beating learnt champion embeddings. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[llambo#^f1]] [lab] [method] An LLM helps BO through zero-shot warm starting, a surrogate model and candidate sampling, and the gains are largest early, while observations are sparse. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-hall-history-nn#^f3]] [method] Test accuracy on engineered features: 59.8%, 84.4% and 70.2% for three networks; raw features about 50%. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-hall-history-nn#^f4]] [method] Dropout and batch norm were tuned for the best testing loss: model selection on the test set. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-hall-history-nn#^f5]] [method] (inferred) "Last 20 games" fetched after the fact can include games played after the predicted one (fetch date not given), so some features are post-game form (LP gained, tilt, rank moves). With 200 test games (about 3.5 points standard error) and architectures spanning 60-84%, the 84.4%... (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[lol-hall-history-nn#^f6]] [method] Admits Riot's matchmaker sees the same history (yet concludes matchmaking is off, not that the result is). (inferred) Pre-game history features compete with MMR, which already summarises them; large gains are a red flag. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-huang-predictor#^f2]] [method] Results: champion picks alone 64% (Naive Bayes); per-player champion win rates 91%; summed per team 92.8%. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-huang-predictor#^f3]] [method] One team's summed win rate alone gave 89%, from a single decision-tree threshold. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-huang-predictor#^f4]] [method] Checked, not there: nothing on whether win rates were read before the match or exclude it. (D/weak, absence)
- [[lol-huang-predictor#^f5]] [method] (inferred) Leakage: a career champion win rate read after the match includes it, and for players with few games on the champion it nearly is the label. One team's five numbers cannot honestly predict 89% of matchmade games when every clean pre-game study lands at 55-60% ([[lol-player-cha... (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[lol-mastery-analysis#^f1]] [draft] [method] About 20% of players in Gold-ish ranked were on champions with under 10k mastery; those won about 44% vs 51.5% above 10k. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-mastery-analysis#^f2]] [draft] [method] Mastery, more exactly its lack, was the best predictor of outcome among the draft factors the author studied, above counter picks. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-mastery-analysis#^f3]] [learning] [method] The win rate crosses 50% around 12,000 mastery, roughly 20 games; beyond that gains are slight. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-mastery-analysis#^f4]] [learning] [method] Per-champion learning curves differ: hardest to pick up (K'Sante, Riven, Ivern, Nunu, Yuumi, Senna) and skill ceiling rankings; jungle is most mastery sensitive, ADC least. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-mastery-analysis#^f5]] [method] Players with 1M+ mastery win no more than 50k-1M (51.73% vs 51.77%), read as one-tricks stabilising at their true elo. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f3]] [method] Checked, not there: no statement that profile stats were taken before the match or exclude it. Since matches were found after they occurred, season win rate, champion games and wins, and the last 20 games probably include it. (C/fair, absence)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f4]] [method] Accuracy: post-draft 72.1% (logistic regression with rolling form features); their reproduction of Chen et al. 60.24%. Pre-draft with both teams' players 65.7%. One player's own pre-draft history 54.30% (pre-draft rows read from Table 3). (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f5]] [method] Feature group ablation (accuracy drop when omitted): champion proficiency 6.5, season history 2.4, global champion stats 2.4, recent matches 2.0, metadata incl. Elo 0.3, champions as categoricals 0.3, global matchups 0.3. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f6]] [method] Proficiency on the chosen champion is the largest post-draft factor; recent matches are about 40% of the pre-draft signal. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f7]] [method] Momentum (last 20 games) adds only 0.1-0.3 points post-draft. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f8]] [method] Logistic regression beat their neural nets post-draft and plateaued at about 70k games. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-momentum-forecasting#^f11]] [method] (inferred) Elo adds 0.3 points because matchmaking equalises it; what survives is skill not captured by MMR (champion-specific skill) and leakage. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[lol-overlays-ai-cheating#^f7]] [draft] [method] Sceptics say draft AI is useless outside pro play because it cannot see players' skill on their champions. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f1]] [method] Headline: 75.1% accuracy (DNN) from player-champion experience after all picks; GBOOST 75.4%, RF 74.7%, SVC 74.3%. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f2]] [method] It says most pre-match LoL predictors land around 55-60%. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f4]] [data] [method] Features: mastery points, the player's ranked win rate on that champion over the 2020 season, season games on it, and recent games on it among the last 20. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f5]] [method] Only win rate and mastery correlated with outcome; games played (season and recent) had none and hurt the models. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f6]] [method] Checked, not there: no statement that the season win rate or mastery were taken before the predicted match or exclude it; the match is each player's latest, so stats pulled afterwards contain it. (D/weak, absence)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f7]] [method] (inferred) That is label leakage: a player with few season games on the champion has a win rate that largely is this game's result (1 game: exactly it). Season games mean 58.95, but the low-count players dominate the signal. The 75% is not a pre-game number. (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[lol-player-champion-experience#^f8]] [method] Split: random 80/20 with stratified 10-fold, no time split. (D/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f2]] [method] [data] Snapshots at a percentage of the final game length (20, 40, 60, 80%), not at a clock minute: at prediction time the length is unknown, so this leaks the outcome-correlated duration. Section IV-C (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f3]] [method] Features: per team kills, first blood, dragons by type (incl. elder), herald, baron, towers, inhibitors, total gold, lane and jungle CS, mean level (Table VI); no draft, no timers, no gold diff. Table VI (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f4]] [method] Eight models; LR best at 20% (accuracy 0.623), LightGBM best at 40-80% (0.854 at 80%, LR 0.848); all GBDTs and RF within about a point of each other, the MLP far behind (0.734 at 80%). Section V, Table VII (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f5]] [method] Random search tuning on LightGBM and RF moved accuracy by a few tenths of a point at most (LightGBM 0.6189 vs 0.6164 at 20%). Table VIII (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f6]] [method] Signal by stage (random-forest importance): total gold leads at every stage; dragon and tower correlations with the win grow with the stage (dragons 0.16 at 40% to 0.43 at 80%). Section V, Tables III-V (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f7]] [method] Authors leave a time-series (sequence) model as future work. Section VI (C/weak, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f9]] [method] (inferred) On match-v5 team aggregates, model family barely matters (GBDT vs LR within a point); gains must come from features (diffs, timers, draft) and from evaluating at clock minutes. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f4]] [method] Table 1 accuracy, LoL: majority class 53.22%, TrueSkill 55.24%, player only 56.75%, champion only 55.62%, player + champion 58.82%, plus player-champion 60.24%. Dota 2: champion only 59.16%, all terms 59.66%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f6]] [method] The logistic models use random 10-fold CV; only TrueSkill is time-split (first 90% of matches to fit, latest 10% to test). (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Random CV with per-player ID weights lets a player's later games inform earlier ones, so the 1.4 to 3.6 point player gains are an upper bound; TrueSkill, the honest time-split player model, gained only 2 points over majority class. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f8]] [method] Authors' guess for Dota 2 showing no player effect: better matchmaking leaves little base-skill difference between teams within a game. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lolalytics-darius-counters#^f4]] [data] [method] Matchup rows show win rate vs, opponent's overall win rate, and two delta measures (Δ1, Δ2) adjusting for champion strength. (B/weak, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f1]] [tools] [method] Hosts a deep learning model predicting match outcome from draft only; desktop app downloadable. `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f6]] [method] Reported draft-only model accuracy 56.1% (solo queue), 56.8% (pro). `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f9]] [method] Model: learned embeddings for each of the 10 champions (126 dims), a patch (64), a champion x patch pair (2 dims), elo and queue (32 each), concatenated in fixed role order and fed to an MLP. `apps/machine-learning/utils/match_prediction/model.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f10]] [method] Champion x patch embedding kept tiny because larger overfits. `config.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f11]] [method] Default MLP: 11 layers 1024 down to 64, dropout 0.5, AdamW weight decay 0.05, one-cycle LR 8e-4, 50 epochs, batch 20480 on GPU. `config.py`, `utils/match_prediction/__init__.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f12]] [method] (inferred) The "residual" add only fires when consecutive layer widths match, so in practice only between the equal-width 512 and 256 layers. `model.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f13]] [method] Multi-task: win loss weight 1, four game-length-bucket win heads at 0.1, ten gold-at-15 heads sharing 0.1, game duration 0.01. `task_definitions.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f14]] [method] Richer priors and auxiliary tasks (class-based init, adjacent-patch smoothing, kills, damage) helped only with less data and were dropped at tens of millions of games. `apps/machine-learning/README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f15]] [method] [data] Augmentation is random masking of champions to an unknown token (draft order is not in the API): 20% of chunks unmasked, 7-9 masked 15% each; one mask count per chunk, not per game. `masking_strategies.py`, `match_dataset.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f16]] [method] [data] Checked, not there: no blue/red side swap or role permutation augmentation anywhere in training, dataset or model code; side is implicit in slot order. (C/fair, absence)
- [[loldraftai#^f27]] [method] Pro model: fine-tune with a new queue embedding, other embeddings frozen, mixing in solo queue data to avoid catastrophic forgetting. `apps/machine-learning/README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f28]] [method] Learning-rate schedule: one-cycle with cosine annealing, peak 8e-4 reached at 20% of steps, start at peak/10, end at peak/(10 x 3e4); continued (online) training instead uses a flat 4e-4 with no schedule. `config.py`, `train.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f29]] [method] Gradient clipping at global norm 1.0; bfloat16 autocast on CUDA; fused AdamW. `config.py`, `train.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f30]] [method] Weight decay 0.05 applies only to MLP weights: all embeddings, biases and norm layers are excluded (minGPT convention). `train_utils.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f31]] [method] [stats] Checked, not there: no early stopping; all 50 epochs run, the best-validation-loss state is saved each time it improves and a timestamped checkpoint every epoch. `train.py` (C/fair, absence)
- [[loldraftai#^f32]] [method] Embedding init is PyTorch default (no custom init in the archived model); class-based champion init was tried earlier and removed. MLP blocks are Linear (no bias), BatchNorm, GELU, Dropout, with half dropout on the last block. `model.py`, `apps/machine-learning/README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f33]] [method] Batch size 20,480 on CUDA (4,096 otherwise), last partial batch dropped. `utils/match_prediction/__init__.py`, `train.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f35]] [method] Checked, not there: no training time, GPU type or wall-clock figure in the READMEs (epoch time goes only to W&B). (C/fair, absence)
- [[loldraftai-site#^f2]] [tools] [method] [ux/features] Custom-trained neural model predicts win rate and recommends champions for the specific draft. (C/weak, verified)
- [[loldraftai-site#^f4]] [method] (claim) Draft-only accuracy 56.7%, calibrated, and +4 to 7% last-pick win-rate uplift. (C/weak, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f1]] [method] GA2M: univariate terms plus a small number of pairwise terms, each visualisable. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f2]] [method] [stats] Adding shaped pairs nearly closes the gap to random forests, and sometimes beats them. Section 5.1.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f3]] [method] Fit order: shape main effects first, then detect and fit pairs on the residual (two-stage; top-K pairs by FAST in one go on large data). Section 4.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f4]] [method] Mains first means pair terms hold only what mains could not explain, which keeps main-effect plots stable. Section 4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f5]] [method] [stats] Pair detection needs far more samples than candidate pairs; with 4950 pairs FAST needed about 10^6 samples for average precision over 0.7. Section 5.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f7]] [method] Rank terms for display by the standard deviation of each term's contribution, sqrt(E[f_u^2]). Section 4.2.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f8]] [method] (inferred) Our student is a GA2M over categorical inputs: mains are champion-role terms, pairs are ally and enemy role-pair cells. ^f5 says that at 230k noisy games most of the ~10^5-10^6 pair cells are below detection; distilling from teacher logits (lower-variance targets) and syntheti... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Use ^f3/^f4 ordering in the distilled fit too: fit champion-role mains to the teacher logit, then pair terms on the residual with heavier shrinkage, so a champion's displayed strength does not change when pairs are added. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f10]] [method] (inferred) ^f7 gives the "what matters in this draft" ranking for the app: per draft, list the terms with the largest contributions. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[lsreg-score-screening#^f1]] [stats] [method] The score test is computed from the base fit only, for screening many candidate covariates; no parameter estimate is produced. `Overview` (C/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f1]] [method] [data] Drift comes in types: sudden, incremental, gradual, and recurring concepts. Section 2.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f2]] [method] [data] Three adaptation families: simple retraining, ensemble retraining, and model adjusting. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f3]] [method] [stats] Window size is a trade-off: small reflects the latest distribution, large gives more training data. Section 5.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f4]] [method] Severity guides the strategy: low severity, update the current learner incrementally; high severity, retrain. Section 4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f5]] [method] When drift is local, partially updating the model is arguably more efficient than retraining all of it. Section 5.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f6]] [method] (inferred) A patch is a known-date, mostly local drift (a few champions and items change). That points to keeping all patches but letting changed champions' parameters move (patch-specific offsets shrunk to the pooled value, or recency weights), rather than a hard last-patch window that ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f1]] [stats] [method] Ridge APM is the MAP of beta ~ N(0, tau^2): every player shrunk towards the average player. Section 2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f2]] [method] Augmented APM: beta / alpha ~ N(alpha x rating, tau^2), alpha ~ N(mu, sigma^2): the prior mean is a fitted multiple of the centred FIFA rating, so beta is still relative to the average-rated player. Section 2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f8]] [method] (inferred) ^f3 is the double-counting mechanism in reverse: with a group prior, two collinear explanations (a kit group and its members' own slopes) are not split arbitrarily; the group gets what the members share, each member only its deviation. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f1]] [method] [stats] Measuring chemistry as lineup APM minus the sum of player APMs fails on small samples: too many lineups, too few minutes each. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f2]] [method] Their way round it: rate each player on six skills (offence and defence for scoring, rebounding, ball-handling) in nested probits, simulate possessions with the ten players, and call synergy the lineup's result minus the sum of the parts. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f3]] [method] [stats] Synergy comes from the nonlinear link: skills that raise a rare event's chance stack positively, skills for an already likely event stack negatively (diminishing returns). (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f9]] [draft] [method] (inferred) The champion version: give each champion a few attribute scores (damage type, engage, waveclear, scaling) and let synergy arise from a nonlinear team function of the summed attributes; pairs then share strength through attributes instead of needing games per pair. That... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f1]] [method] In-game win model: logistic regression per minute on minutes elapsed and each team's kills, towers and large monsters killed; deliberately few features so a phone app could compute it live. Section 3.1 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f2]] [method] [stats] Within-game correlation handled by sampling one random minute per game, giving independent rows. Section 3.1 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f3]] [method] Gold difference left out of the win model on purpose: argued to mislead late and to be hidden from players in game. Section 3.1 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f4]] [method] Event value is the sign of the win probability change across the event: a smart kill raises it, a worthless death does not. Section 3.3 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f6]] [method] Post-game win probability chart annotated automatically at local minima and maxima (e.g. a lost dragon at 35%). Section 4.1.3 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f8]] [method] (inferred) Our timelines give the same features (kills, towers, monsters by type, per minute), so this is a lower bound on our win-by-minute model; the "before vs after" delta is cheap but bundles the event with whatever else happened in the gap. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f1]] [method] The NN vs GBDT debate is overemphasised: often the gap is negligible, or light tuning of a GBDT matters more than the family. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f2]] [method] 30 iterations of random search on one good model beat picking the best family at defaults on about a third of datasets. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f3]] [method] GBDTs did relatively better on larger datasets (dataset size most negatively correlated with NN relative performance), contrary to Grinsztajn's appendix trend; TabNet is an exception that does well on large data. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f4]] [method] GBDTs favour irregular data: heavy-tailed, skewed or high-variance feature distributions. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[mcelfresh-nn-vs-gbdt-2023#^f5]] [method] (inferred) Our inputs are clean one-hot categories with no skew, the irregularity where GBDTs shine; trees split one champion at a time, so pair effects need depth. Neither family is favoured a priori; a tuned baseline matters more. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[meinmmo-loldraftai#^f1]] [method] [stats] Launch claim (2 Apr 2025 Reddit post): the AI predicts 62% of ranked matches. (D/weak, verified)
- [[meinmmo-loldraftai#^f4]] [method] A pro-play version reportedly failed for lack of data compared with solo queue. (D/weak, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f5]] [stats] [method] Teacher accuracy is not enough: what matters is how close its probabilities are to p* in mean square; overconfident accurate teachers give worse students. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[moba-champ-matchmaking#^f1]] [method] Split chronologically to avoid temporal leakage: earliest matches train, latest test. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-champ-matchmaking#^f2]] [method] Ranked ("League") accuracy, time split: LR 50.40%, MLP 51.53%, LSTM 52.31%, Transformer 54.29%, OwO (Cupid's model) 55.65%, their DAWN 56.85%. Casual mode reaches 66.1%, elite 67.7%. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-champ-matchmaking#^f3]] [method] (inferred) OwO scored 64.3% on ranked with Cupid's random split and 55.65% on ranked here with a time split (datasets similar, maybe not identical): most of that gap is likely the split. Ranked is the hardest mode because matchmaking is tightest. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f4]] [method] Under one-step MMR matchmaking, an MMR-based (TrueSkill) predictor gets 52.83%; their player-history model 63.19%. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f5]] [method] Ranked accuracy by feature group: team (MMR) features 56.3%, short-term 58.9%, long-term 58.2%, all 64.3%. Recent games beat long-term stats. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f6]] [method] After re-matchmaking with the predictor, MMR prediction falls below 50% and their own to 52.55%: matchmaking that uses a predictor removes what it predicts. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f7]] [method] Players' position preferences and proficiency matter beyond MMR: equal team MMR with misassigned positions still gives lopsided games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f8]] [method] (inferred) For LoL: role/autofill is the analogue of position satisfaction; match-v5 gives teamPosition but not queue preferences, so autofill must be inferred from a player's role history. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[mobalytics-app-download#^f2]] [tools] [method] Offers counter picks and matchup advice framed as an AI coach. (B/weak, verified)
- [[mobalytics-redbull-comps#^f5]] [method] Checked, not there: win rate numbers in text, sample sizes, how a team is assigned an archetype, any held-out prediction. (D/fair, absence)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f1]] [lab] [method] Learning curves serve three decisions: data acquisition, early stopping, and early discarding in model selection. Abstract, section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f1]] [method] Marginal permutation importance extrapolates to low-density regions when features are dependent; conditional sampling avoids it but changes what is measured. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f2]] [method] Conditional PFI is the unique contribution of a feature given the others ("partial importance"); two strongly dependent features can both look unimportant, which means "less relevant given the other", not "irrelevant". Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f3]] [method] cs-PFI: fit a tree predicting x_j from x_-j on training data, then permute x_j only within each leaf on test data; the leaves are readable subgroups and give local importances. Section 4.2, Algorithm 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f4]] [method] [stats] Ground truth MSE (true model, Table 2): marginal PFI is far off in the linear scenario (n=3000, p=10: 37.40 vs cs-PFI 0.26-0.40); in the non-linear scenario cs-PFI wins (1.00-1.18 vs knockoffs 626.80, imputation 144.77); with many linear dependencies imputation by random forest w... (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f5]] [method] [stats] Strobl's conditional importance for random forests (CVIRF) underestimated conditional PFI at the larger sample size, even with independent features. Section 6.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f6]] [method] Knockoffs assume multivariate Gaussian features, too restrictive on real data; cs-permutation had the best data fidelity. Section 8.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f7]] [method] Recommendation: look at the dependence structure first; impute (model x_j from the rest) when x_j depends on many features, cs-PFI otherwise. Section 11 (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f8]] [method] (inferred) For us: gold lead at minute t depends on many features at once (XP lead, kills, CS, towers, dragons), the multiple-dependency case, so subgroup permutation is the wrong sampler for it; residual resampling from a model of gold given the rest would be the conditional route. But ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f9]] [method] (inferred) cs-PFI's leaves are useful for discrete objective counts (dragons, barons, inhibitors) given a few context features: "barons matter given the gold lead bucket" is the kind of readable conditional statement it gives. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[nba2vec-2023#^f1]] [method] Despite the name, embeddings are learnt supervised, end to end, from possession outcomes, not word2vec-style co-occurrence. Section 2.B (C/weak, verified)
- [[nba2vec-2023#^f2]] [method] Architecture: 8-dim shared player embedding, separate means over the 5 offensive and 5 defensive players, concatenated, one 128 ReLU hidden layer, softmax over 23 play outcomes. Section 2.B (C/weak, verified)
- [[nba2vec-2023#^f4]] [method] Checked, not there: no baseline (plus-minus, logistic, ratings) and no pretrained vs scratch comparison; validation is KL divergence on lineup pairs with more than 15 plays and series simulation. (C/weak, absence)
- [[nba2vec-2023#^f5]] [method] (inferred) Shows the pattern of a richer target (23 outcome classes per possession) to train small embeddings; for us the analogue is predicting per-game stats rather than only win, but this paper gives no measure of how much it helps. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f2]] [method] Hero-only inputs, no player skill, justified by matchmaking. (B/fair, verified)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f3]] [method] Random split 80/10/10; metrics AUC and accuracy. (B/fair, verified)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f4]] [method] Table 2 AUC (accuracy). Dota2015: LR 0.633 (59.6%), LightGBM 0.645 (60.4%), FM/HOI 0.637, OptMatch 0.633, NeuralAC 0.662 (61.6%). Dota2018: LR 0.612 (57.8%), LightGBM 0.622 (59.3%), HOI 0.614, OptMatch 0.617, NeuralAC 0.641 (60.1%). LoL ARAM: LR 0.635 (59.7%), LightGBM 0.641, OptMatch 0.... (B/fair, verified)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f5]] [method] Ablation: dropping competition (counter) terms costs more than dropping cooperation; dropping attention costs little (Dota2015 0.661 vs 0.662). Table 2 variants (B/fair, verified)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Under random splits at 0.6-0.8M games, pairwise terms add about 0.01 (FM, LightGBM) to 0.03 AUC (NeuralAC) over additive LR, i.e. 1-2 accuracy points; Dota 2018 is less predictable from heroes than Dota 2015 (0.612 vs 0.633 LR). (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f7]] [method] NeuralAC config: hidden layers of width 50, ReLU, Kaiming init, dropout 0.2; embedding size k = 20 for HOI and NeuralAC (OptMatch searched 20-320). Experimental setup (B/fair, verified)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f8]] [method] Training: Adam, learning rate 0.001, weight decay 0.0001, batch size 256 for HOI, OptMatch and NeuralAC; LR, TrueSkill and LightGBM from standard packages. Checked, not there: epochs, early stopping, schedule. Experimental setup (B/fair, absence)
- [[neuralac-2021#^f9]] [method] Larger embeddings did not help: with other settings fixed, accuracy and AUC did not improve as k grew (Fig. 4, Dota2018 and LoL). Hyperparameters Effects (B/fair, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f1]] [method] Win probability is a small fully connected head on the agent's LSTM state, i.e. a sequence model over the whole game so far. Appendix D (A/fair, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f2]] [method] Labels are bootstrapped like a value function: on a game's last segment the true result, otherwise the model's own prediction at the end of the segment (a temporal-difference target). Appendix D (A/fair, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f4]] [method] [draft] The same head at game start, when the heroes are the only varying input, is the draft evaluator: the model's t=0 output is its draft win chance. Appendix on hero selection (A/fair, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f5]] [method] Shown on one game: drops on losing three heroes at about minute 5, 95% after a three-kill fight at about minute 18. Figure 9 (A/fair, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Two ideas we can use: (1) a TD-style target (fit minute t towards the model's own minute t+1 prediction, the real result at the end) cuts the variance of training on final outcomes from early frames; (2) one model with the draft as the t=0 input, so its minute-0 output equals ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f1]] [method] Hero2Vec: two hero graphs from match records, a signed synergy graph (co-wins minus co-losses) and a directed suppression graph (who beat whom), embedded with LINE-style first and second-order proximity. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f2]] [method] No player graph in practice: a heterogeneous player-hero graph is named as an option, but players are encoded as the average of their picked heroes' vectors plus mastery, for deployment efficiency. Section 4.3.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f3]] [method] Each player also gets hand-built record features (on Fever Basketball: game count, wins, losses, per-game averages, MVP rate); the net is a self-attention team encoder over players, with separate heads for synergy, suppression and feature vectors summed. Sections 4.3.1, 4.4, Table 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f5]] [method] Time split: games sorted by time, first 80% train, last 20% test; embeddings and player features from training games only. Section 5.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f6]] [method] Table 2 accuracy, LoL: LR 0.599, XGBoost 0.613, BalanceNet 0.624, Blade-Chest 0.636, HOI 0.639, OptMatch without attention 0.655, OptMatch 0.661. Dota2: LR 0.596, XGBoost 0.602, HOI 0.628, OptMatch 0.652. Fever Basketball: LR 0.613, XGBoost 0.639, OptMatch 0.660. (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f7]] [method] Pretrained graph embeddings vs a learnt embedding layer inside the same net (Fever Basketball): 0.639 vs 0.637 accuracy; the graph only saves training time (24.85 s vs 259.59 s). Appendix B, Table 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f8]] [method] Removing the relational (hero-graph) vectors and keeping player features only worsens score-difference error by 5.6% to 23.6% (Fever Basketball). Table 3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f9]] [method] Graph edge definitions matter about as much as the model: synergy weight choices move OptMatch from 0.625 to 0.639 and LR from 0.594 to 0.619; none wins everywhere. Appendix B.3.1, Tables 6-7 (B/fair, verified)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f10]] [method] Checked, not there: what player features the LoL and Dota baselines get, how test players absent from training are encoded, log loss, intervals, a LR or GBDT on the same player features plus hero ids as a fair baseline. (B/fair, absence)
- [[optmatch-2020#^f11]] [method] (inferred) For us: (b) a co-play or win graph over champions gives the same accuracy as learning champion embeddings end to end (0.639 vs 0.637), so it is not worth a run for accuracy. The big LoL lift over LR (0.599 to 0.661) most likely comes from player history features on a 187k-game... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f1]] [method] [stats] Penalty form of a non-zero target: lambda * sum (beta_j - pi_j)^2, where pi_j is the lineup's prior from its members' player ratings, instead of lambda * sum beta_j^2. Section 3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f2]] [method] Prior = league points per possession + the sum of the five players' previous-season RAPM (separate offence and defence). Section 3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f8]] [method] (inferred) The penalty form ^f1 is a one-line change to a ridge or Newton fit: penalise (slope - target) instead of slope, where target = role mean + group slopes for the cell's memberships. Re-estimating the targets between fits is the two-stage version of [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-c... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f1]] [method] [stats] Learning curves cross: logistic regression wins on small training sets, trees on large ones, often within the same domain, so model comparisons must look at the curve, not one size. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f2]] [method] [stats] Which wins at scale is predicted by signal separability: trees for well-separable data, logistic regression where classes separate poorly. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f3]] [method] [stats] Low separability (max AUC <= 0.8): when distinguishable, logistic regression won 8-1-1 on AUC; several sets were indistinguishable within error bars. Section 5.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f4]] [method] Why: a near-linear concept with noise favours logistic regression early; trees need more data to reach the needed complexity. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f5]] [method] (inferred) At max AUC around 0.55 our data is far into the low-separability regime; expect a regularised logistic model (plus factorised pair terms) to stay competitive with tree ensembles well past 10^6 games, and compare families by learning curves, not a single split. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[pletinka-dota-timeslice-2025#^f2]] [method] Design: Transformer over a 20-slice (10-minute) window, pretrained to predict the next slice (MSE), then frozen; an MLP head maps the embeddings to win probability. Section 4.1 (D/weak, verified)
- [[pletinka-dota-timeslice-2025#^f3]] [method] The head is trained on each game's last window; the reported 99% accuracy is on those end-of-game windows, so it says nothing about mid-game. Section 5.1.2 (D/weak, verified)
- [[pletinka-dota-timeslice-2025#^f5]] [method] No prediction before minute 10 (window must fill); the author proposes a draft-based starter model until then. Section 6.2 (D/weak, verified)
- [[pletinka-dota-timeslice-2025#^f6]] [method] (inferred) Training a win head on end-of-game states only teaches it the easy cases; the head must see frames from every minute, weighted so each game counts once, and be scored per minute. (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] TabICLv2 teacher distilled into XGBoost kept 96.5% of teacher AUC (0.882 macro mean) at 1.9 ms on CPU, 38x to 860x faster. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f2]] [method] Teacher labels must be out-of-fold: a teacher scoring its own training rows gives near one-hot targets, and the student then does worse than on hard labels. Section 1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f3]] [method] K in {3, 5, 10} folds made no difference to student AUC. Section 3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f4]] [method] Averaging several teachers helps MLP students but adds under 0.001 AUC for tree students. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f5]] [method] Where the teacher is worse than a tuned GBDT, distillation makes the student worse still. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f6]] [method] Student gains over CatBoost are small on average (+0.011 AUC with 21 features or fewer, +0.001 above). Section 4.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For us OOF means: each seed net in the teacher ensemble is trained on k-1 folds and labels the held-out fold, so every real game gets a teacher logit from nets that never saw its result. Our nets are not in-context learners, but they do memorise at 230k games, so the same rule... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f8]] [method] (inferred) ^f5 is the go/no-go test: distil only if the teacher ensemble beats a well-shrunk additive model fitted to labels on held-out log loss in `meiolol eval`; otherwise the student inherits the teacher's errors. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f1]] [method] Distillation does not reliably improve an individual student; ensembles of the generations improve consistently. Section 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f2]] [method] [data] Dataset B, 100K sampled rows, ROC AUC: single TabNet students went 0.610, 0.619, then fell to 0.578-0.584 by generations 2-5; the TabNet ensemble of generations rose to 0.625; XGBoost went 0.677 to 0.724 and stayed ahead. Table 2 (cells split across lines, quote is the caption) (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f3]] [method] At 10K rows the DNN beat XGBoost and gained far more from ensembling generations (TabNet ensemble 0.718 to 0.792 vs single 0.680 to 0.737; XGBoost single about 0.67-0.68). Section 3.3, Table 7 (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f4]] [method] Why: DNN generations are diverse (consecutive generation correlation under 0.36) while XGBoost generations are near copies (over 0.78). Section 3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f5]] [method] Self-distillation from the previous generation vs from the full ensemble of prior generations: no serious difference on average. Section 3.4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f6]] [method] Denoising: drop rows whose teacher prediction is far from the label (/f(x) - y/ >= 0.99) during distillation; reported to help consistently. Section 3.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For a noisy win label, dropping rows the teacher is confident are "wrong" means dropping upsets, which are real; at our 53-54% ceiling that is likely harmful, so the denoising part should not be copied. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[practical-kd-2023#^f8]] [method] (inferred) A between-generation correlation under 0.36 at 100K rows is a warning about single-net variance on noisy tabular labels: seed ensembles, not distillation, are the likely source of any gain for us. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: with patch-decay weights w_g, use n_eff = (sum w)^2 / sum w^2 in the shrinkage weight and the "n" shown to users; with players contributing m games each, n_eff ~ n / (1 + rho (m-bar - 1)). Estimate rho once per role from data (variance of player mea... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f1]] [method] Cold start defined: TrueSkill and TrueSkill2 ratings are unreliable before about 18 games in their game. Section 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f2]] [method] Idea: regress a novice's future converged rating (game K = 18) from their in-game performance in the current game, and use it instead of the raw rating until the cold-start period ends. Section 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f3]] [method] Inputs: 115 features (own kills, deaths, gold and team and opponent context) sampled every 3 minutes, last 12 snapshots, plus line-up embedding; model is a transformer with attention across time and features. Section 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f5]] [method] Table 3, share of games won by the side with the higher predicted rating (TrueSkill2 labels): raw TrueSkill2 54.0% normal, 53.6% ranked; LR on one-hot features 50.4%, 52.8%; MLP 60.8%, 60.4%; transformer 61.3%, 61.1%; MMR-Net 61.6%, 61.5%; MMR-Net on end-of-game snapshot only 59.8%, 60.2%. (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f6]] [method] The label's own ceiling is far higher: the converged TrueSkill2 rating at game 18 picks the winner 72.3% (normal) and 74.0% (ranked), vs QuickSkill 61.6% and 61.5%. Table 4, K = 18 (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f7]] [method] TrueSkill2 labels beat TrueSkill labels throughout (TrueSkill-trained QuickSkill 55.4% and 54.7%). Section 4.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: no player graph, teammates or opponents used for the novice's skill (only the line-up as context); no log loss or calibration; no statement that the game being scored is excluded from its own rating's inputs. (B/fair, absence)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f9]] [method] (inferred) The ground-truth row (72-74%) is the converged rating of game 18 scored on novice games, so it uses later games: a hindsight ceiling, not a forecast. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[quickskill-2022#^f10]] [method] (inferred) For us: Riot match-v5 gives the same kind of per-player stats (kills, deaths, gold, damage, CS) for every game we crawl, so a player with one or two earlier games can get a performance-based skill prior, not just a win count. That is the industrial cold-start fix, and cheaper ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f1]] [method] [draft] Design: for each ADC, a logistic model of its win rate on indicators for which support it was paired with; a mirror set per support. Pair coefficient = how much ADC i gains from support j. (C/weak, verified)
- [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f6]] [stats] [method] (inferred) Confounded: in ADC i's model the coefficient on support j is support j's own strength plus any interaction, since the model has no support main effect. A strong support looks "mutualistic" with every ADC. Their own footnote says the classic test is an interaction term,... (C/weak, verified)
- [[rd-agent-paper#^f9]] [lab] [method] Evaluation: fixed train, validation, test splits made at the start, and the top solutions from each branch rescored on the same validation set before choosing. `3.3 MD-Evaluation Strategy` (B/fair, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f1]] [method] Tuned defaults make an MLP competitive with GBDTs on 1k-500k-row data; a RealMLP plus GBDT combination works well without tuning. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f2]] [method] Defaults: 3 hidden layers of 256; AdamW with beta2 0.95; 256 epochs, batch 256; cross-entropy with label smoothing 0.1. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f3]] [method] Regularisation defaults: dropout 0.15 after each activation and weight decay 0.02, both on a flat-then-cosine schedule. Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f4]] [method] No classical early stopping: a 4-cycle cosine-log lr schedule over all 256 epochs, then revert to the best validation epoch (last of ties). Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f5]] [method] Categoricals over 8 levels get embeddings of size 8; but they could not show embeddings beat one-hot (few high-cardinality features in their benchmark). App. A (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f6]] [method] Pitfall: label smoothing is not a proper scoring rule and hurt AUC; select epochs on cross-entropy, not accuracy. App. A, B.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f7]] [method] Larger patience and cyclic lr schedules help if affordable; the large weight decay makes the recipe sensitive to other settings such as beta2. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f8]] [method] Learning rate base value 0.04 under neural tangent parametrisation (layer output scaled by fan-in^-1/2), so not comparable to a standard Adam lr. A.1, Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[realmlp-2024#^f9]] [method] (inferred) For a log-loss target with ~0.004 nats of signal, drop label smoothing (it biases probabilities toward 0.5, i.e. directly against our metric) and select the epoch on validation log loss. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f1]] [method] An FM models every pairwise interaction with factorized parameters, so it can estimate interactions even under heavy sparsity where per-pair weights fail. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f2]] [method] Why it works: factorizing breaks the independence of pair weights, so data on one pair informs related pairs, even pairs never seen together. Section III.A.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f3]] [method] Cost: the model equation is linear in features and latent size, O(kn), and parameters are linear in n; scales to 100M training rows (Netflix). Section I (A/fair, verified)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f4]] [method] [stats] Sparse data calls for a small latent size k: restricting expressiveness generalises better. Section III.A.2 (text split by column layout, no verbatim quote) (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f5]] [method] (inferred) For a draft, one-hot (champion, role, side) features with k small would give every champion pair (ally synergy and enemy counter) a dot-product weight from 170 x k parameters per field instead of ~29k free pair weights, which is the regime where our free pair terms don't help ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f1]] [method] [stats] Three ways to compute a proportion mediated (ab/(ab + c'), ab/c, 1 - c'/c) agree for continuous outcomes but not with logistic regression. Background (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f9]] [method] Proposed escape from logistic scale problems: linear probability models, average marginal effects or log-linear models. Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-august-ugg-sample-size#^f1]] [data] [method] Aggregate sites are good for relative balance in Plat+ but poor for exact win rates, especially Diamond+ and Master+, because samples are too small. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f1]] [method] Per-position MMRs are linked: a new position starts from the player's other positions' MMRs, so a Gold mid trying top lands in Silver games, not Bronze. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f2]] [method] The link has slack that tightens when Riot is less confident about the position played (repeated swaps or suspected trades), pulling off-role MMR toward the main. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f3]] [method] Off-role skill is below main-role skill by about one tier in their example (Gold main, Silver off-role start). (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f4]] [method] Before 2019, matchmaking ignored position skill entirely. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f5]] [method] Off-role and autofill games still move the main position (LP splashing): every game is treated as evidence about one underlying skill. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f6]] [method] (inferred) This is the TrueSkill2 base-plus-offset structure ([[trueskill2-2018#^f11]]) applied to positions: one base skill, a per-position offset shrunk toward it when evidence is thin. For us the same holds per champion: player base (rank at start, overall recent results) plus a champ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-matchmaking-2020#^f1]] [method] Riot balances the number of autofilled players and of premades per team. (A/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-matchmaking-2020#^f2]] [method] Riot says position proficiency matters a lot, and that MMR does not measure a player per position. (A/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-matchmaking-2020#^f3]] [method] Rank and hidden MMR are known to diverge. (A/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-ranked-matchmaking-2020#^f4]] [method] (inferred) For a predictor: team MMR, autofill count and premade count are roughly equalised by design, so they add little as team totals; what can add is what the matchmaker ignores: who is autofilled into which role, and champion and role proficiency ([[moba-cupid-rematchmaking#^f7]]). (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[riot-dev-state-of-ranked-2019#^f1]] [method] Positional matchmaking (position-aware MMR, not visible position ranks) stayed: queue times improved and off-position win rates rose. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-state-of-ranked-2019#^f2]] [method] Visible position ranks were withdrawn after satisfaction fell 20 to 30%. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-state-of-ranked-2019#^f3]] [method] Riot considered keeping positional matchmaking to make off-position games fairer without position ranks. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-dev-state-of-ranked-2019#^f5]] [method] (inferred) "Off-position win rates have gone up" once matchmaking used position skill means off-role players lost more than 50% before: an off-role penalty exists and is large enough for Riot to act on. If matchmaking now absorbs part of it, the residual our model can find is smaller, bu... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[riot-rift-rewind-2025#^f7]] [method] Submission requires public open-source repo (MIT or Apache 2.0), public URL, demo video and a methodology write-up of the coaching agent. (A/fair, verified)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f1]] [method] Pro win model is xgboost over game state: side, game time, gold share, team XP, players alive, towers, dragons and soul, herald in inventory, inhibitor respawn timers, Baron and Elder buff timers and players carrying them. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f2]] [method] Objectives enter as live state (buff time left, inhibitor respawn) rather than as counts, so an objective's value fades as its effect expires. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f4]] [method] Gold difference understates objectives: at a 10k lead, Baron moves gold by about 1.5k, but the buff is worth more than its gold, which win probability shows. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f5]] [method] Not yet in the model: team comp strength and scaling, individual dragon types; WPA per event named as a possible next step. (B/fair, verified)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For a gold equivalent of an objective, its direct gold bounty is a floor; the value worth reporting is win points added converted at the local win-per-gold slope, which includes the buff and map effects. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[riot-wp-dev-diary#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Our state model should carry buff and inhibitor timers (derivable from timeline event timestamps) as well as counts, or Baron's value gets smeared into the following minutes' gold and towers. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f1]] [method] Pretraining task: masked player prediction, 25% of a match's players masked and predicted, like BERT. Section 3.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f2]] [method] [data] Data: StatsBomb 2015-16 top five leagues, 1,792 matches, 2,600 players; augmentation by 10 different maskings per match (about 18,000 samples), split 80/20 by sample. Sections 2.1, 3.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f3]] [method] Configs: one transformer layer, D 64 or 128, AdamW lr 1e-4, batch 256, linear decay; fine-tune with warmup 0.1 and weight decay 0.01 to protect pretrained weights, all weights updated. Sections 3.1-3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f4]] [method] Pretrained vs from scratch on the downstream task (next match team stats, global MSE): 1 layer 64d 529.65 vs 607.29, 1 layer 128d 510.33 vs 574.33, so pretraining cut eval MSE by about 11-13%. Appendix Table 7 (table cells split across lines, quote is the caption) (C/fair, verified)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f5]] [method] (inferred) The pretraining corpus is the downstream corpus, so the gain is semi-supervised reuse of the same lineups, not transfer from more data; with a sample level split the held-out matches likely appeared in pretraining. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[risingballer-2024#^f6]] [method] Checked, not there: no win or result prediction downstream; no comparison with a simpler embedding (co-occurrence or stats-derived) or with linear models on the same target. (C/fair, absence)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f2]] [stats] [method] Two steps: estimate the distribution of the quantity over all units, then use it as the prior for each unit. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f3]] [stats] [method] With a beta prior the update is pseudo-counts: add alpha0 to successes and alpha0 + beta0 to the total; one line of code. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup win rates: shrink toward the additive expectation p0 (from both champions' overall win rates) with pseudo-count m: (wins + m * p0) / (games + m), m fitted per role by method of moments. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[rugby-bot-2019#^f1]] [method] Targets predicted together: play selection, metres and try per tackle, try per set, win probability and final scoreline. Section 3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[rugby-bot-2019#^f2]] [method] Motivation is consistency and maintenance, not accuracy: separate specialist models fail to share structure. Section 3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[rugby-bot-2019#^f3]] [method] Loss weighting problem solved by a mixture density output: one negative log likelihood over the whole state vector instead of hand-set weights for regression and classification losses. Section 3.2.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[rugby-bot-2019#^f4]] [method] [data] Data: 2015-2018 NRL, over 750 games and 250,000 tackles, random 80/20 split. Section 3.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[rugby-bot-2019#^f5]] [method] Checked, not there: no single-task win model to compare against, so no measure of whether the auxiliary heads helped win prediction. (C/fair, absence)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f2]] [method] A session is a run of games with at most a 15-minute break between them (the median break). (C/weak, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f8]] [method] Checked, not there: no held-out win prediction from session position, no control for the stopping rule (players ending sessions after losses), no MMR or rank in the data, results binned by final session length. (C/weak, absence)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f9]] [method] (inferred) Stopping-rule bias: grouping sessions by final length and comparing first to last game builds in a loss at the end if losses end sessions, which their own quit model (^f7) suggests. The order-shuffle control keeps each player's game set but breaks the link between the stopping... (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f10]] [method] (inferred) For us: a causal-ordering feature is possible (games already played in the current session, minutes since last game, last game's result), all known at champ select. Expect a small effect: TrueSkill2 found previous-game result and lapse each shift win rate by a few points ([[tr... (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f11]] [method] (inferred) No long-term improvement (^f3) supports treating rank or MMR as the main skill state and our per-champion history as offsets around it, rather than modelling a learning curve per player; the champion-level learning curve is the exception (first games on a champion). (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[sbgames-2026-minute-win-prob#^f1]] [data] [method] Match plus timeline data can be rebuilt into per-minute states using only information up to that minute, mixing composition, gold, xp, farm, vision, position, objectives, structures and items. (C/fair, verified)
- [[sbgames-2026-minute-win-prob#^f2]] [method] [learning] Win probability by minute: ROC AUC 0.821 at 15 minutes, 0.872 at 20. (C/fair, verified)
- [[sbgames-2026-minute-win-prob#^f5]] [method] Short-horizon forecasts of next events (fights, towers, objectives) are weak: weighted F1 0.536 at 1 minute, 0.678 at 5 minutes. (C/fair, verified)
- [[sbgames-2026-minute-win-prob#^f6]] [method] Gold is predictive but treated as an outcome of farming, kills and objectives, not as the explanation. (C/fair, verified)
- [[semenov-mlsa16-slides#^f1]] [method] Features: one-hot hero picks per team (113 heroes); models xgboost, factorisation machines, logistic regression. (B/weak, verified)
- [[semenov-mlsa16-slides#^f2]] [method] Draft-only outcome prediction reached AUC 0.66 to 0.70 depending on skill bracket. (B/weak, verified)
- [[semenov-mlsa16-slides#^f3]] [method] [draft] Picks explain less of the outcome at higher skill: about 6% of the Shannon information for very high skill games vs 10% at normal skill. (B/weak, verified)
- [[semenov-mlsa16-slides#^f4]] [method] In their Kaggle in-class contest (650 entrants, first 5 minutes of play), the winning solution used logistic regression over more complex models, with one-hot picks plus carefully selected hero pairs and triples. (B/weak, verified)
- [[semenov-mlsa16-slides#^f5]] [method] (inferred) If "information explained" is the relative drop in log loss versus a coin flip (1 bit), 6-10% means draft-only log loss of about 0.62-0.65 nats in Dota 2; LoL ranked at AUC 0.55 explains well under 1%. (B/weak, unquoted)
- [[set-transformer#^f1]] [method] Pooling each element independently necessarily discards information about interactions between elements. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[set-transformer#^f2]] [method] Self-attention blocks encode pairwise interactions and stacking them encodes higher-order ones. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[set-transformer#^f3]] [method] Cost: full self-attention is O(n^2) in set size; inducing points make it O(nm). For ten picks n^2 is trivial. Section 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[set-transformer#^f4]] [method] Interaction modelling lost to simple pooling when the elements already carried enough information: Set Transformers won on small sets, lost on large point clouds. Section 6.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[set-transformer#^f5]] [method] (inferred) For us: attention over the ten picks is the neural analogue of FM pair terms; f4 suggests it pays only once interaction signal exceeds what per-champion terms capture, which our pair terms say is not yet the case at 90k. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f1]] [method] Architecture: shared embedding per character, row-wise l2 normalised, one transformer encoder over one team's tokens with no positional encoding, mean pool, sigmoid linear head to a team descriptor; the two descriptors are differenced. (C/weak, verified)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f2]] [method] Sizes: 8-dim embeddings, a single encoder layer with 2 heads and FFN 64; Adam lr 1e-5, BCE, early stop after 40 epochs without improvement, best validation checkpoint. Bigger did not help. (C/weak, verified)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f3]] [method] [data] Data: NBA 10,360 games; LoL Pro 76,318 (168 champions); LoL Amateur 156,348 (136 champions, Kaggle ranked games from 2014); Valorant 12,473; split 10-fold random CV 80/10/10, not time. Table 1 and Evaluation (C/weak, verified)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f4]] [method] Draft-only accuracy, LoL Pro: MLP 52.0, Siamese MLP 52.5, joint transformer 50.4, Siamese transformer 53.7. LoL Amateur: 51.0, 53.2, 50.1, 54.2. NBA: 56.1, 62.3, 49.0, 63.3. No logistic baseline, no log loss. Table 2 (layout interleaved, no clean quote) (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f5]] [method] Siamese (shared team encoder) beat the non-Siamese version in both MLP and transformer form; the joint 10-token transformer stayed near chance. (C/weak, verified)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f6]] [method] (inferred) The head is w'sigmoid(s1 - s2) + b: swapping teams flips the difference, but with a free bias it is only antisymmetric if b = -sum(w)/2, so side symmetry is approximate, not built in. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f7]] [method] [data] Checked, not there: no comparison with logistic regression or champion win rates; the "+ aux info" rows (LoL Amateur 94.9%) use in-game stats such as gold earned and wards bought, i.e. outcome leakage, not pre-game information. (C/weak, absence)
- [[siamese-transformer-synergy#^f8]] [method] (inferred) At 76k-156k LoL games the Siamese transformer's lift over a Siamese MLP is about 1 accuracy point on random folds; with our ratings already at 53-54% on a time split, attention alone is unlikely to add much without a ratings baseline underneath. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f1]] [stats] [method] Player gold is modelled as intercept + champion random effect + player random effect + noise, fitted as a Bayesian hierarchical model. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f4]] [data] [method] Gold is split by phase (0-7, 7-15, 15-25 minutes) as differences of cumulative totals, i.e. gold earned within each window. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f1]] [method] [stats] APM is a regression of each no-substitution stint's margin on +1/-1 indicators of the ten players on court, plus a home constant; ratings are the effect of replacing an average player. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f3]] [method] [stats] Protocol: lambda, minutes cutoff and past-season weight chosen by 10-fold CV on games to February; March and April games held out as a pure test set and scored by RMSE of game margin. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f6]] [stats] [method] Ridge with lambda 3000 on three seasons: test RMSE 11.47, R-squared 0.169, about double; one season with ridge gets 0.159, so shrinkage matters more than extra data. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f9]] [method] Checked, not there: no interaction (pair or lineup) terms, no intervals on ratings, no per-player uncertainty. (A/fair, absence)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f10]] [method] [draft] (inferred) Our win model is the same design with champions in place of players and a logistic link: ridge on +1/-1 champion (by role) indicators, with pair indicators added as a second block that needs its own, larger penalty. Choose each penalty by CV and test on a later patch b... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f2]] [method] Grid of 864 configs: embedding dim 1-6, tanh or sigmoid, 0-3 hidden layers of 10, 20 or 30 units, SGD or Adam, learning rate 0.1 to 1e-5; shuffling each epoch, early stopping, and learning-rate reduction when training loss plateaus. Section 5.3-5.4, Table 5.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f3]] [method] Mean validation accuracy rose with embedding dim up to 4 then flattened; more hidden layers slowly lowered it; ReLU did badly; Adam far better than SGD. Section 5.4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f4]] [method] Best champion-only model on validation (Table 7.1): embedding dim 2, sigmoid, 0 hidden layers, Adam, learning rate 1e-5, 53.94% validation; with player features (Table 7.2): dim 1, tanh, 2 hidden layers of 20, 54.49%. (Table read from the layout text; plain text splits its cells.) (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f5]] [method] [stats] Test (last 15k games): champion-only 54.06%, with player features 53.45%, below its validation score; the author suspects overfitting from the extra features or drift over time. Section 7.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f6]] [method] Checked, not there: no logistic regression or other baseline trained on the same split, only the 50.43% blue-side prior. (C/fair, absence)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f7]] [method] (inferred) At 35k training games the search collapsed to a near-linear model (2-dim embedding, no hidden layer), and the richer player-feature net lost 1 point from validation to test; consistent with nets adding nothing over LR at this size. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[ss-dont-counterpick-unfamiliar#^f5]] [draft] [method] Counter gaps are small (2-4% win rate) and global, so personal win rates per champion and matchup matter more. (B/fair, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f1]] [method] Students often differ a lot from their teacher's predictions, even when they have the capacity to match. Abstract (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f2]] [method] Fidelity and generalisation are different things to measure. Section 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f3]] [method] When the teacher is an ensemble that is clearly better than the student, higher fidelity does mean better generalisation. Section 1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f4]] [method] The main cause of low fidelity is optimisation, not too little data. Abstract (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f5]] [method] [stats] In ensemble distillation the highest-fidelity student is always the best calibrated. Section 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f6]] [method] More distillation data helps the student identify the teacher's function but makes optimisation harder. Section 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For us, report both: fidelity (student logit vs teacher logit, RMSE or R^2 on held-out drafts) and generalisation (log loss on held-out games), since the additive student is only "explaining the teacher" to the extent fidelity is high. The non-additive remainder (teacher minus... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f8]] [method] (inferred) ^f4 is a deep-net problem: an additive student fitted by penalised least squares is convex, so its fidelity gap is pure capacity (what the teacher does that is not additive in our terms), which makes the gap meaningful. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f1]] [stats] [method] Score tests need only the null model fitted; Wald needs the alternative, LRT needs both. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f4]] [stats] [method] The code computes it as a residual correlation: project the candidate off the current design (weighted QR residual), dot it with the weighted working residuals, divide by the projected candidate's norm (and dispersion, 1 for binomial). Result is a z-statistic, standard normal und... (A/strong, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f1]] [method] Each user and item is a sparse feature vector including a one-hot for its id and its metadata; the trait vector is the sum of per-feature trait vectors. Section 1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f2]] [method] [stats] Priors: independent Gaussians (mean and sd) on every component, chosen for memory and fast inference. Section 2.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f3]] [method] Inference: EP and variational message passing; ADF gives a single online pass, more EP passes improve it. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f7]] [method] (inferred) This is the additive alternative to a group-level predictor: slope_c = sum of a role column, kit-group columns and an id column, each with its own N(0, s^2) prior. With a wide prior on the group columns it equals the Gelman- Hill model ([[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f4]]); with ... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f1]] [method] Marginal permutation tests the null that X_j is independent of both Y and the other features, so a correlated but useless predictor scores as important. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f2]] [method] Conditional permutation (permute X_j within groups of Z = z) tests conditional independence of X_j and Y given Z instead. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f3]] [method] Grid: the cut points of each tree on the conditioning variables; Z chosen as variables correlated with X_j above a threshold (r >= 0.2 in their simulation). Sections 2.5, 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f4]] [method] [stats] Simulation: marginal importance strongly overestimated correlated predictors; conditional importance reduced but did not remove the preference, and had lower variability. Sections 3, 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f5]] [method] (inferred) The null-hypothesis framing is the one-line test for us: a marginal permutation score for gold lead mixes "gold predicts winning" with "gold correlates with XP and kills"; only the first is what the dashboard wants to say. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[tabm-2024#^f1]] [method] TabM: one MLP imitating an ensemble of k MLPs that are trained together and share most weights; MLP-based models beat attention- and retrieval-based ones and TabM was best overall. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f2]] [method] The only new hyperparameter is k, fixed at 32 untuned. 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f3]] [method] TabM-mini keeps only one non-shared multiplicative adapter on the input of the first layer, and does slightly better than the naive full version. 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f4]] [method] Training optimises the mean of the k individual losses; inference uses the mean prediction. 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f5]] [method] Submodels look overfitted individually yet the mean generalises much better; even the best single submodel is no better than a plain MLP. 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f6]] [method] Too large k hurts, and too narrow (d = 64) or too shallow (1 layer) TabM is suboptimal on middle-to-large data. 5.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f7]] [method] Optimal learning rate for TabM is higher than for MLP at the same batch size. A.3 (A/strong, verified)
