- [[tabm-2024#^f8]] [method] Protocol: AdamW, no learning rate schedule, no augmentation, gradient clipping at 1.0, dataset-specific batch size, patience 16 epochs, Optuna TPE 50-100 iterations; categoricals one-hot. D.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f9]] [method] TabM tuning space: k = 32, 1-5 layers, width 64-1024, dropout {0, U[0, 0.5]}, lr LogUniform[1e-4, 5e-3], weight decay {0, LogUniform[1e-4, 1e-1]}. Table 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f10]] [method] MLP tuning space: 1-6 layers, width 64-1024, dropout {0, U[0, 0.5]}, lr LogUniform[3e-5, 1e-3], weight decay {0, LogUniform[1e-4, 1e-1]}, 100 iterations. Table 8 (A/strong, verified)
- [[tabm-2024#^f11]] [method] (inferred) For us, TabM-mini on top of a rating offset gives a 32-way ensemble in one training run, which is the cheap route to the convexity gain in log loss ([[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f5]]). (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[tabm-repo#^f1]] [method] Default TabM: 3 blocks (2 with numerical embeddings) of width 512, dropout 0.1, ReLU, k = 32. `tabm.py` TabM.make (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f2]] [method] Default optimiser: AdamW, lr 0.002, weight decay 0.0003. README (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f3]] [method] Train the k heads independently (mean of losses, not loss of the mean); at inference average probabilities, not logits. README (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f4]] [method] k: smaller values (16-24) still competitive for exploration; larger k may need a wider or deeper backbone; avoid n_blocks = 1 at paper-like data sizes. README (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f5]] [method] TabM-mini may train faster and is occasionally better when more regularisation helps. README (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f6]] [method] 30-50 TPE iterations give a reasonable configuration if peak performance is not critical. README (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabm-repo#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Averaging probabilities over k heads is what gives the convexity gain in log loss; with a rating offset each head's logit is offset + head(x). (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] Fit error(N) = a N^-b + c (c the irreducible floor) to nested subsets at seven fractions (1% to 100%) on one fixed test set. Section 3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f2]] [method] [stats] Power laws fit well (R^2 > 0.8) on 77.7% of cells; tree ensembles win at full data, linear models underperform on classification. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f3]] [method] [stats] Logistic regression has the steepest stable exponent (median b = 0.83), boosting 0.48, random forest 0.33. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f4]] [method] [stats] Exponent alone misleads: what matters is exponent and floor together. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f5]] [method] Even with the seed fixed, re-implementations of one protocol moved the fitted exponent by about 14%. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f1]] [method] Mimic model trained by mean squared error between teacher score and student, on unlabelled inputs run through the teacher. Section 2.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f2]] [method] Scores are first mapped to the logit scale and calibrated (a monotone transform, like isotonic regression) so mimic and outcome models are comparable. Section 2.3.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f3]] [method] [stats] Compare per feature: difference of the two models' shape functions with bootstrap-of-little-bags confidence intervals; train both on the same sample to remove sample noise. Section 2.3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f4]] [method] [stats] The mimic is tighter than the outcome model where outcomes are sparse and noisy (ages over 70): soft scores carry less noise than labels. (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f5]] [method] Fidelity: iGAM with pairs was about as faithful as a random forest on three of four scores (Lending Club RMSE 2.52 vs 2.48; COMPAS 2.00 vs 2.02); random forests won clearly only on Chicago Police (14.0 vs 16.5). Table 2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f6]] [method] More teacher-labelled inputs helped little when the student was limited by missing features (COMPAS: +3,000 rows took RMSE 2.0 to 1.98). Section 3.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f7]] [method] (inferred) Distill-and-compare is our validation step: fit the same additive model with shrinkage once on teacher logits and once on win labels; where the two champion or pair curves differ beyond their bootstrap CIs, the teacher has learnt something (or invented it), and those are the e... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f8]] [method] (inferred) ^f4 is the rare-pair argument in small: where a pair has few games, the label-fitted term is all noise and shrinks to zero, while the teacher's score still carries the pair's composition through features the teacher shares with other games. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f1]] [method] Distilled additive student: fit h0 + sum h_i(x_i) by mean squared error to the teacher's prediction function; pairwise terms h_ij can be added (SAT + pairs). Section 3.2.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f2]] [method] Distilled additive explanations are generally the most accurate additive ones; non-additive students that model interactions are more accurate still. Abstract (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f3]] [method] [stats] Accuracy kept on classification (AUC, teacher 2H-512 vs distilled additive SAT): Magic 94.06 vs 90.75, Pneumonia 82.18 vs 82.24, FICO 79.37 vs 79.42. Adding pairs: Magic 90.75 (no gain), Bikeshare RMSE 0.98 to 0.60. Tables 2 and 3 (cells split across lines; quote is the text) (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f4]] [method] Where data is small and wide the gap to the teacher is smallest and a sparse linear student is competitive. Section 5.4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f5]] [method] Pairs are not free: unnecessary on some datasets, harmful on others. (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f6]] [method] Fidelity check: the student explains the teacher, not the data; a student of a 2H teacher got RMSE 0.12 vs teacher 0.14, while a GAM trained on the true labels got 0.02, so the student's errors are the teacher's. Section 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f7]] [method] A distilled additive model absorbs a share of each interaction into the main effects; how it is split is not unique and no split is "right". Section 5.2 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f8]] [method] Correlated features share an effect: a bump injected into temperature also showed in feeling-temperature (r 0.988). Section 5.3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f9]] [method] Global distillation beats aggregating local SHAP into a global additive view (gSHAP Loan RMSE 5.10 vs SAT 2.35). Section 5.4 (B/strong, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f10]] [method] (inferred) For us: fit the additive student by squared error on the teacher's logit (our ^f1 analogue, see [[ba-caruana-deep-2014]]), and expect a champion-role term to absorb part of its pair effects (^f7); report pair terms as "beyond the two champions' main effects", fixed by fitting ... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f11]] [method] (inferred) Champion pairs that nearly always co-occur (bot-lane duos) will share credit like correlated features (^f8); shrinkage chooses the split, so the explanation of such pairs is a modelling choice, not a fact. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f1]] [method] [stats] Two shrinkage modes: a full hierarchical Bayesian model that partially pools players by position (variance components estimated by MCMC), or penalised likelihood to select the few effects that stand out. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f6]] [method] [stats] Pair procedure: fix the player-effect penalties from the main model, add the 1,000 pairs with most shared shifts (same position group only), lasso on the pair block alone, penalty swept. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f11]] [method] [draft] (inferred) The protocol to copy: fit main effects first with their own penalties, freeze them, then add a pair block restricted to pairs with enough games and pick its penalty on held-out log loss. For us the block is per role pair (10 ally, 25 enemy), each with its own penalty o... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f1]] [method] TTT propagates information through one causal network of all games and players in both directions; Elo and TrueSkill only forward. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f2]] [method] Inference is message passing (sum-product, expectation propagation) with repeated forward and backward sweeps until convergence; cheap even for millions of nodes. Abstract, appendix (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f3]] [method] Forward protocol: first 70% to tune hyperparameters, last 30% to evaluate, each game predicted from all games up to the day before. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f4]] [method] Table 1, geometric mean probability of the observed result on the forward test (TrueSkill vs TTT): tennis (186,361 test games) 0.5722 vs 0.5760; basketball 0.5316 vs 0.5318; chess 0.3580 vs 0.3584; football 0.3921 vs 0.3963. Elo tennis 0.5695, KickScore 0.5758. Per game, the gains over T... (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f5]] [method] The large gain is hindsight: using all other games including later ones (LOOCV) lifts tennis to 0.5908, which the authors exclude from model comparison. Section 3, Table 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f6]] [method] Simulation: a new player joining a known community is tracked well, but by smoothing their whole learning curve after the fact. Section 2.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f7]] [method] Checked, not there: no team-game benchmark with ad hoc teammates (the examples are 1v1 and fixed teams), no cold-start breakdown on the forward test, no extra features beyond results. (A/strong, absence)
- [[trueskill-through-time-2025#^f8]] [method] (inferred) For us: message passing over the player graph is what a jointly fitted logistic player-term model already approximates; on a forward split TTT beat TrueSkill by under 0.4 points of probability per game. Smoothing that uses a player's later games is a leak for held-out predicti... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f1]] [method] Classic TrueSkill: per-player Gaussian skill, performance = skill + noise, team performance = time-weighted sum; skill drifts per match and per unit of idle time; modes independent. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f2]] [method] TrueSkill2's changes: kills and deaths inform skill, quits count as losses, skill in one mode borrows from others, drift biased upward early, squads perform better than their sum. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f4]] [method] Evaluation protocol: parameters tuned on the training period; test games processed once in start-time order, predict then update online with the result. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f5]] [method] Model building is metric-driven: a term is added only when a subgroup's actual win rate departs from the model's expected win rate, and kept if it closes the gap. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f12]] [method] Once a player has many games in a mode, other modes barely move that mode's rating: the offset absorbs it (hierarchical shrinkage toward base skill). (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f13]] [method] Suggested, not measured: the same base-plus-offset scheme per character in LoL or Dota 2; beating a first-time pick should count less than beating a main. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f14]] [method] Deliberately excluded: features correlated with skill but gameable (time of day, who you queue with, opponent's skill via matchmaking), for incentive reasons. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f16]] [method] Batch inference (TrueSkill Through Time, smoothing over all games) gives different and better orderings than online filtering; Gears 4 recomputes all skills and parameters in batch before each season. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f17]] [method] (inferred) For us: the paper's checks are cheap to run on our data before any model change: per bucket of (games on champion, days since last game, previous game result, rank) compare actual vs predicted win rate on test games. A bucket off by 2+ points is a missing term. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f18]] [method] (inferred) Per-champion skill for us = player base + champion offset with a mastery-driven experience drift: the offset is shrunk to zero when games on the champion are few, which is our 74% (no earlier game on the champion in our data). Mastery points before the game stand in for the mi... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f19]] [method] (inferred) Its strongest terms do not transfer: LoL ranked solo/duo has at most duos (squad effect small), and in-game stats (kills) are post-game for the player but legitimately pre-game for the next game, like our win-rate history. Expect gains of a point or two, not 16. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[valorant-smurf-detection#^f2]] [method] Randomised test: half the detected smurfs had MMR adjusted, half left as a control. (B/fair, verified)
- [[valorant-smurf-detection#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: a low-games account at a low rank is ambiguous (new player or smurf). Total account level, champion mastery points far above the account's games in our data, and a high early win rate are pre-game signals of an experienced player on a new account; mastery before the ga... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[valve-dota2-ranked-roles-2020#^f1]] [method] Dota 2 moved from separate Core and Support MMRs to one rank with a handicap per role (five roles) for relative strength in each position. (B/fair, verified)
- [[valve-dota2-ranked-roles-2020#^f2]] [method] Queueing a weaker role places the player at a lower effective MMR; every game, in any role, updates the single MMR. (B/fair, verified)
- [[valve-dota2-ranked-roles-2020#^f3]] [method] Role handicaps were seeded from the old Core/Support MMRs plus analysis of the last 100 games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[valve-dota2-ranked-roles-2020#^f4]] [method] (inferred) Valve and Riot ([[riot-dev-ranked-2019-positions#^f1]]) both ended at one skill plus a per-role adjustment, the TrueSkill2 base-plus-offset form ([[trueskill2-2018#^f11]]). Valve tried separate ratings and moved away; Riot linked its per-position MMRs from the start. For us: o... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f1]] [method] [stats] Difference method: total-effect coefficient minus the exposure coefficient once the mediator is added; product method: exposure-to-mediator coefficient times mediator-to-outcome coefficient. Traditional approaches (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f2]] [method] [stats] With linear models fitted by OLS the two always agree; with logistic regression they do not. Traditional approaches (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f3]] [method] [stats] Four assumptions for a causal reading: no unmeasured exposure-outcome (A1), mediator-outcome (A2) or exposure-mediator (A3) confounding, and no mediator-outcome confounder that the exposure itself affects (A4). Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f4]] [method] [stats] Mediator-outcome confounding must be controlled even when the exposure is randomised, because the mediator is not. Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f6]] [method] A4 gets less plausible the longer the gap between exposure and mediator. Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f7]] [method] [stats] With exposure-mediator interaction (theta3), direct and indirect effects are closed-form combinations of both models' coefficients; without it they reduce to theta1 and beta1 theta2. Allowing for exposure-mediator interaction (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f11]] [method] Fix for a common outcome: a log-linear (log-binomial) outcome model, on the risk ratio scale, where product and difference agree. Binary outcomes (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f12]] [method] [stats] Sensitivity analysis for unmeasured mediator-outcome confounding (Ding and VanderWeele): two parameters, gamma (confounder-outcome risk ratio) and lambda (confounder-exposure ratio within mediator levels), give a bias factor that divides the direct effect and multiplies the indir... (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f13]] [method] Report a table of corrected estimates over a grid of the two parameters, and at least how much confounding would bring the estimate, and its interval, to the null. Sensitivity analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f15]] [method] [stats] Summing proportions mediated one mediator at a time fails if mediators affect one another or interact; a joint analysis of the whole mediator set works even when their order is unknown. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f16]] [method] [stats] The effect through one mediator is generally not identified when an earlier mediator affects it, short of strong linear, no-interaction assumptions as in SEM. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f17]] [method] Definitions (Robins and Greenland, Pearl): controlled direct effect fixes the mediator at m; natural direct effect sets it to what it would have been without exposure; natural indirect effect changes only the mediator's counterfactual value. Precise counterfactual interpretation (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f18]] [method] Controlled direct effects need only A1 and A2 and survive exposure-induced confounders (with MSMs or g-methods), but give no indirect effect. Precise counterfactual interpretation (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f19]] [method] Imai et al.'s simulation approach allows flexible mediator and outcome models under the same A1-A4. More flexible models (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f20]] [method] A four-way decomposition separates mediation from interaction: mediation only, interaction only, both, neither. Conclusion (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f21]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: win is a common outcome (50%), so the logistic product-of-coefficients and difference shortcuts (the "draft logit drops when gold@14 is added" reading) are both invalid; estimate natural effects by simulation or g-computation on the probability (risk difference... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f22]] [method] (inferred) For us: player skill drives both gold@14 (mediator) and win (outcome) and also champion choice; matchmaking balances team MMR but not champion-specific skill. A2 is the weak point: adjust for player-history features where we have them, and report the Ding and VanderWeele table... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f23]] [method] (inferred) For us: 14 minutes is long; events before 14 (first blood, early drakes, lane state at 5 or 10) are themselves exposure-induced mediator-outcome confounders (A4 violated) if gold@14 alone is the mediator. Treat the whole 14:00 state as one joint mediator rather than gold alone. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f1]] [method] With time-varying confounders affected by earlier exposure and mediator, natural direct and indirect effects are not identified; a randomised interventional analogue is, via the "mediational g-formula". Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f2]] [method] Exposure-induced confounding is the norm in longitudinal data, and the non-identification holds even if the confounder is measured. Introduction (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f3]] [method] The interventional indirect effect is non-zero only if the exposure shifts the mediator's distribution and that shift changes the outcome; when natural effects are identified, the two coincide. Interventional analogues (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f4]] [method] The mediator at each time may be a vector of mediators. Time-varying setting (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f5]] [method] For a continuous exposure, compare two levels a standard deviation apart around the mean (any two trajectories are allowed). Time-varying setting (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: treat the 14:00 state (a vector: gold, CS, towers, drakes, heralds, kills, levels) as one mediator and earlier minutes as L; the interventional indirect effect then answers "how much of the draft's win effect is carried by the 14:00 state distribution it produces", and... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-tchetgen-time-varying-2017#^f7]] [method] (inferred) For us: draft contrast = draft model logit at mean plus or minus half a standard deviation (or between our draft-score quintiles), per their continuous-exposure suggestion; report effect per one sd of draft logit. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f1]] [method] [stats] On the odds ratio scale the total effect is the product of the natural direct and indirect effect odds ratios, so log ORs add; NIE log OR over TE log OR is a proportion mediated on the log odds scale. Direct and indirect effects odds ratios (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f3]] [method] The no-exposure-induced-confounder assumption may hold if the mediator comes shortly after the exposure. Direct and indirect effects odds ratios (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f6]] [method] Test the A x M product term first; if non-zero, do not use the include-the-mediator approach. Regression analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f9]] [method] [stats] The two most common pitfalls: ignoring mediator-outcome confounding and ignoring exposure-mediator interaction; both can severely bias results. Discussion (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f11]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: the draft-gold interaction is likely real (a lead matters less to a scaling comp, more to a snowball comp), so fit draft x gold@14 (and draft x full state) terms in the outcome model and report the four-way or interaction- aware decomposition, not a single "% m... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f1]] [method] Natural effects fail when a mediator-outcome confounder is itself affected by the exposure, which blocks repeated or correlated mediators. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f2]] [method] Natural effects rest on cross-world counterfactuals that no experiment can reveal. Introduction (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f3]] [method] Interventional direct effect: set each subject's mediator to a random draw from the mediator distribution under the reference exposure, given covariates (a stochastic intervention), rather than to their own counterfactual value. Definitions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f4]] [method] Identification allows mediator-outcome confounders L affected by the exposure, as long as the mediator effect is unconfounded given A, C and L. Identification (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f5]] [method] The VanderWeele, Vansteelandt and Robins 2014 versions need not sum to the total effect when L is present; this paper's versions do. Identification, Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f6]] [method] With one mediator and no exposure-induced confounder, natural effect estimates can be read as interventional effects even if the cross-world assumption fails. Identification (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f7]] [method] Interventional effects are policy-relevant: the effect of shifting the mediator's distribution to what it would be under the other exposure. Interpretation (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f8]] [method] With correlated mediators sharing an unmeasured cause, the path through one of them is not identified as a natural effect, and adjusting for the other does not help. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f9]] [method] Proposed decomposition: direct, via M1, via M2, and via the exposure's effect on the mediators' dependence, valid whatever the causal order of M1 and M2. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f10]] [method] Estimator: Monte Carlo draws of each mediator from its fitted model under a or a*, predict the outcome, average; bootstrap for inference. Estimation (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f12]] [method] Most mediation analyses involve several mediators, because confounders are often mediators too. Discussion (A/strong, verified)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f13]] [method] (inferred) For us: the 14:00 state is a set of correlated mediators (gold, CS, towers, drakes, heralds, kills) with no known order; use interventional effects with joint mediator draws: "how much of the draft's win effect is carried by the distribution of 14:00 states the draft produces"... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f14]] [method] (inferred) For us: a per-game draw from P(state at 14 / draft d', covariates) needs a generative model of the 14:00 state; a cheap version is matching or reweighting real games (draw a 14:00 state from games with similar covariates and draft score d') instead of a parametric state model. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f15]] [method] (inferred) For us: earlier states (minute 5, 10) are exposure-induced confounders of state at 14 and win; interventional effects allow them as L, so reporting mediation by minute (5, 10, 14, 20) is coherent, each one its own analysis, not a sum. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f1]] [method] LOCO (drop X_j, refit, compare risk) is easy to estimate and interpret but depends on X_j's correlation with the rest; under perfect dependence it is 0 ("correlation distortion"). Section 2.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f2]] [method] Shapley values do not fix correlation: with perfectly dependent features and only X1 mattering, LOCO is 0 and Shapley beta^2/d; with X1 irrelevant but correlated to relevant ones, Shapley can be large. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f3]] [method] Removing correlation is a Faustian bargain: any correlation-free measure must evaluate the regression where p(w)p(z) is large but p(w, z) small, i.e. extrapolate; LOCO integrates over the joint and avoids it. Section 6.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f4]] [method] Population Shapley needs O(n) submodel refits for accurate inference (Williamson and Feng 2020); the cost should not be underestimated. Section 2.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f6]] [method] Groups are another way to deal with correlation: compute LOCO for X_j together with its correlated features, or for nested groups grown stepwise by correlation. Section 8 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f7]] [method] Normalised LOCO divides by the residual variance of X_j given the rest, E[(X_j - E[X_j / X_-j])^2]; correlation-free in linear models and often less distorted otherwise. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f8]] [method] [stats] Random forests bias estimated LOCO towards 0 as correlation rises (true value 5 on the square root scale). Section 7, Figure 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f9]] [method] Recommendation: normalised LOCO; Shapley is expensive, hard to interpret and still distorted, not recommended for general use. Section 10 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f11]] [method] (inferred) For us: report group LOCO (economy, objectives, towers, draft prior...) and also the "everything but group G" fit, so a reader sees both the unique share and the stand-alone share; between them lies the shared part no method can assign without a convention. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f2]] [method] Features (457): hero one-hot per side, role counts per side over 9 roles (carry, nuker, initiator, disabler, durable, escape, support, pusher, jungler), each hero's base strength, agility, intelligence and their gains, health, regen, move speed (patch-correct from changelogs), plus up-to... (B/weak, verified)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f3]] [method] Attributes enter per hero slot (five values per side), not as team balance or interaction terms; XGBoost is left to find any interactions. (B/weak, verified)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f4]] [method] 10-fold CV (random, across 2012-2020), AUC: regular games XGBoost 0.65, LR 0.61, RF 0.60; time blowouts 0.86 (XGB), score blowouts 0.78 (XGB). (B/weak, verified)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f5]] [method] [draft] SHAP: player win rate is top for every group, then hero win rate; roles and attributes (pusher count, Dire intelligence gain) only rank high in score blowouts, much less in regular games. (B/weak, verified)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f6]] [method] Secondary report: Semenov et al. 2016 used picked heroes plus the number of hero roles per team as draft features. (B/weak, verified)
- [[viggiato-trouncing-2020#^f7]] [method] Checked, not there: no ablation (ids alone vs ids plus roles and attributes), no log loss or accuracy, no time-ordered split, no within-team balance or missing-role feature. (B/weak, absence)
- [[wang-2016-moba-draft#^f1]] [method] [draft] Secondary report: Agarwala and Pearce 2014 clustered hero roles by PCA of performance stats (kills, deaths, GPM) to model hero interactions; with interactions 57% accuracy, without 62%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[wang-2016-moba-draft#^f2]] [method] Secondary report: Yang et al. 2014 added 50 hand-picked two-hero combinations, not better than Conley and Perry's plain LR; Kalyanaraman 2015 used hero roles implicitly (GA plus LR ensemble, 74.1%); Kinkade and Lim 72.9% by adding hero win rates. (C/fair, verified)
- [[wang-2016-moba-draft#^f3]] [method] Own result: one-hot hero LR on 912,501 very high skill games, 5-fold CV, 60.38%; adding binned game length 61.04%; NN no better. Plateau near 200,000 games. (C/fair, verified)
- [[wang-2016-moba-draft#^f4]] [method] Checked, not there: no hero role, attribute or composition feature in its own models. (C/fair, absence)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f1]] [method] [stats] Importance of a group X_s = oracle predictiveness with all features minus oracle predictiveness without X_s (drop-and-refit), defined for the data, not for one fitted model. Section 2.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f2]] [method] Predictiveness can be deviance for a binary outcome: the information gain over the base rate in a Kullback-Leibler sense, so importance is the drop in log-loss skill. Example 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f3]] [method] Groups are as easy as single features. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f7]] [method] Correlated features: the authors recommend grouping features expected to be highly correlated (from domain knowledge) or a causal model; alternatives are marginal importance (each feature alone vs nothing) or Shapley population VIM, at much higher cost. Section 7 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f12]] [method] (inferred) Drop-and-refit for 5-7 groups (economy, objectives, towers, vision, draft prior, levels) at 5 folds is ~35 GBDT fits per minute bucket; cheap on the gpu-box with LightGBM at 230k rows. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f1]] [method] An action's value is the change in estimated win probability between the state before and after it; a player's WPA is the sum over all their actions, per round. Section IV-B, eq. 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f2]] [method] The victim side is credited with the negative of the value, so values are zero-sum between the two teams. Section IV-B (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] State model chosen for calibration, because WPA depends on calibrated probabilities; XGBoost beat logistic regression and CatBoost (Table II: log loss 0.5353, AUC 0.79). Section IV-C, V (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f6]] [method] The value function takes any two states, so it can value a sequence of actions (a whole play) as well as a single event. Section VI-C (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f7]] [method] Credit for team events (a bomb plant against three defenders) has no clear split between players; left as future work. Section VI-D (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f8]] [method] The win model ignores team strength: WPA assumes both teams are of similar skill, and they suggest pre-match betting lines or rankings as a fix. Section VI-D (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f10]] [method] (inferred) WPA conditions on the state, so a tower taken while far ahead is valued against a win chance already near 1 and gets little; that removes "already ahead" only as far as the state model captures the lead (gold, towers, kills). Any lead the model misses (better players, better c... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f11]] [method] (inferred) For us: per event type, mean of win(after) minus win(before) over all occurrences, bootstrap over games for intervals, our win-by-minute model as the state model. Adding draft win chance and average rank to the state is the analogue of their betting-line fix. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f1]] [method] Game state as a fully connected graph: one node per player with coordinates, health, armour, equipment and side; GAT learns edge weights, the GCN uses inverse distance. Section III (C/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f2]] [method] Split: 70/10/20 over game states, not by game or date. Section III-D3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f3]] [method] Table I, CS:GO log loss (AUC): state vector 0.4351 (0.8715), GCN 0.6111, GAT 0.4465 (0.8684), GCN + state 0.4357, GAT + state 0.4276 (0.8753). The graph alone loses to the flat vector; graph plus vector gains 0.0075 log loss (1.7%). (C/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f4]] [method] Headline gains are against prior work: 9% on NFL (vs the state model, 30 repeats, significant), 20% on CS:GO vs their earlier published model. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f5]] [method] Checked, not there: no game-level or time split for CS:GO, a single split there, no player identity or skill in the nodes, no draft or loadout-only (pre-round) model. (C/fair, absence)
- [[xenopoulos-gnn-sports-2022#^f6]] [method] (inferred) For us: matches [[esta-csgo-2022#^f4]] in spirit; over 10 player slots a structured set or graph model adds little or nothing over a good flat model unless the nodes carry rich per-player continuous state. Our draft nodes would carry champion ids and a few history numbers, the... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f2]] [method] State features are only team-mean gold, XP and deaths, as Radiant minus Dire per minute; a 5-minute window of them feeds the model at minute t. Sections 4.2, 5.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f3]] [method] [draft] Pre-game side: hero one-hot (226), hero attributes, hero-vs-hero win rates, player MMR and player-on-hero history; player-on-hero history alone gives 69.90%, heroes alone about 60%, all 71.49%. Table 2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f4]] [method] Two ways to add the pre-game model: concatenate prior features with the window into one LR, or stack: a new LR over the prior LR's output and the state model's output. Section 5.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f5]] [method] [draft] The prior's added value fades by minute: the gap between state-only and state-plus-prior models shrinks over the game; state-only models pass prior-only after about 15 minutes. Section 6.2, Figure 6 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f6]] [method] Headline 93.73% at minute 40 vs 69% for prior only at minute 40; the minute-40 set is only games still running then. Section 6.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f7]] [method] A sequence model of the trend (their ASM, a discrete transition model per outcome) beat LR on the current window before 20 minutes, when teams are close. Section 6.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f8]] [method] One LR per minute window beat one LR pooled over all minutes: the same gold lead means different things at minute 10 and 40. Section 7.2, Figure 8 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f12]] [method] (inferred) For us: time must interact with every state feature (minute as a GBDT feature does this; for a linear model, per-minute-band fits), and stacking a frozen draft model's logit as one feature is a cheap, tested way to add the draft. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f1]] [method] Play value = post-play minus pre-play expected points (EPA) or win probability (WPA). Section 1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f2]] [method] Expected points come from a multinomial model of the next scoring event in the same half (seven outcomes, touchdown to opponent touchdown), not the final result. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f3]] [method] [stats] "Already ahead" problem in a points model: teams far ahead trade points for time, so they keep all plays but down-weight large score differentials, scaled linearly to zero at the maximum. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f4]] [method] Win probability is a GAM (logit link) on expected score differential, time remaining and timeouts, using the expected points as input, so smooth non-linear effects stay interpretable. Section 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f6]] [method] [stats] Credit split by multilevel models: play values regressed on varying intercepts per player group plus covariates, so low-sample players shrink to the group mean; fitted with lme4. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f7]] [method] Opponent and team context enter as covariates or intercepts (opposing defence, team rushing strength), which is how they control for teammate and opposition quality. Section 1.3, 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f9]] [method] EPA converts to wins with a regression of season wins on score differential, i.e. a points to wins exchange rate. Section 4.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f10]] [method] (inferred) LoL analogue: "expected gold" or "next objective" is the EPA layer and our win-by-minute model the WPA layer; a gold equivalent per event is the WPA of the event divided by the local slope of win chance in gold lead (their points to wins conversion, done per minute). (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[zilean-docs#^f1]] [data] [method] Timeline seen by the community as a per-minute record suited to ML, e.g. predicting results from stats before minute 16. (C/weak, verified)

### stats: statistical estimation: smoothing, shrinkage, pooling, sample sizes, uncertainty (509)

- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] Regime shift: near 10^5 rows GLMs and strongly regularised baselines are hard to beat; in the multi-million range non-linear tabular models keep improving. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f3]] [method] [stats] Gains at full data were small: best model improved on the GLM by 0.00158 nats per policy of held-out log-likelihood. Section 8 (B/fair, verified)
- [[actuarial-scaling-2026#^f6]] [method] [stats] Example fit: reducible loss falls by ~25% per doubling of rows (exponent 0.409) above a fitted floor. Section 2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f1]] [method] [stats] RLFM: biases and latent factors of users and items get a Gaussian prior whose mean is a regression on their features, not zero. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f3]] [stats] Sparse ids fall back to the feature prediction, heavy ids deviate from it as data allows; zero-mean shrinkage instead pulls light users towards 0 (biased). Section 1, Figure 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f4]] [stats] Nesting: zero variance for the id deviations gives the features-only model; zero feature coefficients give plain zero-mean factorisation. Section 2.5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f5]] [stats] Prediction for a new id is the feature prediction; for a known id, the posterior mean balancing both. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f7]] [stats] MovieLens-100K, 5-fold, no new users or items in test: RLFM 0.8956 RMSE vs zero-mean 0.9064 vs features only 1.0968; the whole gain is the feature prior's better regularisation of known ids. Section 5, Table 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f8]] [stats] MovieLens-1M time split (last 25% test): RLFM 0.9742 / 0.9528 / 0.9363 vs zero-mean 0.9862 / 0.9614 / 0.9422 at 30 / 60 / 75% training; the gain shrinks as data grows. Features only ~1.09 throughout. Table 3 (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f9]] [stats] Features alone are weakly predictive, worse than item popularity; features help when combined with id terms. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f10]] [stats] Advice: evaluate on time-based splits; random splits only tell you about persistent dyads. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f11]] [stats] Checked, not there: overlapping or hand-made groups as features (features are demographics and genres, linear), intervals on the gains, logistic results beyond an ROC plot. (A/fair, absence)
- [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f13]] [stats] (inferred) ^f8 predicts the size of our gain: the feature prior helps most when per-id data is thin and fades as ids fill in. At ~14k games per champion-role most slopes are thin (about 2 pp/sd detection), so a real but small gain is the expected outcome, concentrated in thin champions; t... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[agarwal-nam-2021#^f4]] [method] [stats] MIMIC-II AUC: logistic 0.791, NAM 0.830, EBM 0.835, XGBoost 0.844, DNN 0.832; Credit AUC: NAM 0.980, EBM 0.976, XGBoost 0.981. Table 1 (cells split; quote is the caption) (B/fair, verified)
- [[ai-scientist-v2#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Selected best experiments are rerun several times at the end of each stage so reported results carry mean and standard deviation. `3.2.1` (C/fair, verified)
- [[akhmedov-dota-ml-2021#^f2]] [stats] "Accuracy" is R squared (adjusted for LR). Section VI (D/weak, verified)
- [[au-2022-grouped-importance#^f3]] [method] [stats] Two near-copy groups: permutation methods gave both the same high importance; LOGO gave both near zero, revealing that they are interchangeable. Section 3.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[au-2022-grouped-importance#^f5]] [method] [stats] When within-group correlation differs across groups, all methods misstate group importance (equal true importance; the weakly correlated group scored under half of the strongly correlated one for permutation), worse with random forests. Sections 3.2, 6 (B/strong, verified)
- [[ba-caruana-deep-2014#^f6]] [method] [stats] Why a student beats the same model on labels: the teacher removes label errors and replaces hard 0/1 with its uncertainty. Section 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Adding parameters is relaxing a prior that was concentrated at zero, so not all new terms can be allowed a big effect at once. `7.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f5]] [stats] As a model grows on the same data, priors on all parameters need to get tighter, or the information budget is spread too thin. `7.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f7]] [method] [stats] Grouping a predictive check by a covariate not in the model exposes misfit the marginal check hides: one subgroup falls systematically outside the replications. `figure 14` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f8]] [method] [stats] Cross-validation diagnostics worth running: calibration of held-out predictions, which observations or groups are hardest to predict, and influence; importance-sampling LOO avoids refitting. `6.2` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f9]] [lab] [stats] Keep the old models: the sequence is for understanding how inference for the quantity of interest changes with each expansion, not for picking or averaging. `8.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f10]] [stats] Fitting many models raises researcher degrees of freedom: overfitting if a single best is picked. `8.1` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f11]] [stats] With non-negligible uncertainty in a cross-validation comparison, do not just take the best CV model; with many similar candidates, selecting by minimum CV error overfits. `8.2, 8.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f12]] [stats] [method] Alternative to fitting each candidate: fit one expanded model with all candidates and use projection predictive selection of submodels, which avoids the overfitting of searching a big space. `8.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bayesian-workflow-2020#^f16]] [stats] [lab] Iterating on discrepancies double-dips the data; they avoid the worst of it by not searching for the best-fitting model, and by embedding candidates in a larger model instead of selecting among many. `12.3` (A/strong, verified)
- [[bcappu-high-elo-draft#^f4]] [stats] (inferred) The win rates and synergy scores are computed over the dataset that includes the test games, with no sign of out-of-fold computation; on 20k games pair scores carry each game's own result, so 62% is target leakage, not draft signal. (D/weak, unquoted)
- [[bilalic-ham-home-advantage-2021#^f3]] [stats] Data: 4,356 matches, 224 teams, 12 leagues, 1,131 behind closed doors. Results (B/fair, verified)
- [[bilalic-ham-home-advantage-2021#^f4]] [stats] Indirect effect of venue through team performance 0.26 with fans vs 0.12 without; through referees 0.03 vs -0.01; total 0.57 vs 0.36. Results (B/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f3]] [method] [stats] Per-item held-out predictive scores can be inspected like residuals: look for patterns in poor predictions to find where the model fails. `5.1` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blei-2014-box-loop#^f4]] [method] [stats] Posterior predictive checks use a discrepancy chosen for what matters to the task, so a simplified model is judged only on the aspects we care about. `5.2` (A/fair, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Repeated leaderboard evaluation lets participants overfit the holdout; Kaggle's existing defences are heuristics (bit precision, resubmission rate). Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Kaggle's public/private split protects the final ranking but not the public scores, which forums report as inflated. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Ladder: only release a new score if it beats the previous best by a margin; otherwise repeat the previous best. Section 3, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Error of naive leaderboards grows like sqrt(k) in the number of submissions; the Ladder's grows only with log(k), an exponential improvement. Section 1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f5]] [lab] [stats] Parameter-free variant: the margin is a one-sided paired t-test on the per-example loss vectors of the new and previous best submission (threshold s/sqrt(n)), since paired tests have more power when losses are correlated. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f6]] [lab] [stats] The paper says its significance test is heuristic: adaptivity breaks the t approximation and multiple comparisons are not controlled. Section 4.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Boosting attack: submitting random label vectors and majority-voting those that scored better makes a submission biased on the public labels only; on the hidden 8000 labels it stays at chance, while Kaggle's mechanism reports a strongly biased loss. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Under that attack the Ladder stays within the expected maximum deviation of k random vectors: each new best makes the next crossing less likely, so only O(log k) steps. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[blum-hardt-ladder#^f9]] [lab] [stats] An analyst who only uses the holdout to check whether the latest attempt is well above the previous best effectively runs the Ladder. Conclusion (A/strong, verified)
- [[bohb#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Where low budgets are too noisy to predict the full budget, cheap evaluations just add overhead and plain BO wins (64-dimension case). Section 5.1, appendix H (A/fair, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Recommendations: randomise as many sources of variation as possible; decide on improvement with variance, via the probability A beats B in a run; prefer resampling (out-of-bootstrap) over one fixed held-out test set for small gains. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Data sampling (bootstrap of the data) is the largest source of variance; weight initialisation is usually under half of it, on par with data order. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f3]] [stats] Test-set noise in accuracy matches a binomial with n test samples; with correlated errors the distribution is wider. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The total variance from arbitrary choices is on the order of published state-of-the-art increments. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f6]] [lab] [stats] Fixing seeds conditions on arbitrary values and correlates runs, so more splits stop reducing the estimator's variance; randomising seeds and data together decorrelates them. Section 3.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Re-seeding only the init (common practice) barely helps beyond k = 1; randomising data splits too, and all non-HPO sources, is far better and 51x cheaper than redoing HPO each time (21 vs 1070 hours at k = 100). Section 3.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Decision error rates (simulated, k = 50): a single-run comparison has about 10% false positives and 75% false negatives; a mean difference with a published-improvement threshold has under 5% false positives but about 90% false negatives; P(A>B) gives about 5% and 30%. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bouthillier-variance-2021#^f9]] [lab] [stats] Rule: A beats B if P(A>B) over paired splits is significantly above 0.5 (bootstrap CI) and meaningfully reaches gamma, with gamma = 0.75 robust across their cases. Sections 4.1, 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f3]] [method] [stats] Defensive chemistry (JDI) credits opponents' offensive impact below their expectation to the pair, weighted by a positional responsibility share. (B/fair, verified)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f4]] [stats] Shrinkage is ad hoc: under 700 minutes, the expected impact is a linear blend of a position prior and the player's own average. (B/fair, verified)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f5]] [method] [stats] Unseen pairs: CatBoost regressors on pair features (roles, physique, shared nationality and language, matches together), trained 2015-17, validated 2017/18, tested 2018-19; only pairs with 700+ minutes together. (B/fair, verified)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f6]] [stats] Held-out gain is small offensively and near nil defensively: RMSE 0.0446 vs 0.0545 mean baseline (JOI), 0.8891 vs 0.8908 (JDI). Table 3 (no single-line quote in raw) (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: JOI does not subtract what each player does with other partners, so a pair of two good players scores high without any synergy; no intervals, no check that chemistry adds to match outcome prediction. (B/fair, absence)
- [[bransen-2020-player-chemistry#^f10]] [stats] (inferred) The confound to avoid: a pair metric must be measured relative to the two members' own effects (interaction beyond additive), or it rewards strong champions; fitting pairs jointly with main effects in one model does this. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[breck-ml-test-score-2017#^f15]] [lab] [stats] Monitor 7: calibration by slice as a canary (90% of 0.9 predictions should be positive); unbiased is not good, but bias flags a problem. Section V (A/fair, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f1]] [stats] Using each unit's raw average as its prediction (the naive method) is worse than ignoring individual data and predicting the overall mean. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f2]] [stats] Shrinkage scales with sample size: units with fewer observations are pulled harder toward the group mean; the best estimator cut squared error by about 40% relative to the overall mean. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f3]] [stats] [method] Normal-normal model: true values drawn from N(mu, tau^2), Bayes estimate mu + tau^2/(tau^2 + sigma_i^2) * (X_i - mu); empirical Bayes plugs in estimates of mu and tau^2 from the data. Eq. 4.3-4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f4]] [stats] [method] Method of moments estimate of tau^2: unweighted spread of the observed means minus the average known sampling variance, floored at zero; mu is the precision-weighted mean; one iteration is usually enough. Eq. 4.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f5]] [stats] Out-of-sample ranking (relative squared error, naive = 1): nonparametric EB 0.508, James-Stein 0.525, EB method of moments 0.593, group mean 0.852, harmonic prior 0.884, EB maximum likelihood 0.902. Table 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f6]] [stats] Why ML failed: sample size correlated with the true value (better hitters bat more), so the n-weighted ML estimate of tau^2 was too small and over-shrank; the unweighted method of moments estimate did better. (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) Same risk for us: popular matchups (high n) are likely the balanced, meta ones, rare matchups (off-role picks) more extreme, so estimate tau^2 with an unweighted or method-of-moments estimator, not n-weighted. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f8]] [stats] Splitting into clearly different groups first (pitchers vs others) and shrinking within groups helps a lot; group means differ (0.255 vs 0.153). (A/strong, verified)
- [[brown-2008-batting-eb#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) For us the analogue of "group" is the additive champion baseline: shrink a matchup toward A's lead plus B's deficit, not toward the role mean. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[bucila-compression-2006#^f4]] [method] [stats] With 400k MUNGE rows and 256 hidden units, the mimic nets kept on average 97% of the gain the ensemble had over a net trained on the original 4k rows. Table 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Acceptance rule: mean change in accuracy and ROC AUC over ten random validation splits, kept when the average of both is above 0; ten iterations per run, TabPFN as the fast evaluator. `3` (B/fair, verified)
- [[caafe-2023#^f5]] [stats] The authors admit the keep rule is not a statistical test. `conclusion` (B/fair, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f1]] [stats] With large enough samples any minuscule difference becomes significant; fixing a minimally relevant effect size (MRES) before the experiment avoids reading that as practical superiority, yet it is rarely done. `1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Design the experiment to have the desired power at the MRES; smaller effects are by definition uninteresting, so missing them costs nothing. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f3]] [stats] Power rises with alpha, N and the standardised effect d = delta / sigma of the paired differences. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f4]] [stats] Fixing the MRES also adds a practical-relevance dimension to significance tests, beyond the p-value. `3.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f5]] [stats] The independent units are the per-instance paired differences, not the individual runs; treating runs as independent is pseudoreplication with inflated degrees of freedom. `4.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f6]] [stats] Required N is the smallest N whose paired t-test reaches the target power at the MRES; computed iteratively, available in R. `4.1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f7]] [stats] [lab] Their procedure checks first whether the needed N exceeds the instances available, and if so investigates power or changes parameters before running. `algorithm 2` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f8]] [stats] Sequential testing (add samples and retest until significant) without correcting alpha inflates type I errors; they flag prior work for this. `1` (B/strong, verified)
- [[campelo-2019-sample-size#^f9]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: with sigma_d the SD of per-game log-loss differences between candidate and current model (clustered by game, as the harness already does), the two-sided 80% power MDE at alpha 0.05 is about 2.8 * sigma_d / sqrt(N_eff); an idea whose plausible gain is below th... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f1]] [stats] [lab] Overfitting the model selection criterion can cost as much as the difference between learning algorithms, so it cannot be ignored when comparing. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f2]] [stats] [lab] Low variance in the selection criterion matters at least as much as unbiasedness: variance is what lets selection overfit. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f3]] [stats] [lab] Demo (1000 realisations, 64 samples, four-fold CV): the CV estimate keeps falling while true test error rises again after about 30-40 iterations of tuning, the classic overfitting curve one level up. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f4]] [stats] [lab] It is worst with small data and many tuned choices; remedies are the same as for training overfitting: regularisation, early stopping, averaging, fewer hyperparameters, or for very small data integrate them out (Bayesian). Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f5]] [stats] [lab] Reported performance is optimistically biased unless model selection is redone inside each evaluation trial (nested cross-validation). Section 1, 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cawley-talbot-2010#^f6]] [stats] [lab] Rigorous evaluation uses multiple randomised partitionings with selection repeated in each, which is easy to automate and parallelise. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-2020-true-to-model#^f5]] [method] [stats] Observational values split a coefficient's credit between correlated features, and higher correlation slows their convergence. Section 3.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f1]] [method] [stats] Augmentation is averaging over the orbits of a symmetry group that keeps the data distribution (approximately) invariant, and it reduces variance. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Under only approximate invariance, augmentation adds bias as it cuts variance, so it may not help; the trade-off depends on the distance between the data and its transformed copy. Section 1, item 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[chen-augmentation-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Augmented maximum likelihood cannot always match a model where the invariance is built in by constraint. Section 1, item 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f5]] [stats] Bootstrapped over games for 90% intervals on coefficients; kills' interval includes zero once gold diff is in (kills mostly act through gold). Quantifying Uncertainty (D/weak, verified)
- [[cmu-capstone-lol-wp-2024#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no draft, no other minutes, no log loss, AUC or calibration. (D/weak, absence)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f1]] [tools] [stats] Raw matchup rates are biased by each side's overall strength; they subtract the opponent's average rate, then the hero's average normalised rate, and show the remainder (the pair effect) re-centred on 50%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f2]] [tools] [stats] Worked example: a raw 48% matchup becomes about 51% after removing both heroes' overall strength, i.e. the sign flips. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f3]] [stats] Selection bias from context: a hero picked into strong comps looks worse in raw matchup numbers than the matchup really is. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f4]] [stats] Survivorship in weighting: countered heroes get swapped out, so playtime weighting would hide the counter; each pair counts as 1 instead. (C/fair, verified)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no minimum sample, shrinkage or Bayesian adjustment is described in this post (a search summary claimed shrinkage; the page text does not say so). (C/fair, absence)
- [[counterwatch-method#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) Two sequential mean-subtractions are an approximation of the additive two-way fit; with unbalanced counts (popular vs rare opponents) a proper additive fit (alternating means, i.e. backfitting) is better and just as cheap. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f7]] [stats] The sampler's variance falls as 1/n, giving standard errors and a stopping rule; these are Monte Carlo intervals for fixed model and data, not sampling uncertainty over games. Section 3.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[covert-2020-sage#^f8]] [method] [stats] Sum of importances vs loss of models retrained on feature subsets (correlation): SAGE best or near best, but permutation is close on tabular data (Bank 0.9937 vs 0.9918, Bike 0.9815 vs 0.9798, Credit 0.9565 vs 0.9571); ablation collapses with many features (MNIST -0.4536). Table 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[csgo-skill-rating-2024#^f2]] [stats] Per-player TrueSkill (10 ratings per match) beats all per-team systems by about 1 point, best 64.1%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[csgo-skill-rating-2024#^f3]] [stats] Among per-team systems, Glicko2 beat TrueSkill and Elo, most with few training matches. (C/fair, verified)
- [[csgo-skill-rating-2024#^f4]] [stats] Prior work cited: TrueSkill on CS:GO 62% (Makarov et al.); Dehpanah et al. found new players hard to fold in. (C/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f5]] [method] [stats] Probabilities must be well calibrated; gradient boosted trees (CatBoost) won, Brier and AUC used for evaluation. Section 4, 6.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[decroos-vaep-2019#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: no confidence intervals on action values and no causal design; values are prediction deltas. (A/fair, absence)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f3]] [stats] LoL accuracy, all players (Table I): Elo 49.2/50.2/49.5, Glicko 49.3/50.1/ 49.8, TrueSkill 49.8/50.4/49.3 (SUM/MAX/MIN); previous-rank baseline 47.6. Players with 100+ games, scored on their first 100: 49.2-50.5 for every system. (C/fair, verified)
- [[dehpanah-team-skill-aggregation#^f4]] [stats] CS:GO (26k games, 4.9k players, denser): 57-65% accuracy, SUM as good as or better than MAX; they note far fewer new players there. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f1]] [method] [stats] Team success as a function on lineups splits orthogonally into first (individual), second (pair) up to fifth-order parts; ANOVA is a special case. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f2]] [stats] Orthogonality means a pair's part excludes what its members do alone; a ridge regression with interaction terms does not separate the orders this cleanly. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f3]] [method] [stats] Raw shares are misleading because higher-order spaces have more dimensions; they compare against the shares after randomly permuting lineup values. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f4]] [stats] [draft] Pair-space share of squared mass across seven teams: 0.027 to 0.092, against a permuted null of 0.030; individual space 0.004 to 0.031 vs 0.005. Pairs and individuals sit near or modestly above noise in one season of lineup data. Table 13 (column layout in raw, no clean quote) (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f5]] [stats] Better teams carry more mass in first to third order: correlation with win percentage 0.51, 0.58, 0.55. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f6]] [stats] Uncertainty by bootstrap over plays (500 resampled seasons): a triple's plus-minus SD was 82.8 around a mean of 145.9, so group values are noisy even at 2,500 possessions. (C/fair, verified)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f7]] [stats] Checked, not there: no shrinkage of spectral parts, no held-out test, no opponent adjustment (contributions are aggregated over all opponents). (C/fair, absence)
- [[devlin-2020-nba-lineup-spectral#^f8]] [stats] [method] (inferred) The permutation null is cheap and transfers directly: fit our pair block (ally role pairs, enemy role pairs) on real data and on data with outcomes shuffled within patch, and compare the pair-block share of explained log loss (or the fitted pair variance) with the shuf... (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[draftcompromise#^f2]] [tools] [stats] Tools were close together, and within margins of error none recommended compositions that statistically win more than 50%. (C/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f7]] [stats] [method] Matchup expectation is additive on an Elo-like logit scale: expected rating = rating(A's role win rate) - rating(B's role win rate), with rating(w) = -400 log10(1/w - 1). `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts`, `packages/core/src/rating/ratings.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f8]] [stats] [method] [ux/uncertainty] Thin matchups are shrunk with pseudo-games: priorGames games at the additive expected win rate are added to the observed wins and games, then the matchup's rating is reported as the deviation from that expectation. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f9]] [stats] Both directions of a matchup are averaged (A's wins vs B, B's losses vs A) before shrinking. `packages/core/src/draft/analysis.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f10]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] Prior strength is a user setting ("risk level"): 3000, 2000, 1000 (medium), 500 or 250 pseudo-games; fixed constants, not estimated from data. `packages/core/src/risk/risk-level.ts` (B/fair, verified)
- [[draftgap#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) A 1000-game prior means a matchup needs about 1000 games before its own data counts as much as the additive baseline: far stronger than Lolalytics' 100 game display floor ([[lolalytics-counters-threshold#^f1]]), and close to what Tango's k = luck variance / talent variance give... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f1]] [lab] [stats] Reusing a holdout adaptively overfits the holdout itself; reporting the best of many tested predictors is the well-known cause. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Static multiple-testing corrections only work when the set of tested hypotheses is fixed in advance; adaptive choice (hyperparameter tuning, analyst in the loop, stopping conditions) breaks them. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Synthetic demo: with labels independent of data (true accuracy 50%), standard holdout reuse reported over 63% on both training and holdout for k = 500; a fresh set showed the truth. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f4]] [lab] [stats] Thresholdout: if training and holdout scores agree within a noisy threshold, return the training score; otherwise return the holdout score plus noise and spend one unit of an overfitting budget B; stop answering when B runs out. Section 4.1, Figure 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f5]] [lab] [stats] The number of queries Thresholdout can answer is exponential in holdout size n, as long as overfitting answers stay at most quadratic in n. Section 1.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f6]] [lab] [stats] SparseValidate: the analyst only gets a bit back per query, with budgets for total queries m and for failed validations B; information leaks only on failures. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Cross-validation and bootstrap do not fix adaptive reuse; before this the only known safe approach was a fresh holdout for each dependent validation. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f8]] [lab] [stats] With a real signal (20 informative attributes) Thresholdout still finds a good classifier while preventing overfitting: the guard does not block true discoveries. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[dwork-holdout-reuse#^f9]] [lab] [stats] The experiment used T = 0.04, below the values the proof needs, and Gaussian instead of Laplace noise: practical settings are empirical. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f1]] [stats] [method] P-splines: many equally spaced basis functions plus a difference penalty on neighbouring coefficients; smoothness is set by the penalty weight alone. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f2]] [stats] Large lambda tends to a straight line (second-order penalty) or a quadratic (third order); d = 2 or 3 is usual. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f3]] [stats] [method] For data already on an equally spaced discrete grid, use the identity basis: one value per point, a banded system; this is Whittaker's 1923 smoother for life tables. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f4]] [stats] The discrete smoother is fast even for 100,000 points, including leave-one-out cross-validation (Eilers 2003). (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f5]] [stats] Gaps with no data are interpolated smoothly by the penalty (zero weights); extrapolation past the data continues as a polynomial of lower degree. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f6]] [stats] [method] Lambda need not be hand-tuned: in the mixed-model view it is the ratio of residual variance to the variance of the differences, re-estimated in a few iterations. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f7]] [stats] Alternatively choose the penalty by AIC or cross-validation against the effective dimension. (A/strong, verified)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f8]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve: y_t = mean diff at minute t, weight w_t = n_t / s_t^2 (games alive at t over within-minute variance); solve (W + lambda D2'D2) z = W y, a pentadiagonal system of ~30 unknowns. Thin late minutes get small weight and follow the smooth trend instead o... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[eilers-marx-2010-splines#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) Smoothing across minutes and shrinkage across matchups are the same idea (penalised least squares with a variance-ratio penalty), which makes one coherent story for the method page. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[fakoor-augdistil-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] Binary tasks, mean test accuracy of the best single student: standard training 82.85, Hinton soft labels on real rows only (KNOW) 83.89, MUNGE 83.72, GIB-1 84.21, teacher ensemble 84.40: GIB-1 keeps about 88% of the teacher's gain, soft labels alone about 67%. Table 1 (cells spli... (B/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f1]] [method] [stats] MNIST: depth-8 soft tree 94.45% on labels, 96.76% on soft targets (mixed with true labels), teacher net 99.21%: about half the gap. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f2]] [method] [stats] Connect4 (game outcome from board): 78.63% on labels, 80.60% distilled. (C/fair, verified)
- [[frosst-soft-tree-2017#^f3]] [method] [stats] Letter: 78.0% on labels, 81.0% distilled from an ensemble at 95.9%: under a fifth of the gap. (C/fair, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f1]] [method] [stats] Window size trade-off: a short window adapts fast but hurts in stable periods; a long window is better when stable but reacts slowly. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f2]] [method] [stats] Recency is not relevance when data is noisy or concepts recur; slow change longer than the window also defeats windowing. Section 3.1.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f3]] [method] [stats] Abrupt forgetting raises reactivity but also the risk of fitting noise. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gama-drift-survey-2014#^f4]] [method] [stats] Gradual forgetting keeps all examples and weights them by age, e.g. exponential w = exp(-lambda j), lambda = 0 being equal weights. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f1]] [stats] [method] Treat every ANOVA row (main effects, interactions) as a batch of random effects with mean 0 and a standard deviation estimated from data. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f2]] [stats] Coefficients are pulled toward zero by an amount set by the batch's variance component; batches with near-zero variance are shrunk most. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f3]] [stats] Classical (point estimate of variances) and Bayesian inference agree given the variances; the difference matters only when a variance is hard to tell from 0. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f4]] [stats] Setting such a variance to zero gives falsely precise estimates; near-zero variances are expected for higher-order interactions. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-2005-anova#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For us: the matchup interaction gamma_AB (beyond A's average lead and B's average deficit) is the batch to shrink; if its estimated variance per role and minute is small, most thin matchups should collapse to the additive prediction. Floor tau^2 above zero (or report i... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f1]] [stats] [method] Multilevel model: the coefficients get a model of their own whose parameters are estimated from the data. Abstract (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f2]] [stats] [method] County intercept alpha_j ~ N(gamma0 + gamma1 u_j, sigma_alpha^2): shrunk towards a regression on the county-level predictor u_j (uranium), and sigma_alpha is the spread left beyond what u_j explains. Section 2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f3]] [stats] The two-step version (fit units unpooled, then regress their estimates on group predictors) is possible; the multilevel fit does both levels at once and counts each unit's estimation uncertainty. Section 2.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f4]] [stats] Held-out (leave one house out) RMSE: complete pooling 0.84, no pooling 0.86, multilevel with the county predictor 0.79. Section 2.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f5]] [stats] Leave one county out (a new unit, predicted from its group predictor only): 0.50 complete pooling vs 0.40 multilevel; the gain is largest for unit averages and unseen units. Section 2.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f6]] [stats] The model assumes unit errors are independent of the row-level predictor; if not, add the unit's mean of that predictor to the group-level regression. Section 2.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f7]] [stats] Group-level coefficients (contextual effects) are predictive, not causal: easy to misread with observational data. Section 2.4, 3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: varying slopes, several or overlapping group predictors, logistic outcomes, fitting at scale (85 groups, full Bayes). (A/fair, absence)
- [[gelman-2006-multilevel-can-cant#^f10]] [stats] (inferred) ^f5 says where to look for the gain: thin and new champion-roles (leave-one-champion-out), not the per-game log loss averaged over all seats, where a 0.84 to 0.79 type gain shrinks to whatever share of seats the thin cells hold. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f1]] [stats] [method] Group-level predictor: county intercepts ~ N(gamma0 + gamma1 u_j, sigma_alpha^2); with lmer the group predictor is copied to each row (u.full, the county value per house). Section 12.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f2]] [stats] The group predictor cut the unexplained between-county sd from 0.33 to 0.16 and left within-county sd at 0.76; the group-level model is then worth about 23 observations per county. Section 12.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f3]] [stats] It also narrowed the standard errors of every county's coefficient. Section 12.6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f4]] [stats] [method] Varying slopes with a group-level predictor: (alpha_j, beta_j) bivariate normal with means gamma0 + gamma1 u_j each; fitted as y ~ x + u + x:u + (1 + x / county). The cross-level interaction x:u is the group predictor's effect on the slope. Section 13.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f5]] [stats] In the example the county slope sd went from 0.34 (no group predictor) to 0.31 with uranium in the slope model; the x:u coefficient was -0.42 ± 0.23. A group predictor that barely reduces the slope spread adds little for the slopes. Section 13.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f7]] [stats] Partial pooling includes both extremes: zero group variance is complete pooling, large is no pooling; with few groups the group variance is hard to estimate and tends to be overestimated, so estimates end near no pooling. Section 12.9 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f8]] [stats] Two observations per group is enough to fit; small groups still inform the group-level coefficients and variances. Section 12.9 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f9]] [stats] Non-nested (crossed) groupings get one variance component each; the components show which grouping varies (airports 0.32, treatments 0.04 in their example). Group means fold into the intercept, so each batch is centred at 0. Section 13.5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f10]] [stats] Checked, not there in these sections: multiple membership, scale beyond lmer, logistic varying slopes at millions of rows (chapter 14 covers logistic multilevel, not read). (A/strong, absence)
