- [[gelman-hill-2007-ch12-13#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) ^f2 gives the expected size of the help: shrinkage towards the group line is worth sigma_y^2 / sigma_beta^2 "observations". Our champcomp spread is near 0 in most roles (only Q-test roles keep spread), so the champion level is already almost complete pooling; a group term then ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f1]] [stats] An interaction's standard error is about twice a main effect's at the same sample size (four cells of N/4 against two of N/2). (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f2]] [stats] If interactions are half the size of main effects, a study with 80% power for the main effect has 10% power for the interaction. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f3]] [stats] Under low power, the estimates that reach significance overstate the true effect about threefold (2.4 vs 0.7 standard errors). (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-interactions-16x#^f4]] [stats] Take-home: 16 times the sample for an interaction; interactions found by looking after the main analysis are mostly noise. (A/strong, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f1]] [stats] [method] Tango's method: talent variance = observed variance between teams minus the theoretical luck variance; luck from the binomial. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f2]] [stats] [method] The regression constant k is the number of games where luck SD equals talent SD; add k games of league-average performance to every record. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f3]] [stats] k does not depend on the sample size: the same pseudo-games are added to an 8-game or a 162-game record; k = within-unit variance / talent variance. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f4]] [stats] Weight on the observed value is t^2/v^2, the reliability (r-squared of outcome on talent). (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f5]] [stats] Gelman: this is hierarchical Bayes with a point estimate of the group-level variance, as in BDA chapter 2. (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f6]] [stats] Insurance credibility theory (Buhlmann) uses the same N/(N+K) weight, K the ratio of within- to between-individual variance. Comment (A/fair, verified)
- [[gelman-luck-skill#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For a matchup curve at minute t: k_t = sigma_t^2 / tau_t^2, where sigma_t^2 is the within-matchup game-to-game variance of gold diff at t and tau_t^2 the between-matchup variance of true interaction effects; estimate = (n * observed + k_t * baseline) / (n + k_t). This ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f1]] [stats] [method] GLS and Gibbs for crossed random effects cost more than N^(3/2); backfitting inside penalised quasi-likelihood brings it to O(N) per iteration. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f3]] [stats] [method] Variance update per batch: sigma^2 = //a//^2 / (R - nu), where nu is the trace of the batch's block of the inverse penalised Hessian over sigma^2, a degrees-of-freedom estimate; an EM step after the Laplace approximation. Section 3.1, eq. 16 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f4]] [stats] The estimate is the posterior mode under a diffuse prior on the fixed part and zero-mean Gaussian priors on the random effects, close to the MLE except near 0 or 1 probabilities. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f7]] [stats] Timings: glmer grows about N^1.68 for their design; backfitting about O(N), in both per-iteration and total time. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f8]] [stats] Stitch Fix: 5,000,000 ratings, 744,482 clients, 3,547 items, 34 fixed parameters including item material (items nested within material, as our champions within a kit group); converged in 14 iterations. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f9]] [stats] Ignoring the random effects, plain logistic regression understated the variance of item-level fixed coefficients by 2.7x to 1467x; the worst were material indicators. Section 6 (A/strong, verified)
- [[ghosh-2022-logistic-crossed-re#^f10]] [stats] Checked, not there: random slopes, three or more crossed factors, held-out prediction comparison; the paper is about estimation and variance, not predictive gain. (A/strong, absence)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f3]] [stats] [method] Priors: Laplace (L1) on player effects for selection, normal (L2) on nuisance team effects; the L1 penalty gets a hyperprior so the data pick it. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f4]] [stats] Pairs are badly imbalanced: about 27k of over a million possible pairs are ever on ice together for a goal. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f6]] [stats] [draft] Result: little evidence of pair effects; only four non-zero interactions under the lasso, and they barely move the player effects. (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f7]] [stats] Full posterior with interactions was infeasible; they sampled only a model with the four selected pairs added (a post-selection posterior). (B/strong, verified)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: no out-of-sample test of the interaction model, no variance share of pairs, no low-rank pair model. (B/strong, absence)
- [[gramacy-2013-hockey-logistic#^f9]] [stats] [draft] (inferred) Lasso on raw pair indicators at sparse data selects almost none; this is a statement about power, not that synergy is zero. Our "ally synergy not detected at 90k" matches; to get intervals rather than a selection, use L2 (Gaussian) pair priors with a variance fitted to ... (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f1]] [method] [stats] Generalisation error falls as a power law in training-set size; empirical exponents sit between -0.07 and -0.35, flatter than the -0.5 theory predicts. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f3]] [method] [stats] Three regions: best guessing at small data, then power-law scaling, then a floor at irreducible (Bayes) error. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f4]] [method] [stats] A counting model for a weighted coin flip converges with exponent -0.5, the textbook rate. Section 2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hestness-scaling-2017#^f6]] [stats] (inferred) For a win model the floor is huge: Bayes log loss is near 0.69 because outcomes are mostly noise. Fit the power law to excess loss over the floor (or to AUC), with the floor as a free parameter, else extrapolation is meaningless; per f2, a better model buys a shift, while more ... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f2]] [method] [stats] Soft targets give more information per case and less gradient variance, so the student needs less data. Section 1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f6]] [method] [stats] Ten seeds of one architecture (no data variation needed) gave frame accuracy 58.9% to 61.1%; one distilled net reached 60.8%, over 80% of the gain. Table 1, section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hinton-distilling-2015#^f8]] [method] [stats] Soft targets as regulariser: on 3% of the data, hard targets overfit (44.5% with early stopping) while soft targets from a full-data model reached 57% (full data 58.9%) without early stopping. Section 6 (A/fair, verified)
- [[hodge-2019-win-prediction#^f13]] [stats] Live test on 28 ESL games: 70-90% by minute (85% at minute 5), noisy at N=28; 17 of 28 games had two or more swings in the predicted winner. Section VI-A (B/fair, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f3]] [method] [stats] Simulation: at rho 0.9, random forests and neural nets rank x1, x2 above features with larger true coefficients; linear models keep the true order. Strong correlation keeps the bias as N grows. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f5]] [method] [stats] Under squared error all four target the same quantity, E(f(x) - f_-j(x_-j))^2 (Theorem 2); in simulation they agree on the ordering and shrink the importance of correlated features. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f7]] [method] [stats] Permute-and-relearn tests reject far above 5% for noise features correlated with real ones; substitutes from the true conditional or model-X knockoffs hold about 5%. Any test comparing models with features dropped or permuted shares the issue. Section 5.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hooker-2021-permutation-extrapolation#^f11]] [stats] (inferred) With ~230k games, the gap between drop-and-refit and conditional measures is about the unique share versus the shared share, not sampling noise: report the group (economy: gold, XP, kills, CS) and, within it, unique shares, and say which is which. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f1]] [method] [stats] Plus-minus models face both kinds of interaction we care about: teammates and opponents. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f2]] [stats] Ridge is a Gaussian prior; it stabilises ratings with few observations and under collinearity; lambda by cross-validation. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f3]] [stats] [method] Alternative to CV: Fearnhead and Taylor 2011 estimate the prior variances (separate offence and defence Gaussians) by maximum likelihood, an empirical Bayes variance component per block. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f4]] [method] [stats] NHL logistic plus-minus (Gramacy et al. 2013): L1 or L2 penalised logistic regression on which side scored each goal, MCMC for the full posterior, so pairwise "player A better than B" probabilities. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f5]] [stats] [draft] Gramacy 2013 tested player-pair synergy terms and found little evidence; the few non-zero pairs barely moved the main effects. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f6]] [stats] [method] Thomas et al. 2013 screened the 1,000 pairs with most shared minutes, lasso to select, MCMC to fit: 221 pairs non-zero. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f8]] [stats] Under L1 most players are not distinguishable from their team's average; lasso logistic leaves only half the estimates non-zero (Smith 2016). (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f9]] [stats] Gramacy 2017 penalised only the player effects (team-season effects free) and chose the penalty by corrected AIC instead of CV. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f10]] [stats] Informative priors: centring each player's prior on a video game rating instead of zero predicted results better than plain ridge (Matano 2018). (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f11]] [stats] The field has no agreed quality measure; out-of-sample prediction of future results is one used option. (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f12]] [stats] Covariates that are significant can still barely change ratings when balanced across players (home advantage in soccer). (A/fair, verified)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f14]] [draft] [stats] (inferred) In sports with far fewer distinct pairs than we have, pair synergy mostly came out null or sparse; with ~170 champions and 10 ally role pairs per game, an undetected ally synergy at 90k games is the expected result, not a bug. Screen to well-sampled pairs (as Thomas did... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hvattum-2019-plusminus-review#^f15]] [stats] (inferred) Penalise only the pair block and leave main effects (champion by role, patch) lightly penalised, as Gramacy 2017 left team effects free; otherwise shrinkage on the main effects leaks into the pairs. (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[hyperband#^f2]] [lab] [stats] Telling two candidates apart needs more resource when partial results are noisier or their final scores are closer. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[hyperband#^f7]] [lab] [stats] On 117 data sets, Bayesian methods won on validation rank by more than on test rank, a sign of overfitting the validation set; random search at twice the budget beat all. Section 4.2.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f5]] [method] [stats] Mediation and direct effects differ by treatment arm (delta(1) vs delta(0)) when there is interaction; they sum to the total only under no interaction or when averaged across arms. Defining causal mediation effects (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f6]] [stats] A near-zero total effect does not imply small mediation: direct and indirect effects can offset. Defining causal mediation effects (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f7]] [stats] With a probit or logit outcome, neither difference nor product of coefficients consistently estimates the average causal mediation effect; product bias can be substantial. Binary outcome (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f8]] [stats] Proportion mediated is the average mediation effect over the total effect; it only makes sense when both have the same sign. The traditional gamma beta2 / (gamma beta2 + beta3) is a latent (logit) scale share, not the probability scale share. Binary outcome (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f9]] [stats] In the binary JOBS II outcome the proportion mediated was 6% with a 95% interval of about -10% to 30%: proportions are imprecise. Table 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f11]] [method] [stats] Sensitivity analysis: rho, the correlation between mediator and outcome model errors, is 0 under sequential ignorability; report the rho at which the effect, and its interval, cross zero. Sensitivity analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f13]] [stats] Example of fragility: JOBS II mediation vanished at rho -0.165, its interval at -0.06; another study needed rho 0.48. No absolute threshold exists. Empirical illustration (A/strong, verified)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f17]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: the natural plug-in is our own models. Mediator model = gold@14 (or the 14:00 state) given draft and pre-game covariates; outcome model = win given draft, state and covariates (meiolol-state's model at minute 14). Simulate: for each game, draw M under draft d',... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[imai-keele-tingley-mediation-2010#^f18]] [stats] (inferred) For us: the "ability bias" example is our case exactly: player skill on the champion raises gold@14 and win. Calibrate rho or R-squared against the observed covariates (how much player history explains of gold@14 and win), rather than guessing. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[insurance-nn-benchmark-2023#^f2]] [method] [stats] CANN on a GBM base was lowest deviance for frequency on all four sets; the plain FFNN was the one model slightly worse on the low-feature set. 4.5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[itero-draft-model#^f4]] [stats] Evaluation is time-split: only games before the predicted period are used for training. (D/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f3]] [method] [stats] Two statistics per action: mean initial win chance (selection: is it taken in winning states) and mean win probability added (impact), averaged over many similar states. Section 4.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f4]] [stats] A single event's delta is not a reliable estimate (variance, other players); only averages over many similar states are. Section 4.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f7]] [stats] Model: 75.9% accuracy, ECE 0.90% on 5.65M held-out states; accuracy 55.8% at 0-4 minutes rising to 84.8% at 24-28, then falling late. Section 5.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f8]] [stats] Example of the selection split: Heartsteel has the higher raw win rate (54.15%) but is bought in better states (W 53.66%), and adds less (+0.66 points) than Titanic Hydra (+1.46 points). Table 7 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f9]] [stats] Their own stated limit: a trade-off between how specific the state set is and sample size. Section 6 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f10]] [stats] Checked, not there: no intervals or tests on delta-W, no objective (dragon, baron) values, no check that the state model removes the "already ahead" bias. (C/fair, absence)
- [[jalovaara-lol-wpa-items-2024#^f15]] [stats] Accuracy falls after the 24-28 minute band: fewer training states late, or Elder and long death timers making late games swingier. Section 5.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f1]] [method] [stats] Draft-only on patch 16.6 test (15,092 games, trained on 16.1-16.5): every model lands at AUC about 0.554 and 53.5-53.8% accuracy; log loss 0.6883-0.6894 vs constant 0.6930. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.1 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f5]] [method] [stats] Symmetry built in: score = side + h(blue, red) - h(red, blue), so swapping teams flips the probability; XGBoost gets both orientations as training rows instead. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f6]] [stats] Learning curves have not levelled off at 73k training games; improvement over constant roughly 1.3, 2.0, 2.5, 4.0 (x1000) at 9k, 18k, 37k, 73k games for strength. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.2 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f7]] [data] [stats] Recency weighting by patch age: strong down-weighting (gamma 0.5 or less per patch) hurts, 0.7 about equals no weighting. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 7.3 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f8]] [data] [stats] Leak: player season win rates fetched after the games include the predicted game; with them the first transformer reached about 63%, and a one-line higher-team-win-rate rule 62.4%. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 3 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f10]] [stats] Calibration fine but predictions narrow: about two thirds between 45% and 55%, 95% between 40% and 60%. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f11]] [stats] With 15k test games, log loss differences below about 0.0005 are indistinguishable from noise. `docs/PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` section 10 (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f19]] [method] [stats] Selected configs: FM k=4, lam 20, best at epoch 14; transformer lam 1, best at epoch 2, i.e. the transformer barely moved from the LR warm start before validation loss turned. `outputs/model_comparison/configs.json` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f20]] [stats] Protocol: tune on train with validation = latest 40% (per platform, by time) of the second-latest patch, then refit on train + validation for the chosen number of epochs and score the latest patch. `src/draft_features.py`, `scripts/compare_models.py` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jcj-lol-draft#^f21]] [method] [stats] Test log loss (patch 16.6): LR strength 0.68851, LR with pair one-hots 0.68834, FM 0.68861, transformer 0.68847, XGBoost 0.68943; accuracy 53.5%, 53.6%, 53.8%, 53.6%, 53.6%. `outputs/model_comparison/results.csv` (C/strong, verified)
- [[jihan-yin-champ-select#^f2]] [stats] (inferred) With two rows per match and a random row split, a match's mirror can land in train while the other side is in test, a duplicate-style leak. (D/fair, unquoted)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f2]] [data] [stats] Split by match id (80/20), so a game's frames never cross train and test. `common.py` load_or_create_split (C/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f5]] [stats] By minute band (XGBoost): AUC 0.629 / log loss 0.667 / Brier 0.237 at 1-5; 0.733 / 0.604 at 6-10; 0.823 / 0.515 at 11-15; 0.862 / 0.461 at 16-20; 0.905 / 0.388 at 21-25; 0.909 / 0.381 at 26-30; 0.866 / 0.455 at 31-45. LR within 0.02 everywhere and better at 31-45 (AUC 0.884). `results/min... (C/fair, verified)
- [[jjzhao-lol-win-probability#^f7]] [stats] Late band dips (31-45 minutes): fewer, unusually even games. README (C/fair, verified)
- [[kapoor-narayanan-leakage-2023#^f11]] [lab] [stats] 9 of 12 papers had no significance test or uncertainty for classifier comparison; one claimed win had a bootstrap AUC interval of [0.66-0.95]. Section 4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f4]] [method] [stats] Unlike images, LoL confidences spread evenly over the range, so under- as well as over-confidence matters. Section I (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f5]] [stats] Uncalibrated MLP: 72.95% accuracy, ECE 4.47%, NLL 0.515; temperature scaling ECE 1.11%, NLL 0.505 (best NLL); their data-uncertainty loss ECE 0.57%, NLL 0.515, accuracy 73.81%. Table I (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f6]] [stats] ECE with 10 equal-width confidence bins; reliability diagram pooled over all minutes. Section IV (C/fair, verified)
- [[kim-calibrated-moba-2020#^f7]] [stats] Checked, not there: no accuracy or calibration by minute, no non-neural baseline, no comparison with the same features without champions. (C/fair, absence)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f1]] [stats] Berger and Pope's original claim: NBA teams slightly behind at half-time were 5.8 to 8.0 percentage points more likely to win than teams slightly ahead. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f2]] [stats] It does not replicate: no effect in Australian football, American football or rugby, nor in NBA games outside BP's period; meta-analytic effect 1.2 points, not significant. Abstract, Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f3]] [method] [stats] Design: regression discontinuity with the half-time score difference as running variable and cutoff zero; a jump in win rate at zero is causal if everything else is continuous there. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f4]] [stats] Estimation: local-linear regression with the Calonico et al. (2014) MSE-optimal bandwidth, bias correction and triangular weights, plus robustness to other bandwidths. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f5]] [stats] Validity check: re-run the same discontinuity with pre-match team skill as the outcome; it must show no jump. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f6]] [stats] The design fails where one unit of score is decisive (low-scoring sports): the win curve is not linear even near the cutoff. Section 2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[klein-teeselink-losing-winning-2023#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) The lesson for event values: a strong published "effect of being in a state" shrank to nothing at scale and out of sample; per-event values from one patch or one sample need held-out checks before we print them. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f1]] [method] [stats] Many delicate shifts happen at once, each with few instances; time windows and instance decay lose too much signal by discarding data. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f2]] [method] [stats] Measured: across exponential decay rates on two model families, quality improved as decay was moderated, best with no decay at all. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[koren-temporal-2009#^f3]] [method] [stats] Why: old data still carries persistent and cross-entity patterns, so down-weighting it loses signal with the noise when data per entity is scarce. Section 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f1]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] Readers misjudge error bars: asked for the separation needed for P = 0.05, only 22% were within a factor of 2. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f2]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] s.d. bars show the spread of the population and predict the range of new samples; they are not the error in the mean. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f3]] [stats] s.e.m. = s.d. / sqrt(n) shows uncertainty in the mean and shrinks with n. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f4]] [stats] 95% CI is about 2 s.e.m. for n > 15; a CI is more intuitive than s.e.m. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f5]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] [ux/charts] Always state which bar type is shown. (A/fair, verified)
- [[krzywinski-altman-error-bars#^f6]] [stats] [ux/uncertainty] (inferred) For players the useful band is the game-to-game spread ("in half of games you'll be between X and Y gold at 10"), i.e. percentiles of the observed diffs, labelled as such; the uncertainty of the mean belongs in n, the grey-out and a thin CI line or text, not as... (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f1]] [stats] Partly conditional summaries are cross-sectional means among those still observed at each time point, describing the dynamic cohort of survivors, not individual trajectories. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f2]] [stats] Unconditional models (e.g. random effects on unbalanced data) implicitly impute values after death, describing an "immortal cohort"; valid only if deaths do not happen or are independent of the response. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f3]] [stats] Fully conditional models stratify trajectories by time of death. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f4]] [stats] Joint models describe the whole cohort at each time point with a combined outcome (alive and healthy), so data stay balanced. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f5]] [stats] The choice must match the research aim. (A/strong, verified)
- [[kurland-2009-truncated-by-death#^f6]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: the honest default is partly conditional (plain mean of games still running at minute t), labelled "games still running"; do not smooth or model across minutes in a way that imputes ended games (a smoother weighted by n_t is fine, it only borrows ac... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f3]] [stats] Reported log loss about 0.68 after tuning (constant 0.693); most common comps sit at 45-55% win; coefficients stable across CV folds. (C/fair, verified)
- [[leaguephd-about#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no accuracy, AUC, split method or patch window. (C/fair, absence)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f1]] [stats] [method] Multiple membership: a lower-level unit belongs to several higher-level units at once (students with several teachers), unlike strict nesting. Introduction (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f2]] [stats] Assigning each unit to just one of its groups underestimates the higher-level variance, i.e. the groups' importance (Chung and Beretvas 2012). Section 2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f6]] [stats] Variance components per classification compare how much each level contributes, after the covariates. Section 4 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f7]] [stats] Software: few packages; MLwiN (ML and MCMC); for discrete outcomes with many classifications Bayesian MCMC is often the faster route there. Section 5 (B/fair, verified)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: varying slopes, fitting at scale, predictive checks. (B/fair, absence)
- [[leckie-2019-multiple-membership#^f10]] [stats] (inferred) ^f2 applies to how champgroups estimates a group effect from member cells: a champion counted in two same-trait groups must be in both, or each group's estimate absorbs the other's. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f1]] [method] [stats] Mains and interactions are not identifiable: mass can move between them with identical predictions, so contradictory readings describe one model. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f3]] [method] [stats] Even centring is not enough with correlated inputs: x1 x2 terms move strength between mains depending on the chosen centres. Section 1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f6]] [stats] The weight choice (uniform, empirical, smoothed) changes the purified effects, sometimes a lot; the right one is the data distribution. Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lengerich-2020-purification#^f7]] [stats] Two-stage fitting (mains first, as GA2M) leaves less mass to move but purification still changed GA2M's mains; unconstrained depth-2 boosting needed big changes, flipping signs on COMPAS. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f2]] [stats] Evaluation: 5-fold cross-validation (random, not by time), neural approximators trained 100 epochs with Adam. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lin-2024-comp-counters#^f5]] [method] [stats] The authors read LoL as overfitting: draft counter-picking and the huge composition space make unseen matchups hard; more games needed. (B/fair, verified)
- [[lol-realtime-2023#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: no calibration, log loss or Brier; no per-minute results; no statement that folds group frames by game (one snapshot per game per stage, so likely fine). (C/weak, absence)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f1]] [stats] In LoL three components predict outcomes: champion base skill, player base skill and player-specific champion skill; in Dota 2 mostly champion base skill. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f2]] [stats] Adding player-champion terms gave the best accuracy in LoL. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lol-skill-decomposition#^f3]] [stats] (inferred) So for a thin matchup where a few one-tricks supply many of the games, game-level standard errors understate uncertainty; count distinct players and cap or down-weight games per player. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[lol-wiki-surrender#^f5]] [data] [stats] (inferred) So curves to minute 14 are close to a full sample (only AFK games end early, and those should be dropped anyway); from 15 on, games that are lost badly drop out, and the surviving games at minute 20+ are biased toward closer lanes. Curves past 15 must be read as "among g... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[loldraftai#^f19]] [data] [stats] Train and test drop games where any player has under 2400 gold, over 10 deaths, under 25 CS (non-support) or under level 4 at 15 minutes, which also removes every game ending before 15:00. `prepare_data.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f20]] [stats] (inferred) Those filters use in-game information unknown in champ select and remove stomps and AFK games from the test set too, so reported accuracy is on easier, cleaner games than the tool sees live. (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[loldraftai#^f21]] [stats] Default split is a seeded random 90/10 by export file, not by time; an optional next-patch mode tests on the 2 newest patches mapped to the third newest. `prepare_data.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f23]] [stats] The checkpoint kept is the epoch with best loss on the same test split that reports accuracy, so the test set doubles as validation. `train.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f24]] [stats] Reported solo queue figures: win log loss 0.684 after the fix (constant 0.693) and calibration buckets over about 783k validation games, 50-55% predicted bucket 428,768 games at 0.525 actual. `correction-reddit-post/page.tsx` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f25]] [stats] The duplication bug had made true accuracy 52% while claims were higher; README later states loss 0.681, accuracy 56.1% at epoch 46. `correction-reddit-post/page.tsx`, `README.md` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f26]] [tools] [stats] The DraftGap vs LoLDraftAI accuracy comparison was withdrawn because validation data leaked into training. `draftgap-vs-loldraftai-comparison/page.tsx` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loldraftai#^f31]] [method] [stats] Checked, not there: no early stopping; all 50 epochs run, the best-validation-loss state is saved each time it improves and a timestamped checkpoint every epoch. `train.py` (C/fair, absence)
- [[loldraftai#^f34]] [stats] Validation runs on both the plain and a masked copy of the test split every epoch; `validation.py` (subgroup losses by elo, patch and champion play rate, quantile ECE, matchup calibration) is marked as out of date for the current architecture. `train.py`, `validation.py` (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-item-recs#^f3]] [stats] Item win rates are biased by purchase context: win-more items look strong, desperation items (Zhonya's) look weak, one-trick items look good. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-item-recs#^f4]] [stats] (claim) Its model adjusts for gold difference, player champion skill and enemy matchups to isolate an item's effect. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-recommendations#^f1]] [tools] [stats] The team comp analyzer adjusts win rates for champion experience, either from your own ranked history or a fixed 25 games per champion. (C/fair, verified)
- [[loltheory-recommendations#^f2]] [stats] Example: Gragas players averaged far fewer games on him than Shaco players, worth 1.6% win rate. (C/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f8]] [lab] [stats] Evaluate stochastic models several times: different seeds at least, better also varied data (CV); keep a subject's rows in one fold; report spread (SD or 95% CI). 4.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f11]] [lab] [stats] Comparing two model instances by per-sample outputs (McNemar) needs the per-sample predictions recorded. 5.3 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lones-ml-pitfalls#^f12]] [stats] Many pairwise tests multiply false positives (20 tests at 95%: one likely wrong); correct, e.g. Bonferroni. 5.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f2]] [method] [stats] Adding shaped pairs nearly closes the gap to random forests, and sometimes beats them. Section 5.1.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f5]] [method] [stats] Pair detection needs far more samples than candidate pairs; with 4950 pairs FAST needed about 10^6 samples for average precision over 0.7. Section 5.2 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lou-ga2m-2013#^f6]] [stats] Weak true interactions and chance interactions look alike near the detection threshold. Section 5.2.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[lsreg-score-screening#^f1]] [stats] [method] The score test is computed from the base fit only, for screening many candidate covariates; no parameter estimate is produced. `Overview` (C/fair, verified)
- [[lsreg-score-screening#^f2]] [stats] On simulated data the two true covariates of ten candidates had the largest score statistics, and values matched statmod's to numerical precision. `Results` (C/fair, verified)
- [[lu-concept-drift-review#^f3]] [method] [stats] Window size is a trade-off: small reflects the latest distribution, large gives more training data. Section 5.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f1]] [stats] [method] Ridge APM is the MAP of beta ~ N(0, tau^2): every player shrunk towards the average player. Section 2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f3]] [stats] Collinear players (always on together) are split by the attribute: ridge gives both the same credit, the attribute prior gives the higher-rated one more. Section 2, 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f4]] [stats] 10-fold CV MSE of game results, three EPL seasons: Augmented APM best; FIFA-only beats intercept; plain APM beat FIFA-only in 2015 and 2017. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f5]] [stats] By time in season: the attribute model predicts best early (little data), APM catches up by February; the prior matters most when ids are thin. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: numeric gains, intervals, a fitted tau (set to 0.1 by hand), several attributes or groups at once. (C/fair, absence)
- [[matano-2018-augmented-apm#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) Same direction as [[agarwal-chen-2009-rlfm#^f8]]: the attribute prior's gain is front-loaded where the id data is thin. For champions, a new patch or a rarely played champion-role is the "early season". (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f1]] [method] [stats] Measuring chemistry as lineup APM minus the sum of player APMs fails on small samples: too many lineups, too few minutes each. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f3]] [method] [stats] Synergy comes from the nonlinear link: skills that raise a rare event's chance stack positively, skills for an already likely event stack negatively (diminishing returns). (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f5]] [stats] [draft] Team lineup synergies range from about +1 point per game (Orlando) to about -1 (Minnesota); 1-2 points per game is 3-6 wins a season. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f6]] [stats] Teams show positive and negative chemistry equally often. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f7]] [stats] Validation: bucketed predicted vs actual steals over 987,343 possessions sit inside 95% bands (3 of 100 buckets outside); a calibration check of the mechanism, not of lineup synergy values. (B/fair, verified)
- [[maymin-2013-nba-chemistry#^f8]] [stats] Checked, not there: no shrinkage, no intervals on lineup synergies, no out-of-sample test of lineup results. (B/fair, absence)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f2]] [method] [stats] Within-game correlation handled by sampling one random minute per game, giving independent rows. Section 3.1 (C/weak, verified)
- [[maymin-lol-optical-tracking#^f7]] [stats] Checked, not there: no per-event or per-objective win probability values, no intervals, no control for the team already being ahead beyond the state features. (C/weak, absence)
- [[meinmmo-loldraftai#^f1]] [method] [stats] Launch claim (2 Apr 2025 Reddit post): the AI predicts 62% of ranked matches. (D/weak, verified)
- [[meiolol-filters-prototype#^f3]] [render] [stats] Only summable numbers filter: model estimates (lane-curve gold at 14) and rates with other denominators (pick, ban) do not. (B/fair, verified)
- [[meiolol-matchup-counts#^f2]] [seo] [stats] All regions, last 30 days: 165 of 12,925 matchup pages have 200+ games, 635 have 100+, 2,576 have 30+. (B/fair, verified)
- [[meiolol-matchup-counts#^f3]] [seo] [stats] EUW, last 30 days: 12 of 7,508 have 200+ games, 54 have 100+. (B/fair, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f1]] [stats] A good teacher estimates the true (Bayes) class-probabilities; distilling them gives an unbiased student risk with lower variance than one-hot labels. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f2]] [stats] The variance gain vanishes only when labels are deterministic; the noisier the label, the larger it is. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f3]] [stats] Experiment, binary logistic regression with known p*: Bayes-distilled beats one-hot fits at finite N, the gap closing as N grows. Section 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f4]] [stats] The harder (more overlapping, noisier) the classes, the larger the gain from distillation. Section 5.1, Figure 2(b) (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f5]] [stats] [method] Teacher accuracy is not enough: what matters is how close its probabilities are to p* in mean square; overconfident accurate teachers give worse students. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f6]] [stats] Distorting teacher probabilities while keeping its accuracy fixed progressively removed the student's gain. Section 5.1 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f7]] [stats] Teacher variance across training samples is a penalty: high-capacity teachers lower bias but vary more between fits. Section 3 (B/strong, verified)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) Our setting is the favourable extreme of ^f2 and ^f4: draft explains only a few points of win rate, so the label is close to a coin flip and one-hot win/loss has nearly maximal variance; a calibrated teacher probability is worth many games per game. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[menon-statistical-distil-2021#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) ^f5 and ^f7 say how to pick the teacher: by held-out log loss and calibration, not accuracy, and as a seed average (which cuts teacher variance). Check calibration of the seed-ensemble mean before distilling. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[metabot-garen-vs-darius#^f2]] [seo] [stats] It shows a 95% interval for the win rate, as meiolol's site does. (C/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f3]] [lab] [stats] Whether more data would help and how much data to use both reduce to locating the saturation point of the curve. Section 4.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f4]] [lab] [stats] If performance is already near the data's intrinsic noise, more rows will not help; acquire new features instead. Section 4.1.1 (A/fair, verified)
- [[mohr-learning-curves-survey#^f7]] [lab] [stats] Naive stopping rules on noisy curves stop too early; confidence intervals are wide at small anchors and narrow with size, so choose the point where the interval is narrow enough. Section 5.1.4 (A/fair, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f4]] [method] [stats] Ground truth MSE (true model, Table 2): marginal PFI is far off in the linear scenario (n=3000, p=10: 37.40 vs cs-PFI 0.26-0.40); in the non-linear scenario cs-PFI wins (1.00-1.18 vs knockoffs 626.80, imputation 144.77); with many linear dependencies imputation by random forest w... (A/strong, verified)
- [[molnar-2023-conditional-subgroup#^f5]] [method] [stats] Strobl's conditional importance for random forests (CVIRF) underestimated conditional PFI at the larger sample size, even with independent features. Section 6.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[openai-five-2019#^f3]] [stats] Calibration only asserted, not measured. Appendix D (A/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f1]] [method] [stats] Penalty form of a non-zero target: lambda * sum (beta_j - pi_j)^2, where pi_j is the lineup's prior from its members' player ratings, instead of lambda * sum beta_j^2. Section 3.2 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f3]] [stats] Sparse units: an NBA team uses 600+ lineups a season, about 25-30 possessions each. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f4]] [stats] Gain over raw lineup ratings grows as the lineup's sample shrinks; at several hundred possessions raw ratings do almost as well. Section 1, 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f5]] [stats] For unseen lineups the prior alone beats the league-average fallback by about 5% RMSE. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f6]] [stats] One lambda for the lineup level (no variance estimate); the authors suggest separate offence and defence constants. Section 5 (C/fair, verified)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f7]] [stats] Checked, not there: a comparison with lineup ridge shrunk to 0 or to league average (which would isolate the informed prior), intervals, overall RMSE numbers. The paper also mentions ESPN RPM and 538 RAPTOR shrink players towards box-score priors, but these are proprietary and untested he... (C/fair, absence)
- [[pelechrinis-2026-lrapm#^f9]] [stats] (inferred) The critic's point for our test: compare the group-target fit with the same fit shrunk to the role mean (our shipped champcomp), not with raw slopes, or the gain is mostly shrinkage itself (^f7). (C/fair, unquoted)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f1]] [method] [stats] Learning curves cross: logistic regression wins on small training sets, trees on large ones, often within the same domain, so model comparisons must look at the curve, not one size. Abstract (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f2]] [method] [stats] Which wins at scale is predicted by signal separability: trees for well-separable data, logistic regression where classes separate poorly. Section 1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[perlich-tree-vs-logistic-2003#^f3]] [method] [stats] Low separability (max AUC <= 0.8): when distinguishable, logistic regression won 8-1-1 on AUC; several sets were indistinguishable within error bars. Section 5.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[pletinka-dota-timeslice-2025#^f4]] [stats] Outputs were overconfident; temperature was set by a manual grid over T in 2-4 to make curves look intuitive (T = 2.5), not by validation log loss. Section 5.2 (D/weak, verified)
- [[pocket-tfm-gbdt-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] TabICLv2 teacher distilled into XGBoost kept 96.5% of teacher AUC (0.882 macro mean) at 1.9 ms on CPU, 38x to 860x faster. Abstract (C/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f1]] [stats] Effective sample size is n divided by the design effect: the simple random sample size giving the same variance. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f2]] [stats] Kish's weighting design effect is 1 + relvariance of the weights, i.e. n_eff = (sum w)^2 / sum w^2; easy but derived under restrictive assumptions. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f3]] [stats] Clustering increases the design effect with within-cluster homogeneity rho and cluster size: deff = 1 + rho (n-bar - 1). (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f4]] [stats] Even a small positive rho matters when clusters are big. (B/fair, verified)
- [[practools-design-effects#^f5]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup curves: with patch-decay weights w_g, use n_eff = (sum w)^2 / sum w^2 in the shrinkage weight and the "n" shown to users; with players contributing m games each, n_eff ~ n / (1 + rho (m-bar - 1)). Estimate rho once per role from data (variance of player mea... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f3]] [stats] Pairs classified by Bonferroni-adjusted p < 0.05 on each one-way coefficient; no shrinkage, no interval reported. (C/weak, verified)
- [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f6]] [stats] [method] (inferred) Confounded: in ADC i's model the coefficient on support j is support j's own strength plus any interaction, since the model has no support main effect. A strong support looks "mutualistic" with every ADC. Their own footnote says the classic test is an interaction term,... (C/weak, verified)
- [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f7]] [stats] (inferred) So the 53% vs 45-47% gap is mostly champion main effects, not bot lane synergy; it bounds pair-plus-main effects from above, not synergy. (C/weak, unquoted)
- [[rendle-fm-2010#^f4]] [method] [stats] Sparse data calls for a small latent size k: restricting expressiveness generalises better. Section III.A.2 (text split by column layout, no verbatim quote) (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f1]] [method] [stats] Three ways to compute a proportion mediated (ab/(ab + c'), ab/c, 1 - c'/c) agree for continuous outcomes but not with logistic regression. Background (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f2]] [stats] Cause: logistic error variance is fixed (3.29), so adding a variable that explains the outcome inflates every coefficient's scale. Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f3]] [stats] So c - c' systematically underestimates a positive indirect effect, more so the stronger the mediator-outcome link and the larger the sample. Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f4]] [stats] Even with no mediation at all, c - c' is likely non-zero. Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f5]] [stats] In simulation (outcome prevalence 0.10, 0.30, 0.50), the potential outcomes estimates and crude ab and ab/(ab + c') had the lowest bias and highest efficiency; c - c', ab/c and 1 - c'/c did worse at every prevalence. Results, Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f6]] [stats] Standardising logistic coefficients (y-standardisation, full, standardised logistic solution) mostly did not help. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f7]] [stats] With a binary mediator, potential outcomes and ab disagreed in real data: 7% vs 25% mediated for the same question. Results (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f8]] [stats] Odds ratios from the potential outcomes method do not approximate risk ratios at 10-50% prevalence. Discussion (B/fair, verified)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f10]] [stats] Checked, not there: no confounders, no exposure-mediator interaction, a single effect size per path. Methods (B/fair, absence)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f11]] [stats] (inferred) For us: a binary early-state mediator (first tower, first drake, "ahead at 14") is the case where the two families disagree most (7% vs 25%); use the continuous gold diff or the full state, and compute effects on the win probability scale by simulation rather than from coeffici... (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[rijnhart-logistic-mediation-2019#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) For us: comparing the draft logit's coefficient with and without gold@14 in a logistic win model (c - c') will understate mediation, and with 230k games the bias is not swamped by noise. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f1]] [stats] Hard minimum-count filters throw away information; shrinkage is the alternative. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f2]] [stats] [method] Two steps: estimate the distribution of the quantity over all units, then use it as the prior for each unit. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f3]] [stats] [method] With a beta prior the update is pseudo-counts: add alpha0 to successes and alpha0 + beta0 to the total; one line of code. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f4]] [stats] Prior strength fitted from real data was about 300 pseudo at-bats for career batting averages: a 4/10 batter is estimated at 0.264, a 300/1000 batter at 0.29. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f5]] [stats] Shrinkage depends on evidence: little data moves a lot, much data barely. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f6]] [stats] Better priors depend on known factors (era, team, position), i.e. a hierarchical model; he recommends fitting the prior on all data weighted by evidence (beta-binomial ML) rather than a filtered subset. (B/fair, verified)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f7]] [stats] [method] (inferred) For matchup win rates: shrink toward the additive expectation p0 (from both champions' overall win rates) with pseudo-count m: (wins + m * p0) / (games + m), m fitted per role by method of moments. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[robinson-eb-baseball#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) Plain-language line for our method page: "Extraordinary outliers require extraordinary evidence". (B/fair, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f3]] [stats] No long-term win-rate improvement with experience (rho 0.02); win rates revert to about 0.5, which they attribute to Elo-based matchmaking. (C/weak, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f4]] [stats] Within a session, for sessions of 3+ games, win rate falls by more than 10% and KDA by 8% from first to last game; not seen with shuffled game order. (C/weak, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f5]] [stats] Longer sessions show an initial rise before the decline, which they read as warm-up; stronger for win rate than KDA. (C/weak, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f6]] [stats] The decline is much smaller for the most experienced players (top 5% by games) than the least experienced (bottom 5%), who also play shorter sessions. (C/weak, verified)
- [[sapienza-session-performance#^f7]] [stats] Predicting whether a game is the session's last: AUC 0.976 (gradient boosting); top features current in-session win rate, session win rate, game index. (C/weak, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f1]] [stats] [method] Player gold is modelled as intercept + champion random effect + player random effect + noise, fitted as a Bayesian hierarchical model. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f2]] [stats] They chose hierarchical Bayes because it works with scarce and imbalanced data through shrinkage and pooling. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f3]] [stats] Heavy-tailed (Student t, 3 df) random effects so outlier champions or players are not over-shrunk. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f6]] [stats] Player and champion effects confound: most players stick to a few champions, so a model without a champion effect credits champion strength to the player. (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f7]] [stats] Champion effects are stable across patches (concordance typically above 0.8 on EUW and NA). (B/strong, verified)
- [[sido-lol-performance#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) For matchup curves: remove AFK or disconnect games before averaging (a lane "win" against an absent player is not the matchup); and one-trick players may dominate a rare champion's games, so a thin matchup may measure a few players, not the champions. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f1]] [method] [stats] APM is a regression of each no-substitution stint's margin on +1/-1 indicators of the ten players on court, plus a home constant; ratings are the effect of replacing an average player. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f2]] [stats] Sill blames overfitting more than collinearity: too many parameters on noisy data. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f3]] [method] [stats] Protocol: lambda, minutes cutoff and past-season weight chosen by 10-fold CV on games to February; March and April games held out as a pure test set and scored by RMSE of game margin. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f4]] [stats] Plain APM on one season predicts the held-out games worse than the trivial home-court model (RMSE 12.76 vs 12.58). (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f5]] [stats] Three seasons, plain regression: test RMSE 12.01, R-squared 0.088. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f6]] [stats] [method] Ridge with lambda 3000 on three seasons: test RMSE 11.47, R-squared 0.169, about double; one season with ridge gets 0.159, so shrinkage matters more than extra data. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f7]] [stats] Lambda is the noise to prior variance ratio; the CV lambdas imply a prior SD of about 3 points per player, so the penalty doubles as an estimate of the spread of true effects. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f8]] [stats] Shrinkage reorders players a lot, not just a fine tuning. (A/fair, verified)
- [[sill-2010-rapm#^f11]] [stats] (inferred) Ridge on unpenalised main effects plus penalised pairs gives lambda_pair = sigma^2_noise / tau^2_pair; reading tau_pair from the CV lambda is a cheap estimate of how big pair effects are, comparable with the bounds we already have (35-45 gold for non-lane enemy pairs). (A/fair, unquoted)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f1]] [data] [stats] 60k high-elo games, all on patch 10.16, sorted by time: first 35k train, next 10k validation, last 15k test. Section 4 (C/fair, verified)
- [[siska-moba-draft-2021#^f5]] [method] [stats] Test (last 15k games): champion-only 54.06%, with player features 53.45%, below its validation score; the author suspects overfitting from the extra features or drift over time. Section 7.3 (C/fair, verified)
- [[stanton-kd-work-2021#^f5]] [method] [stats] In ensemble distillation the highest-fidelity student is always the best calibrated. Section 7 (B/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f1]] [stats] [method] Score tests need only the null model fitted; Wald needs the alternative, LRT needs both. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f2]] [stats] [lab] That makes them cheap for testing many candidate covariates against a simple null model. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f3]] [stats] The three tests are asymptotically equivalent in ideal conditions. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f4]] [stats] [method] The code computes it as a residual correlation: project the candidate off the current design (weighted QR residual), dot it with the weighted working residuals, divide by the projected candidate's norm (and dispersion, 1 for binomial). Result is a z-statistic, standard normal und... (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f5]] [stats] Limits: one numeric covariate per statistic; factors and multi-column terms need anova.glm's score option, which refits. `man/glmscoretest.Rd` (A/strong, verified)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: no estimate of the effect size or of the predictive gain (log-loss) the covariate would bring; only a test statistic. (A/strong, absence)
- [[statmod-glm-scoretest#^f7]] [stats] [lab] (inferred) For meiolol: a candidate per-game feature (say a matchup term) can be screened as z = sum(x_resid * r) / norm(x_resid) on the current model's per-game residuals, in milliseconds, before any harness run; the z for a screen is not evidence of held-out gain and many screens ... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f2]] [method] [stats] Priors: independent Gaussians (mean and sd) on every component, chosen for memory and fast inference. Section 2.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f4]] [stats] Intended behaviour: metadata carry new users, ids take over once their ratings suffice. Section 1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f5]] [stats] MovieLens cold-start test (test users seen at 5% or 75% of their ratings): metadata "seem to improve" MAE, most at larger K; the per-user rating-threshold model helped far more. Section 4.1 (B/fair, verified)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f6]] [stats] Checked, not there: a number for the metadata gain, per-feature-group prior variances learnt from data (the priors are set, not estimated by EB), any check that the id and metadata parts are identifiable. (B/fair, absence)
- [[stern-2009-matchbox#^f8]] [stats] (inferred) ^f5 is one more case where the structural term that matches how the outcome is produced (user thresholds) beat descriptive metadata; for us, a trait that is mechanistically right (enemy auto-attack build for G1) should matter more than the label "kit group". (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[strobl-2008-conditional-importance#^f4]] [method] [stats] Simulation: marginal importance strongly overestimated correlated predictors; conditional importance reduced but did not remove the preference, and had lower variability. Sections 3, 5 (A/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f1]] [method] [stats] Fit error(N) = a N^-b + c (c the irreducible floor) to nested subsets at seven fractions (1% to 100%) on one fixed test set. Section 3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f2]] [method] [stats] Power laws fit well (R^2 > 0.8) on 77.7% of cells; tree ensembles win at full data, linear models underperform on classification. Abstract (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f3]] [method] [stats] Logistic regression has the steepest stable exponent (median b = 0.83), boosting 0.48, random forest 0.33. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f4]] [method] [stats] Exponent alone misleads: what matters is exponent and floor together. Section 4.2 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tabular-scaling-bench-2026#^f6]] [stats] (inferred) Our curve (-0.0008 log loss per doubling) should be fitted with a floor c, at several nested fractions, and compared between model families on the same test set; a single exponent from one run is ±15% at best. (B/fair, unquoted)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f3]] [method] [stats] Compare per feature: difference of the two models' shape functions with bootstrap-of-little-bags confidence intervals; train both on the same sample to remove sample noise. Section 2.3.3 (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-distill-compare-2018#^f4]] [method] [stats] The mimic is tighter than the outcome model where outcomes are sparse and noisy (ages over 70): soft scores carry less noise than labels. (B/fair, verified)
- [[tan-global-additive-2018#^f3]] [method] [stats] Accuracy kept on classification (AUC, teacher 2H-512 vs distilled additive SAT): Magic 94.06 vs 90.75, Pneumonia 82.18 vs 82.24, FICO 79.37 vs 79.42. Adding pairs: Magic 90.75 (no gain), Bikeshare RMSE 0.98 to 0.60. Tables 2 and 3 (cells split across lines; quote is the text) (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f1]] [method] [stats] Two shrinkage modes: a full hierarchical Bayesian model that partially pools players by position (variance components estimated by MCMC), or penalised likelihood to select the few effects that stand out. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f2]] [stats] Cross-validation works for one penalty; several pooled groups need the hierarchical model to estimate the variances jointly. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f3]] [stats] Every comparison is on withheld games: a random 20% of each season's games. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f4]] [stats] Held-out log likelihood: player terms beat team-only terms in all five seasons (total doubled negative log likelihood 76,087 vs 76,397 team, 76,416 score only), though single-game prediction barely improves. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f5]] [stats] Even team effects are rarely distinguishable from average: few team-season 95% intervals exclude zero. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f6]] [method] [stats] Pair procedure: fix the player-effect penalties from the main model, add the 1,000 pairs with most shared shifts (same position group only), lasso on the pair block alone, penalty swept. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f7]] [stats] [draft] Held-out likelihood picks pair penalty 8.5, which keeps 247 of 2,000 pair parameters (221 pairs): about a quarter of the best-sampled pairs show some chemistry. (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f9]] [stats] Adding the pairs left individual ratings mostly unchanged (one swap at the top). (B/strong, verified)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f10]] [stats] Checked, not there: no held-out gain of the pair model over the player model reported as a number, no intervals on pair effects (lasso point estimates only), no variance component for pairs. (B/strong, absence)
- [[thomas-2013-hockey-hazard#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) Freezing main effects is also the confounding guard: a pair term then only absorbs what is left after both champions' own strength, unlike [[ramler-2019-lol-symbiosis#^f6]]. (B/strong, unquoted)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f3]] [stats] Held-out accuracy: classic TrueSkill 52%, TrueSkill2 68%, on 3M Halo 5 games played after a 23M-game training period, predicting from team composition and squad membership only. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f6]] [stats] Squads: actual win rate rises with squad size (4: 57%, 8: 68%, 10+: 89%) while TrueSkill expected about 49-52%; a per-size offset fixes it. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f7]] [stats] Experience: new players win 45% at 0 prior matches rising to 49% at 10+, while TrueSkill expected 48-49%; a per-match skill increment by experience count (capped at 200, 200 parameters per mode) fixes it. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f8]] [stats] Previous-match kill rate predicts the next game: 43% win at the lowest band, 59% at the highest, against TrueSkill's 48-50%; kills and deaths modelled as truncated Gaussians from performance, monotonic so incentives stay aligned. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f9]] [stats] Quitting the previous game: 45% next-game win after a quit win vs 51% after a completed win; the quit model fixes it but over-penalises slightly. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f10]] [stats] Lapse (days since last game): win rate falls from 49% (under 1 day) to 43% (7-9 days); no lapse term needed, the experience term reproduced it. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f11]] [stats] Skill in two modes correlates 0.6; modelled as one base skill (drifting) times a per-mode weight plus a per-mode offset with its own experience drift. (A/strong, verified)
- [[trueskill2-2018#^f15]] [stats] Halo 5 fitted values: new-player skill spread small (v0 = 1.6 with performance noise 1), skill drifts slowly, 10^5 minutes idle equals one match of drift. (A/strong, verified)
- [[valorant-smurf-detection#^f1]] [stats] Before adjustment, 32% of games with a smurf ended in a stomp (a win by 8 or more rounds). (B/fair, verified)
- [[valorant-smurf-detection#^f3]] [stats] Adjusted smurfs came within 1% of the target stomp rate; the control group still stomped in 25% of games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[valorant-smurf-detection#^f4]] [stats] Convergence: adjusted smurfs reached their right MMR in as few as 4 games; unadjusted ones were still about 3 divisions off after 10 games. (B/fair, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f1]] [method] [stats] Difference method: total-effect coefficient minus the exposure coefficient once the mediator is added; product method: exposure-to-mediator coefficient times mediator-to-outcome coefficient. Traditional approaches (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f2]] [method] [stats] With linear models fitted by OLS the two always agree; with logistic regression they do not. Traditional approaches (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f3]] [method] [stats] Four assumptions for a causal reading: no unmeasured exposure-outcome (A1), mediator-outcome (A2) or exposure-mediator (A3) confounding, and no mediator-outcome confounder that the exposure itself affects (A4). Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f4]] [method] [stats] Mediator-outcome confounding must be controlled even when the exposure is randomised, because the mediator is not. Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f5]] [stats] Ignoring it can flip signs: in a randomised therapy trial the naive analysis made antidepressants look harmful and the direct effect larger than the total. Confounding assumptions (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f7]] [method] [stats] With exposure-mediator interaction (theta3), direct and indirect effects are closed-form combinations of both models' coefficients; without it they reduce to theta1 and beta1 theta2. Allowing for exposure-mediator interaction (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f8]] [stats] Logistic closed forms need a rare outcome; if it is common (over about 10%), neither product nor difference method is valid. Binary outcomes (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f9]] [stats] Cause: odds ratios are non-collapsible; adding any covariate to a logistic model tends to grow coefficients, so adding the mediator can hide mediation. Binary outcomes (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f10]] [stats] With logistic regression the difference method is conservative: it tends to underestimate the indirect effect. Binary outcomes (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f12]] [method] [stats] Sensitivity analysis for unmeasured mediator-outcome confounding (Ding and VanderWeele): two parameters, gamma (confounder-outcome risk ratio) and lambda (confounder-exposure ratio within mediator levels), give a bias factor that divides the direct effect and multiplies the indir... (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f14]] [stats] Nondifferential error in the mediator biases the indirect effect toward the null and the direct effect away from it (always for a binary mediator, or continuous mediator and outcome without interaction; often otherwise). Measurement error (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f15]] [method] [stats] Summing proportions mediated one mediator at a time fails if mediators affect one another or interact; a joint analysis of the whole mediator set works even when their order is unknown. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f16]] [method] [stats] The effect through one mediator is generally not identified when an earlier mediator affects it, short of strong linear, no-interaction assumptions as in SEM. Multiple mediators (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f21]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: win is a common outcome (50%), so the logistic product-of-coefficients and difference shortcuts (the "draft logit drops when gold@14 is added" reading) are both invalid; estimate natural effects by simulation or g-computation on the probability (risk difference... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-mediation-guide-2016#^f24]] [stats] (inferred) For us: gold@14 is a noisy proxy for "early advantage" (scaling comps trade gold for later power), so its indirect effect is a lower bound on how much the draft works through being ahead early, and the direct effect is an upper bound. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f1]] [method] [stats] On the odds ratio scale the total effect is the product of the natural direct and indirect effect odds ratios, so log ORs add; NIE log OR over TE log OR is a proportion mediated on the log odds scale. Direct and indirect effects odds ratios (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f2]] [stats] Mediator-outcome confounding is often neglected because studies collect only exposure-outcome confounders. Direct and indirect effects odds ratios (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f4]] [stats] Product and difference methods coincide only under a rare outcome, a normal homoscedastic mediator error, A1-A4, and no interaction. Appendix (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f5]] [stats] Ignoring an exposure-mediator interaction can make the exposure coefficient average to near zero and look fully mediated. Regression analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f7]] [stats] The controlled direct effect odds ratio from a logistic model with interaction needs no rare outcome assumption; the natural indirect effect formula does. Regression analysis (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f8]] [stats] Simulations: with small indirect effects the formulas held up even at common prevalence, but with indirect effects 5 times larger the rare outcome failure biased them (NIE coverage 79.1% at prevalence 0.5 in Table 1); ignoring a present interaction also biased them. Illustration and simul... (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f9]] [method] [stats] The two most common pitfalls: ignoring mediator-outcome confounding and ignoring exposure-mediator interaction; both can severely bias results. Discussion (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f10]] [stats] The identifying no-confounding assumptions cannot be checked in data; use subject knowledge and sensitivity analysis. Discussion (A/strong, verified)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f11]] [method] [stats] (inferred) For us: the draft-gold interaction is likely real (a lead matters less to a scaling comp, more to a snowball comp), so fit draft x gold@14 (and draft x full state) terms in the outcome model and report the four-way or interaction- aware decomposition, not a single "% m... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vanderweele-vansteelandt-or-mediation-2010#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) For us: our indirect effect is large (draft moves gold@14 by hundreds, and gold@14 is a strong win predictor), the regime where these authors' own simulations show the rare-outcome formulas break; avoid the closed forms entirely. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[vansteelandt-daniel-interventional-2017#^f11]] [stats] Example (29,580 patients): of a 2.8 point survival gap, 1.4 points mediated jointly; under interventional effects with no causal-order assumption, 0.7 via the mediators' dependence term, which they expected small but which was not. Illustration (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f5]] [stats] Intervals for these quadratic measures break down near zero (rate and limit change); intervals with uniform coverage are unsolved. Cross-fitting or the HulC (B blocks, fit on 4/5 and evaluate on 1/5 of each, interval from min to max) give "variability intervals". Sections 6.2, 6.3, 10 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f8]] [method] [stats] Random forests bias estimated LOCO towards 0 as correlation rises (true value 5 on the square root scale). Section 7, Figure 3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[verdinelli-2023-shapley-loco#^f10]] [stats] (inferred) The HulC splits data into blocks; splitting by game (all minutes of a game in one block) makes the interval game-clustered for free, and with 230k games each of 5 blocks still has 46k games. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f1]] [method] [stats] Importance of a group X_s = oracle predictiveness with all features minus oracle predictiveness without X_s (drop-and-refit), defined for the data, not for one fitted model. Section 2.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f4]] [stats] The plug-in estimator (difference of held-out predictiveness of the two fits) needs no debiasing for a large class of measures, so intervals come from influence functions. Section 3.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f5]] [stats] Cross-fitting (K folds, fit on K-1, evaluate on the held-out fold) is critical with flexible learners; without it coverage fails. Section 5 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f6]] [stats] At zero importance the estimator is degenerate; a valid test needs sample-splitting (full and reduced fits evaluated on different halves), at a cost in power. A beta-null (importance at most beta) tests for meaningful size, since tiny effects become significant in large samples. Section 3.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f8]] [stats] Correlated null features did not worsen bias or coverage beyond what extra dimensions do; both fade with sample size. Section 5.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f9]] [stats] A bootstrap that keeps the fitted functions fixed gave similar intervals to the influence-function ones. Section 7 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f10]] [stats] Assumes independent observations. Section 2.1 (A/strong, verified)
- [[williamson-2023-vim-inference#^f11]] [stats] (inferred) For us: fit per minute t (or per minute bucket) one row per game, so the iid assumption holds and the influence-function interval is already clustered by game. If minutes are pooled into one model, the per-row influence values must be summed within a game before taking the vari... (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f3]] [method] [stats] State model chosen for calibration, because WPA depends on calibrated probabilities; XGBoost beat logistic regression and CatBoost (Table II: log loss 0.5353, AUC 0.79). Section IV-C, V (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f4]] [stats] Adjacent states are highly correlated; they rely on a date-split test set rather than thinning to one state per round. Section V (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f5]] [stats] Uncertainty by bootstrap: resample rounds with replacement, 100 samples, and report the spread of mean WPA per round. Section VI-B (B/fair, verified)
- [[xenopoulos-csgo-wpa-2020#^f9]] [stats] WPA was more stable month to month (0.40) than KDR (0.38) or HLTV Rating 2.0 (0.29) for 479 players with 100+ rounds a month (numbers from Table IV). (B/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f9]] [stats] Pre-game-only accuracy drops with game length: under 65% for games over 55 minutes. Section 7.1 (C/fair, verified)
- [[yang-dota-realtime-2016#^f11]] [stats] Checked, not there: no log loss, AUC, Brier or calibration; numbers by minute only in a figure. (C/fair, absence)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f3]] [method] [stats] "Already ahead" problem in a points model: teams far ahead trade points for time, so they keep all plays but down-weight large score differentials, scaled linearly to zero at the maximum. Section 3.1.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f5]] [stats] Models picked by leave-one-season-out calibration error across all outcome classes, not by accuracy. Section 3.1.3 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f6]] [method] [stats] Credit split by multilevel models: play values regressed on varying intercepts per player group plus covariates, so low-sample players shrink to the group mean; fitted with lme4. Section 4.2 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f8]] [stats] Intervals by resampling whole drives (1000 simulated seasons), because events within a drive are dependent and player usage depends on team decisions. Section 4.4 (A/strong, verified)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f11]] [stats] (inferred) Our resampling unit should be the game (or the cluster of events in one fight), not the event: events in a game share the outcome, so event-level bootstrap intervals would be too narrow. (A/strong, unquoted)
- [[yurko-nflwar-2019#^f12]] [stats] (inferred) Their weighting fix maps to snowballed LoL games: an inhibitor at 95% win chance carries little information; down-weight or report value by leverage (win chance band) rather than drop. (A/strong, unquoted)
